STATE OF NEW YORK 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC SERVICE 
 
Case 19-G-0309 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY for 
Gas Service. 
 
Case 19-G-0310 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of KeySpan Gas East Corp. d/b/a National Grid for Gas Service. 
 
 
STAFF INITIAL BRIEF 
I. 
INTRODUCTION 
 
 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (KEDNY) and 
KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (KEDLI) (collectively, the Companies) filed 
on April 30, 2019, initial testimony, including panels and individual witnesses, and exhibits in 
support of a request to increase KEDNY’s annual gas revenue requirement by approximately 
$236.8 million (19.3% increase in delivery revenues or 13.6% increase in total revenues) and to 
increase KEDLI’s annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $49.4 million (6.0% 
increase in delivery revenues or 4.1% increase in total revenues) for the rate year ending March 
31, 2021 (Rate Year).  On January 29, 2020 the Companies filed its third supplemental 
testimony, including panels and individual witnesses, and exhibits in support of its corrected and 
updated request, to increase KEDNY’s annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $179.8 
million (16.0% increase in delivery revenues or 11.05% increase in total revenues), and KEDLI’s 
by approximately $37.5 million (7.34% increase in delivery revenues or 5.06% increase in total 
revenues). 
Over the past 12 months, Department of Public Service Staff (Staff) has 
vigorously prosecuted its statutory duty conducting a thorough analysis of the Companies’ rate 
request on behalf of the Companies’ customers and the State of New York.  Through this 
extensive analysis Staff has determined that the Companies’ rate requests are excessive.  The 
Companies requests cannot be said to be just and reasonable and are not necessary to enable the 
Companies to provide safe, adequate, and reliable service. 
Accordingly, Staff recommends that the New York State Public Service 
Commission (the Commission) reduces the KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s requests, as of the 


--- Page 6 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
2 
evidentiary hearing in these proceedings, by approximately $149.2 million and $77.3 million, 
respectively.  Staff recommends authorizing rates for KEDNY that will increase its annual gas 
revenue requirement by approximately $30.588 million, or an increase of approximately 4.46% 
in delivery revenues and 3.08% in total revenues.  Staff recommends authorizing rates for 
KEDLI that will decrease its annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $39.737 million, 
or a decrease of approximately 2.08% in delivery revenues and 1.43% in total revenues. 
In support of its recommendations Staff has logged numerous hours in 
quantitative and qualitative analysis of the Companies’ filings, issued over 1000 discovery 
requests seeking responses to multifaceted points of inquiry, held technical conferences with the 
Companies’ and other parties’ subject matter experts, and participated in nine days of 
Evidentiary Hearings during which Staff Counsel conducted cross examination of the Companies 
and other parties’ witnesses, and Staff witnesses were subject to cross-examination regarding 
their positions. 
Where appropriate, Staff has supported expenditures in the public interest and 
have similarly recommended removal of unsupported or poorly conceived expenditures and 
programs.  Staff also propose mechanisms necessary to protect customers and ensure the 
efficient and effective management of the Companies’ operations.  It is for these reasons that the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations as discussed herein. 
II. 
PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND 
 
 
On April 30, 2019, the Companies filed tariff leaves, initial testimony, and 
exhibits in support of their request to increase gas delivery rates. for the rate year ending March 
31, 2021 (Rate Year).  In May 2019, the New York State Department of Environmental 
Conservation (DEC) denied Transcontinental Gas Pipe Line LLC’s (Transco) application for 
water quality certification permits for the Northeast Supply Enhancement (NESE) Project.1  
National Grid contracted to transport gas via the pipeline to serve additional customers and meet 
anticipated forecast demand.2  In response to this permit denial, National Grid implemented a 
moratorium effective May 15, 2019 ceasing to fulfill requests for new firm service connections

On May 30, 2019, Administrative Law Judges (ALJs) Maureen F. Leary and 
James A. Costello presided over a procedural conference and technical conference in these 
proceedings.  Thereafter, the ALJs issued a ruling providing a procedural schedule for these 
proceedings.5  The ALJ’s ruling provided that: the Companies would file corrections and updates 
on July 3, 2019; Staff and intervenors would file testimony and exhibits on August 30, 2019; all 
parties would file rebuttal testimony and exhibits on September 18, 2019; and the evidentiary 
hearings would commence on October 2, 2019. 
 
 
On June 11, 2019, the Companies filed their first supplemental testimony and 
exhibits that explained the net impacts to the Companies’ proposed revenue requirements in the 
event that the NESE project was not available during the Rate Year.  This adjusted KEDNY’s 
revenue requirement to an increase of $242.5 million and an increase of $64.6 million for 
KEDLI.  On July 3, 2019, the Companies filed corrections and updates testimony and revised 
exhibits that reflected a total revenue requirement increase of $195.6 million for KEDNY and 
$61.2 million for KEDLI.  These adjustments were based on the assumption that NESE would be 
in service by the winter spanning 2020 and 2021. 
 
 
On August 30, 2019, Staff filed initial testimony and exhibits and proposed a 
revenue requirement increase for KEDNY of $ 2.205 million and a decrease for KEDLI of

Organizing Fund.  Then, on or about September 18, 2019, Staff, the Companies, Estates, NYC,

Companies in the same financial position as if the rates had gone into effect on April 1, 2019.8

the ensuing paragraphs, the evidentiary hearings were ultimately postponed until February

III. 
SUMMARY OF REVENUE REQUIREMENT AND RATE DRIVERS 
 
 
As set forth in the previous section, these proceedings have had more than the 
usual number of rounds of testimony.  Each round provided revised revenue requirement 
requests from the Companies and recommendations from Staff.  Appended to this brief, Staff 
provides its current revenue requirement recommendations for KEDLI and KEDNY in Appendix 
1 and Appendix 2, respectively.  These recommendations build on the ones Staff provided at the 
evidentiary hearing, with some movement based on Staff’s analysis of the record as presented 
through the hearings. 
 
 
Staff recommends authorizing rates for KEDNY that will increase its annual gas 
revenue requirement by approximately $30.588 million, or an increase of approximately 4.46% 
in delivery revenues and 3.08% in total revenues.15  Staff recommends authorizing rates for 
 
15 Staff’s recommended overall base revenue increase for KEDNY is $55.666 million, and is reflected 
by Staff’s recommended base delivery revenue requirement increase of $30.588 million, less 
incremental gross revenue taxes of $1.076 million, plus the impact of removing both the current sur-
credit for the effects of the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of $44.641 million and the surcharge for 
energy efficiency programs of $18.487 million.  Staff recommends reflecting these two items 
components in KEDNY’s base rate revenue requirement. 
 


--- Page 11 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
7 
KEDLI that will decrease its annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $39.737 million, 
or a decrease of approximately 2.08% in delivery revenues and 1.43% in total revenues.16 
 
 
The major drivers of increases from the Companies’ current rates to Staff’s 
recommended revenue requirements are as follows.  First, capital investment is increasing rate 
base.  The revenue requirement impact of the increases in rate base are $114.0 million for 
KEDNY and $52.9 million for KEDLI.  Second, Other Initiatives, which includes incremental 
employees and new programs represents an increase of $50.3 million for KEDNY and $32.8 
million for KEDLI.  Third, depreciation expense is increasing by $36.6 million for KEDNY and 
$20.4 million, as a result of the increases in capital investment, and also as a result of the 
proposed amortization of leak prone pipe (LPP) assets.  Two additional drivers impacting 
KEDNY, but not KEDLI are: a NYC law regarding paving requirements has led to a $26.0 
million increase; and site investigation and remediation (SIR), which reflects a $25.3 million 
increase. 
 
 
Other items offset these increases.  First, increasing levels of sales, which result in 
an increase in net margins, decreases the revenue requirements by approximately $77.0 million 
for KEDNY, and $48.7 million for KEDLI.  Second, due to the change in federal tax rates in 
2017, the Companies have excess accumulated deferred income tax (ADIT) balances that need to 
be returned to customers.  The Companies proposed to return the unprotected excess ADIT 
balance to customers over 44 years for KEDNY and 47 years for KEDLI.  Staff recommends 
returning these monies to customers over 10 years, which results in a revenue requirement 
decrease of $20.5 million for KEDNY and $8.1 million for KEDLI. 
 
 
It should also be noted that one of the major differences between the Companies’ 
revenue requirement requests and Staff’s recommended revenue requirements is the return on 
equity (ROE).  The Companies current rate plan reflects a 9.0% ROE.  Staff recommends that 
the Commission authorize an 8.2% ROE, whereas KEDNY and KEDLI seek a 9.65% ROE, a 
difference of 145 basis points.  Ten basis points is worth approximately $3.4  million for 
 
16  Staff’s recommended overall base revenue decrease for KEDLI is $16.796 million, and is reflected by 
Staff’s recommended base delivery revenue requirement decrease of $39.737 million, less 
decremental gross revenue taxes of $0.592 million, plus the impact of removing both the current sur-
credit for the effects of the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of $31.827 million and the surcharge for  
energy efficiency programs of $9.478 million.  Staff recommends reflecting these two items 
components in KEDLI’s  base rate revenue requirement.

KEDNY and $2.1 million for KEDLI.  Staff’s recommendation reduces the Companies’ 
requested revenue requirements  by $49.0 million and $31.8 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, 
respectively.  Thus, the difference in ROE plays a major part of the $149.2 million and $77.3 
million difference between the Companies’ and Staff’s revenue requirement changes for 
KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively. 
 
 
As explained in detail in the sections below, Staff’s recommended revenue 
requirements appropriately reflect KEDNY and KEDLI’s cost of providing safe, adequate, and 
reliable service in the Rate Year.  The Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations as 
they result in just and reasonable rates for customers. 
IV. 
REVENUES 
A. 
Gas Sales Forecast 
To forecast customers and volumetric throughput during the Rate Year, the 
Companies created econometric models by correlating historic customer counts and throughput 
with economic variables and evaluated those models in the Rate Year.  The Companies used 
economic models with historical data up to February 2018 to create their initial forecast.17  The 
Companies use the methodology of classifying their customers by service class, and within those 
service classes, further classify customers by rate code to distinguish between variations in 
services and rates, e.g., sales and transportation, low income discount, etc.18 
The Companies performed their forecasts by integrating the individual rate codes 
into six separate rate groups for each Company: Residential Heating, Residential Non-Heating, 
Commercial, Multi-Family, Temperature-Controlled, and Other.19  Two separate models were 
developed for each rate group, one to forecast the number of customers, i.e., the number of active 
flowing meters, and one to forecast use-per-customer.20  The forecast provided by the Companies 
in their initial filing assumed that the NESE project would be in service, and that no moratoriums 
would be enacted in the Companies’ service territories.21  With their Corrections and Updates

filing, the Companies added historical data up through February 2019 to the models which is 
then reflected in the re-calculated forecast.22 
In the Companies’ first Supplemental filing, they addressed the effect on the 
Companies’ forecast if the NESE project were not in service during the Rate Year.23  In the 
Companies’ Second Supplemental Testimony, they updated the forecast to reflect the November 
2019 Order, which includes the impact of the incremental energy efficiency and demand 
response programs the November 2019 Order required the Companies to undertake.24  In the 
Companies’ Third Supplemental Testimony, they added the impacts of the energy efficiency 
amounts set by the Commission in the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order to the forecast provided in 
their Second Supplemental Testimony.25 
 
 
Staff reviewed the Companies’ forecasts and took issue with the Companies’ 
Second Supplemental Testimony forecast because they did not accurately reflect the existence or 
effect of the moratorium on the Companies’ projected growth in customers.26  The moratorium 
lasted six months, preventing new gas service connections to existing and new customers within 
the Companies’ service territory in the boroughs of Brooklyn, Queens, Nassau and Suffolk 
counties.  The November 2019 Order lifted the moratoriums in the Companies’ service territories 
and required the Companies to expand their demand response programs.  In the Companies’ 
Second Supplemental Testimony, they used the same growth assumptions as in their Corrections 
and Updates Testimony, and although they reflected the impact of the incremental energy 
efficiency and reduction of demand response during peak day usage resulting from the 
November 2019 Order on the gas usage volumes,27  the Companies forecasted an overall decline 
of demand response customers.28  The impacts of the moratorium were also not reflected in in 
the Companies’ Third Supplemental Testimony where they adjusted their forecast for the 
incremental energy efficiency impacts, in response to the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order which

required utilities to increase incremental energy efficiency amounts.29  Other than reflecting 
changes from the Commission’s order, the Companies’ forecasts remain consistent with what 
they provided in their Second Supplemental Testimony.  Thus, they did not account for the 
impacts of the moratorium on the Companies’ gas forecasts. 
 
 
In Staff’s Supplemental Testimony, Staff accounted for the moratorium impacts 
and the increase in demand response by: using the actual meter counts from January 2019 to 
November 2019 provided by the Companies in the response to IR-DPS-1063, by maintaining the 
historical proportion of non-firm customers to the customers in the otherwise applicable firm and 
multi-family service classes, and by increasing the energy efficiency impact agreed to in the 
November 2019 Order to account for the amounts ordered in the 2020 Energy Efficiency 
Order.30  After examining the Companies’ Third Supplemental workpapers, Staff identified an 
error in Staff’s Supplemental forecast.  Initially, Staff adjusted the Companies’ Second 
Supplemental forecast by increasing the energy efficiency impact by the ordered increase in the 
incremental energy efficiency amounts in the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order.  However, the 
Companies adjusted their Second Supplemental forecast by reflecting the entire incremental 
energy efficiency impacts, not just the increase in the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order.  In 
response to IR NG-88, Staff agrees with the Companies’ incremental energy efficiency impact 
but opposes the Companies’ proposed forecasts as they do not properly reflect the moratorium 
impacts or the increases in demand response programs.31  Based on the information received in 
NG-88, Staff has made the appropriate adjustments to our forecasts which reflect the Rate Year 
forecast at 1,262,868 total customer count for KEDNY, and 610,765 total customer for KEDLI, 
and the Rate Year total usage of 1,693,859,460 therms for KEDNY, and 1,051,581,445 therms 
for KEDLI. 
 
 
The Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations because it depicts a more 
accurate forecast for the rate year reflecting the impacts of the moratorium, the expanded 
demand response program from the November 2019 Order, and the incremental efficiency 
impact from the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order.  Staff’s recommended forecasts will benefit rate 
payers because it will minimize reconciliation amounts in the revenue decoupling mechanism.

B. 
Newtown Creek Imputation 
 
 
For years, KEDNY has been working on the Newtown Creek renewable gas 
project.  This project, when operational, will capture waste gas from the Newtown Creek 
wastewater treatment facility, process it to pipeline quality, and inject it into KEDNY’s system.  
In 2009, KEDNY estimated that the project would cost $8.3 million, in 2012 KEDNY increased 
that estimate to $14.4 million.32  When KEDNY filed these cases, it estimated the capital costs at 
$32 million,33 and now, KEDNY estimates the project will cost almost $38 million.34  In April 
2019, KEDNY explained that it began construction in July 2018 and expected to bring the 
project online in November 2019.35  However, the project still remains incomplete.  As of the 
evidentiary hearing in these cases, the Companies latest guess was that the project would come 
online in April or May 2020.36  One can only speculate as to how the COVID-19 pandemic has 
affected and will continue to affect this most recent projection. 
 
 
In Case 16-G-0059, Staff raised concerns with this project and it did not believe 
that the in-service date would occur in the rate year in question in that case, calendar year 2017.37  
In that case, Staff proposed a number of rate making options for the Commission’s consideration.  
Ultimately, Case 16-G-0059 culminated in a joint proposal adopted by the Commission in the 
2016 Rate Order.38  The 2016 Rate Order adopted a number of provisions to address the 
Newtown Creek project.39  First, it excluded the projected costs from the revenue requirement.  
Second, it allowed KEDNY to defer the return on and return of the project and have those costs 
reviewed for the potential recovery in its next base rate filing, i.e., these proceedings.  Notably, 
this did not guarantee KEDNY recovery of these costs, just that they would be reviewed and 
considered in these proceedings.  Additionally, KEDNY has not deferred any costs, as the 
project has yet to go into service.  Third, in calculating any revenue requirement for purposes of

agreed to support KEDNY’s reasonable efforts to mitigate the effects of the revenue requirement

In these proceedings, KEDNY proposed to include the entire costs of the 
Newtown Creek project in rates.  At the time of the initial filing, this included a total capital cost 
of approximately $32 million, depreciated over 20 years, and $0.668 million in Rate Year O&M 
costs.40  Four months later, by the time Staff filed its direct testimony in these proceedings, the 
estimate of the total capital cost had increased to approximately $38 million plus or minus 10 
percent.41  The Rate Year O&M costs do reflect a 20-year tax abatement New York City has 
granted, for $14.4 million of the capital costs. 
 
 
KEDNY forecasts that the revenue requirement impact of the Newtown Creek 
project would be offset by revenues of $1.0 million from the sale of the renewable natural gas, 
and $0.9 million from the sale of the project’s environmental attributes.42  KEDNY, however, is 
not willing to risk these revenues not materializing.  KEDNY proposes to true-up the actual 
revenues, such that any variances will be deferred for future recovery from, or refund to, 
KEDNY customers.  Any revenues realized above the level necessary to fully reimburse 
customers for the project costs will be shared evenly between customers and NYC beginning in 
the fifth year after the project becomes operational.  Revenue sharing will then be assessed every 
year thereafter for the remainder of the project.  KEDNY will track these costs on an annual 
basis and compare to the project’s cumulative revenue requirement.43 
 
 
Staff is concerned with the overall cost of the project and the resulting ongoing 
annual revenue requirement impact to customers.44  The project began with a more reasonable 
capital cost of $8.3 million, however the cost has since escalated exponentially.  Yet, KEDNY

continues to want customers to bear all the costs, and all the risks of the project.  Based on the 
most recent estimate of total capital costs of approximately $38 million, a 20-year depreciable 
life, Rate Year O&M expenses of $0.688 million, and offset by $1.9 million in revenues, 
KEDNY proposes that customers pay $3.3 million in the Rate Year to subsidize this project.  
KEDNY asks that customers pay up to an additional $1.9 million, if the forecasted revenues do 
not materialize.  Put another way, in order for the revenues from the sale of the gas to meet the 
revenue requirement of the project that produces the gas, KEDNY would need to sell the gas 
produced by the Newtown Creek Project for approximately $29/dth gas in the system.45  This is 
approximately seven times current gas costs of approximately $4/dth.46  This cannot be justified. 
 
 
Even if the Commission were to adopt the adjustments set forth in the 2016 Rate 
Order, i.e., a 40-year depreciable life and an annual revenue requirement imputation of $1.6 
million in addition to guaranteeing customers the benefit of at least $1.9 million in annual 
revenues, customers would still be subsidizing KEDNY’s runaway Newtown Creek project by 
$0.8 million.  Such an action should be the minimum of what the Commission should adopt with 
regard to the Newtown Creek project. 
 
 
However, Staff does not believe that even this represents a reasonable and 
equitable resolution of this issue.  KEDNY continued to pursue this project even as it allowed the 
capital costs to spiral out of control, to almost five times the original estimate.  KEDNY claims 
that the project should be viewed in light of the environmental benefits, and not simply with a 
view of the costs.47  This, however, does not support requiring customers to bear all the costs and 
risks as KEDNY asks, nor even the costs with the minimal adjustments identified in the 
preceding paragraph.  Pursuant to the 2016 Rate Order, KEDNY was supposed to mitigate the 
revenue requirement of this project.  Despite New York City’s signature on the joint proposal 
that resulted in the 2016 Rate Order, KEDNY failed to extract any concessions from New York 
City with regard to additional property tax abatements or otherwise.48  Faced with that failure, 
KEDNY merely forged ahead, increasing the costs of the project; it now proposes to foist those 
costs, and all risks, onto customers.

In light of the tortured history of KEDNY’s flailing implementation of this 
project, Staff proposes that KEDNY bear the risks of the project not providing sufficient 
revenues to meet its costs.  To that end, Staff recommends that the Commission impute a level of 
revenue in KEDNY’s revenue requirement to equal the actual annual costs of the project.  
KEDNY should test annually to determine if the actual revenues exceed the actual revenue 
requirement of the project.  If the actual revenues do not exceed the actual revenue requirement 
of the project, no reconciliation should be permitted.  If the actual revenues exceed the actual 
revenue requirement, shareholders can retain any revenues from previous cumulative shortfalls.49 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation reasonably places the risks that the project cannot 
produce revenues sufficient to meet its costs squarely where it belongs, on KEDNY.  The 
Company has forged ahead with this project even when faced with set back after set back.  It 
should not be allowed to shift the costs of its stubbornness onto customers.  Accordingly, the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation and impute a level of revenues sufficient to 
meet the Rate Year revenue requirement of the Newtown Creek project. 
C. 
Per Class Versus Per Customer Revenue Decoupling Mechanism 
 
 
The Companies proposed to implement a revenue per class mechanism for 
Service Classification (SC) No. 1B – Residential Heat, remove the growth incentives in the

reconciliation with the Companies’ fiscal year.50  The Companies proposed to implement a 
revenue per class mechanism for SC No. 1B because it would support the Commission’s goals to 
promote energy conservation.51  The growth incentives were removed from SC Nos. 2 and 3 
because the forecasts used to develop the RDM targets already reflect the Companies’ historic 
robust level of customers and load growth.52  The Companies proposed to expand the RDM to 
SC No. 1A to account for the customer migration between SC No. 1A and 1B.53  Without the 
RDM for SC No. 1A, the Companies would lose the revenue associated with conversions from

D. 
Rate Year Revenues at Current Rates 
 
 
The Companies forecasted Rate Year revenues by multiplying the applicable 
current rates by the forecasted billing determinants derived from the sales forecasts.58  The 
Companies allocated the total Rate Year usage to the usage blocks in each SC using an ogive 
analysis.59  Then they priced out revenues at the rate code level and aggregated the revenues to 
the SC level.60  The Companies believe that pricing out revenues at the rate code level is more 
accurate because the rate codes that comprise a service class could have different usage 
profiles.61 
 
 
The Companies initially normalized the bill frequency data used in the ogive 
analysis by removing negative values that primarily represent canceled bills.62  Additionally, in 
the Companies rebuttal testimony, they agreed to revise the data normalization methodology for 
the bill frequency data used in the ogive analysis to include negative values to account for 
canceled bills or rebills that would increase the amount in a block step or cause the block step to 
be negative.63

applicable current rates by the forecasted billing determinants derived from the forecasts 
assuming NESE would not be in service during the Rate Year, updated for the corrections and

V. 
EXPENSES 
A. 
Operation and Maintenance 
1. 
Consultants 
 
 
The Companies use third-party consultants for a variety of reasons.  Some 
consultants are hired directly by KEDNY and/or KEDLI, while other consultants are hired by 
National Grid Service Company.  For those hired by National Grid Service Company, the total 
cost is allocated among the operating utilities, including KEDNY and KEDLI. 
 
 
To forecast Consultants expense for the Rate Year, the Companies began with the 
Historic Test Year actual expenses and made normalizing adjustments to exclude the costs 
associated with the GBE program, the Appreciate program, and the additional review of Historic 
Test Year costs done for this rate case.72  The Companies then increased this normalized Historic 
Test Year figure by an inflation factor of 3.96%.73 
 
 
Included in the normalized Historic Test Year, and ultimately the Rate Year, are 
charges from the consultant AlixPartners LLP (AlixPartners).  The Rate Year forecast of 
Consultants’ expense includes a total of $1.146 million and $0.714 million for KEDNY and 
KEDLI, respectively, resulting from AlixPartners costs in the Historic Test Year.74 
 
 
Staff recommends that the charges associated with the vendor AlixPartners should 
be normalized out of the Historic Test Year when developing the Rate Year forecast of 
Consultants’ expense.  These Historic Test Year charges were extraordinary and similar charges 
cannot reasonably be expected to recur during the Rate Year. 
 
 
The Companies engaged AlixPartners to design, launch, and execute a “Fact 
Base, Opportunity Assessment, and Roadmap” of National Grid’s Information Services division, 
as well as help with executing a transformation plan.75  As shown in the single/sole source 
procurement documentation, the Companies were seeking to employ a refreshed strategy that 
“goes beyond traditional reviews and analysis offered by technology consultants” and that 
AlixPartners is a “niche consultancy firm.”76  In response to IR DPS-932 Attachment 1, the

Companies state that AlixPartners “exceed and outweigh general consultancy companies that

reasonable, or cost-effective, for the Companies to hire a consultant to perform all this work 
again during the Rate Year.  Indeed, the Companies stated that this work is unique to the Historic 
Test Year.78 
 
 
In their rebuttal testimony, the Companies assert that they will use consultants 
like AlixPartners to ensure the efficient delivery of the IT portfolio and look proactively for ways 
to maintain existing efficiencies, further arguing that it would be unreasonable and imprudent if 
the Companies did not.79  However, the Companies assertion ignores their own 
acknowledgement that the engagement with AlixPartners was extraordinary and went “beyond 
traditional reviews and analysis offered by technology consultants.”80  For example, until the 
Companies commenced this undertaking at the end of 2017, they did not incur costs from 
AlixPartners in the two years prior to the Historic Test Year.81  The Companies have not pointed 
to any particular review they intend to conduct during the Rate Year analogous to the work done 
by AlixPartners during the Historic Test Year.  Accordingly, the reasonable conclusion is that the 
costs incurred for AlixPartners during the Historic Test Year will not recur during the Rate Year. 
 
 
Additionally, the Companies state that the work performed by AlixPartners 
resulted in annual savings reflected in the revenue requirement.  Since customers receive a 
benefit from the costs, the Companies’ argue that it is inappropriate to disallow the costs.82  
However, the Companies ignore the fact that the costs are one-time expenses that occurred 
during the Historic Test Year, while customers will see the benefit in the Rate Year because the 
savings recur annually.  It is appropriate to compare costs and savings during the same time 
period.  Here, the Companies have achieved savings during the Historic Test Year and will 
achieve savings during the bridge period.  The Companies get to retain the benefit of the savings

Year, and that there will be savings of $22.1 million annually in the Rate Year, and continuing 
through the next few years at least83  Assuming these National Grid Service Company level 
savings were and will be allocated to the operating companies in line with IT costs, KEDNY and 
KEDLI would have received $0.732 million and $0.456 million of the Historic Test Year 
savings, respectively, and will receive $2.941 million and $1.832 million of the annual savings, 
respectively.  Thus, the Companies have already retained the savings related to the AlixPartners 
costs and Staff is not seeking to reach back and capture those savings for the benefit of 
customers. 
 
 
As a corollary, the Companies attempt to justify including the AlixPartners costs 
based on the savings those one-time non-recurring costs produced, and that some undefined but 
analogous costs will recur in the Rate Year.  This argument has two problems.  First, to the 
extent the Companies would incur analogous costs during the Rate Year, the costs should result 
in analogous savings.  However, the Companies have not included those analogous incremental 
savings in their Rate Year forecast.  Second, the Companies have failed to identify any 
analogous reviews they expect to occur during the Rate Year. 
 
 
With regard to Consultants’ expense, Staff has appropriately identified the 
AlixPartners costs as one-time non-recurring costs in the Historic Test Year.  Where there are 
one-time, non-recurring costs such as these, it is appropriate to remove them from the forecast of 
the Consultants’ expense for the Rate Year to ensure that customers do not pay for expenses that 
likely will not be incurred during the Rate Year.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt 
Staff’s recommended downward adjustments to KEDNY and KEDLI’s respective Rate Year 
Consultants’ expense of $1.146 million and $0.714 million.  Alternatively, if the Commission 
decides to reject Staff’s adjustment to remove the AlixPartners’ costs is rejected, then the 
Commission should impute additional savings in the Rate Year to reflect the savings these future 
costs could produce.

2. 
Contractors 
 
 
To forecast Rate Year Contractors expense, KEDNY began with the Historic Test 
Year expense amount of $113.209 million, then made normalizing adjustments for its Historic 
Test Year review, Gas Business Enablement Project costs, Appreciate Program costs, 
Capitalization Policy Changes, Leak Repair Cost Incentives, reclassification of Paving expenses, 
out of period disconnect/reconnect costs, and Local Law 30 (LL30) costs.  KEDNY then applied 
inflation to arrive at a total Rate Year forecast of $78.724 million.  Similar to KEDNY, KEDLI 
began with the Historic Test Year expense amount of $32.337 million, then made normalizing 
adjustments to remove non-recurring costs, Gas Business Enablement Project costs, Appreciate 
Program costs, Capitalization Policy Changes, and Leak Repair Cost Incentives.  KEDLI then 
applied inflation to arrive at a total Rate Year forecast of $32.961 million.84  Staff reviewed the 
Companies’ proposed contractor costs and recommends two adjustments.  First, Staff 
recommends reducing KEDNY’s snow removal costs in the Rate Year.  Second, Staff 
recommends reducing KEDNY’s Local Law costs. 
a. 
Snow Removal Costs (KEDNY) 
 
 
KEDNY included the self-explanatory cost category “snow removal” within 
Contractors expense.  In calendar year 2016 KEDNY incurred charges of $1.228 million.  In 
calendar year 2017 KEDNY incurred charges of $1.800 million.  In the Historic Test Year, 
KEDNY incurred charges of $3.337 million.  KEDNY’s reported Historic Test Year charges 
represent a 172% increase from 2016 and an 85% increase compared to 2017.85  KEDNY did not 
provide any other reasonable explanation for why the Historic Test Year amount would be 
expected to appropriately indicate the snow removal expense level during the Rate Year. 
 
 
Given the significantly higher expense level in the Historic Test Year, and the 
lack of explanation for why it should be trusted for the Rate Year, Staff recommends using a 
three-year average to forecast snow removal expense for the Rate Year.  Specifically, the Staff 
Revenue Requirements Panel projected Rate Year snow removal costs using a three-year average 
of the snow removal charges incurred in calendar years 2016, 2017, and 2018, plus inflation.86  
 
84 T. 2350 
85 T. 2352. 
86 T. 2353-2354. 


--- Page 25 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
21 
This reduces snow removal expense from $3.337 million to $2.074 million, or reduces 
Contractors expense by $1.263 million. 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation to rely on a three-year average snow removal expense 
level, rather than on the Historic Test Year expense level is reasonable because it appropriately 
moderates what is plainly an abnormally high outlying year.  Snow removal is, by definition a 
weather dependent expense.  There is no reason to expect that the high level of snow removal in 
the Historic Test Year will necessarily reoccur in the Rate Year.  Thus it is reasonable to 
normalize this expense, as the Companies do for a number of other costs.  Indeed, where 
forecasts are highly dependent on weather, forecasts often seek to “normalize” the weather’s 
impact, rather than forecast based on the weather’s impact on a single year.  For example, both 
Staff and the Companies use weather normalization for their gas sales volumes and revenues. 
 
 
In its rebuttal, KEDNY asserts that the use of the Historic Test Year to forecast 
snow removal is consistent with several past National Grid rate cases.  Specifically, KEDNY 
notes that, in Case 16-G-0059, the rate allowance for snow removal expense was approximately 
$0.689 million, and actual expenses for calendar years 2017 and 2018 were $1.8 million, and 
$3.337 million.  Further, states KEDNY, there is no reason to believe that it will result in a more 
accurate forecast.87  While snow removal costs may have been forecasted differently in the past, 
the facts in this case demonstrate that, for KEDNY, relying on only a single year’s experience 
results in an unreasonable result.  Indeed, even using Staff’s recommended methodology, 
KEDNY will recover more in rates for snow removal than it incurred in calendar years 2016 or 
2017. 
 
 
KEDNY also argues that Staff uses a different methodology, relying on the 
Historic Test Year, to forecast snow removal costs for KEDLI. (RRP rebuttal, KEDNY page 14-
15).  This is true, because KEDLI’s Historic Test Year snow removal costs did not deviate from 
recent years’ experience so dramatically.  Thus, Staff does not believe it is imperative to use a 
three-year average to forecast KEDLI’s snow removal expense.  That said, as demonstrated in 
the response to IR DPS-237,88 KEDLI incurred snow removal costs of $2.346 million during the 
Historic Test Year.  During calendar year 2016, and calendar year 2017 KEDLI incurred snow 
 
87 T. 1990. 
88 Ex. 635, part 1 


--- Page 26 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
22 
removal charges of 2.437 million, and $1.763 million.  If, for the sake of consistency, the 
Commission uses a three-year average, with inflation, to forecast KEDLI’s Rate Year snow 
removal expense, this would result in a decrease of $0.265 million.  Moreover, this demonstrates 
that Staff’s adjustment to KEDNY’s snow removal expense was not result driven, but rather a 
reasonable effort to smooth-out the variability of these costs and produce a more reasonable Rate 
Year forecast.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended snow removal 
costs for KEDNY. 
b. Local Law 30 costs 
 
 
Local Law 30 (LL30) refers to the New York City requirement that KEDNY 
install shut off valves on its gas services outside of the buildings served.  Staff made an 
adjustment to correct an error in KEDNY’s forecasts of LL30 costs.  As explained in response to 
IR DPS-869,89 KEDNY did not reflect the most recently updated LL30 forecast in the 
Corrections and Updates filing.  KEDNY’s Rate Year LL30 expense forecast in its Corrections 
and Updates filing is $16.642 million.  Using the updated LL30 forecast, the Rate Year LL30 
expense would be $10.264 million.  As such the Rate Year expense amount should be decreased 
by $6.378 million.  Of the reduction of $6.378, $5.201 million is related to Contractors 
expense.90  In its rebuttal testimony, KEDNY agreed with Staff.91

purposes that are not otherwise identified in specific expense types.92  Staff recommends making

Year amount resulting from New York City Notices of Violation Fines.  Second, Staff 
recommends removing $1.131 million from KEDLI’s Other Expense to remove certain out of 
period costs.

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
23 
a. 
NYC Notices of Violation Fines (KEDNY) 
 
 
This expense results from fines KEDNY pays to New York City for instances 
where KEDNY has failed to properly comply with the conditions of work permits.  In the 
Historic Test Year, KEDNY paid New York City fines totaling $3.541 million.  To forecast the 
fines it will incur for failing properly comply with work permits during the Rate Year, KEDNY 
adjusted this Historic Test Year amount to reflect inflation.  With the inclusion of inflation, 
KEDNY requests that customers pay $3.681 million in Rate Year to reimburse KEDNY for fines 
resulting from its failure to properly comply with the conditions of work permits. 
 
 
This has been a long-standing issue for KEDNY.  In the 2016 Rate Order, which 
adopted the terms of a joint proposal, the Commission ordered the Company to submit a report 
within 90 days after each Rate Year.  The report included the Company’s efforts to reduce 
roadwork violations.93 The revenue requirement adopted in the 2016 Rate Order also reduced the 
recovery by 50%.  While KEDNY has made progress in reducing the level of fines, KEDNY’s 
request that customers to pay for $3.681million in fines it incurred for failing to properly comply 
with the conditions of work permits is wholly unacceptable. 
 
 
Accordingly, Staff recommends reducing the Rate Year rate recovery afforded 
KEDNY for these fines by 25%, or $0.920 million.  Under Staff’s proposal, KEDNY will still 
recover $2.761 million from customers for fines it incurs for failing to properly comply with the 
conditions of work permits.  This is far from ideal.  However, it will ensure that KEDNY 
continues to strive to decrease, and ultimately eliminate these fines. 
 
 
Staff’s 25% reduction has two components, one reducing KEDNY’s Rate Year 
allowance for fines by 15%, and the other by 10%.  Staff recommends reducing the Rate Year 
allowance for fines by 15% to reflect the fact that at least 15% of the fines incurred by KEDNY 
in the Historic Test Year resulted from violations within KEDNY’s control.  Staff recommends 
reducing the allowance for fines by 10% to reflect the expected fruits of KEDNY’s efforts to 
reduce the fines it incurs, and to encourage it to take actions to further reduce the fines it incurs.94 
 
 
To ascertain which violations resulted from actions within KEDNY’s control, 
Staff first provided KEDNY with an opportunity to identify which violations resulted from 
 
93 2016 Rate Order, Attachment 1, Joint Proposal, pp. 24-26. 
94 T. 2600. 
 


--- Page 28 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
24 
actions outside of its control.  In its response to IR DPS-929, KEDNY refused to make use of 
this opportunity.  It responded to Staff’s question by stating “The Company does not track 
specific NOVs that the Company attributes to factors outside the Company’s control.”95  
KEDNY cannot reasonably assert that absolutely none of the fines it incurred were for reasons 
within of its control.  Nor can KEDNY reasonably argue that customers should pay for fines that 
KEDNY could have avoided through its own efforts.  Arguably, faced with KEDNY’s 
stonewalling on this issue, Staff, and the Commission could find that KEDNY has not 
demonstrated that any of the fines it incurred were unavoidable, and thus KEDNY should not 
receive rate recovery for such fines during the Rate Year. 
 
 
Staff, however, took a conservative approach.  Staff reviewed the variety of fines 
KEDNY has incurred, as provided in response to IR DPS-768,96 and identified those types of 
fines that should reasonably be subject to KEDNY’s control.  Some of these include KEDNY’s 
failure: (1) to comply with terms and conditions of permit; (2) to install raise  plow signs; (3) to 
ensure plates are pinned and ramped; (4)to provide adequate protection at work sites; (5) to post 
a flag person at worksite to give directions; (6) to display required signs at worksite; (7) to seal 
street opening joints; (8) to restore lane markings; and (9) to replace lose utility covers.97  Staff 
calculated the fines KEDNY incurred for these violations that were within its control and found 
that these violations amounted to more than 15% of the fines KEDNY incurred during the 
Historic Test Year.98 
 
 
Staff arrived at its recommended 10% reduction by reviewing KEDNY’s efforts 
in the recent past to reduce the number of fines it incurs, and to reflect how those efforts should 
reduce the amount of fines KEDNY incurs during the Rate Year.  As identified above, as part of 
the 2016 Rate Order, KEDNY has made efforts to reduce the amount of fines it incurs, and 
provides annual reports on its progress.  Staff reviewed KEDNY’s 2018 Road work and Traffic 
Violations Report,99 which shows the efforts made by KEDNY in the beginning of calendar 
2017.  The anticipated benefits have not yet been fully realized.  As stated in the 2018 Report,

KEDNY is committed to reducing the number of traffic violations and violations related to street 
opening permits and continues to review and analyze NOVs to identify root causes and review 
work practices, while implementing process and reporting enhancements to increase awareness 
and improve performance, and inform if additional training is required to reduce NOVs.  
KEDNY asserts that it has undertaken, and continues to undertake, significant efforts to reduce 
the number of violations for the benefit of customers and the public.100 
 
 
Some examples where improvement can be expected over KEDNY’s 
performance subsequent to the Historic Test Year follow.  In its 2018 Report, KEDNY explained 
that in June 2018 the DOT required KEDNY to transition to a web-based platform for ordering, 
reissuing and renewing all permits by December 2018.101  For a short period during the Historic 
Test Year, this transition negatively affected efforts to reduce overall summonses due to a shift in 
focus to the required transition work.  Now that the transition occurred, and with future 
enhancements to the web-based platform, KEDNY expects that it will allow it to more 
effectively manage its permits to DOT’s expectations.102  Additionally, KEDNY expects to 
realize a reduction in “no permit” related summonses by utilizing the robotic process automation 
technology in a more widespread manner for permit reissues and renewals and new permit 
orders.103  Furthermore, KEDNY explained that it is collaborating monthly with DOT and 
holding process improvement meetings with its major contractors to discuss and resolve issues 
related to NOVs.104 
 
 
These show that KEDNY is taking efforts to improve its performance, i.e., to 
incur fines less frequently during the Rate Year than during the Historic Test Year.  It would be 
unreasonable and irresponsible of Staff to ignore these efforts when forecasting the amount of 
money KEDNY should be able to recover from customers for fines KEDNY incurs.  Staff looked 
at the efforts KEDNY has been and continues to make and assessed that KEDNY should be 
improving its performance by reducing the fines it incurs by at least 10% in the Rate Year.  
Again, Staff recommends allowing KEDNY to recover $2.761 million from customers for fines

KEDNY incurs for failing to comply with the conditions of work permits.  This amount should 
be zero.  KEDNY has been working on this issue at least since Staff raised this issue during the 
2016 rate case.  While it has improved its performance, asking customers to pay for even one 
fine is too much.  KEDNY must continue to drive down the number of fines it incurs.  Staff’s 
adjustment merely seeks to reflect the efforts KEDNY has taken, to ensure that customers pay 
for more of KEDNY’s violations of work permit conditions than necessary. 
 
 
KEDNY opposes Staff’s recommendation.  KEDNY asks that customers pay for 
all of the fines KEDNY incurs when it fails to comply with work permit conditions.  KEDNY’s 
main arguments are as follows:  (1) the fines are a cost of doing business in New York City; (2) 
process improvements will keep cost from raising higher than the historical costs, but that 
KEDNY cannot achieve an additional 25% savings; (3) New York City used to be more lenient, 
but has been stricter regarding violations of work permit conditions; (4) KEDNY already 
identified reductions to “fines and summonses” as benefits of implementing Gas Business 
Enablement. 
 
 
First, KEDNY’s assertion that the fines are a cost of doing business in New York 
City is too broad, and, in any event, unacceptable.  As an example, KEDNY states that it “could 
take additional measures solely for purposes of avoiding violations, such as repeated 
demobilizations and remobilizations to avoid working pas permit time, but such measures would 
not be as efficient and would result in more customer disruptions.”  Staff recognizes that such 
circumstances may exist.  KEDNY, however, has resisted all efforts to quantify fines that are 
truly unavoidable, or in this case, uneconomic to avoid.  The Commission should not reward 
KEDNY for this stonewalling by providing it with full recovery from customers of the costs of 
all the fines it incurred, without demonstrating that those fines are truly unavoidable or less 
expensive than alternatives. 
 
 
Second, KEDNY’s argument that its process improvements have kept cost from 
rising even higher than the historical cost, but not lead to further reductions should be rejected.105  
The 2018 Report makes clear that KEDNY was actively implementing measures during the 
Historic Test Year, and that benefits would take time to be fully realized.  Additionally, the 2018 
Report notes ongoing efforts by KEDNY to further reduce the fines it incurs.  Thus, it would be 
 
105 T. 1993. 


--- Page 31 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
27 
unreasonable to expect that KEDNY would not be able to reduce the fines it incurs further during 
the Rate Year. 
 
 
Third, KEDNY asserts that New York City Department of Transportation 
(“DOT”) continues to expand its code enforcement resources and capabilities.106  As an example, 
KEDNY points to code violation pertaining to roadway and sidewalk clearing at the end of each 
workday.  KEDNY asserts these violations were only selectively enforced by DOT in prior years 
but are now being enforced by DOT on every job site where a permit for roadwork exists.107  
However, this argument amounts to nothing more than, “but I used to be able to get away with 
it.”  Such an argument cannot be credited to justify foisting onto customers the cost of the fines 
KEDNY incurs for its failure to comply with work permit conditions. 
 
 
Fourth, for the first time at the evidentiary Hearing, KEDNY, through cross 
examination of the Staff Accounting Panel sought to assert that Staff’s adjustment to Other 
Expense was somehow a double count of reductions to “Summons and fines” KEDNY had 
identified separately as benefits of implementing Gas Business Enablement.108  However, 
KEDNY has not demonstrated that the costs related to New York City NOVs in Other Expense 
equate to the GBE-related “Summons and fines” to which KEDNY points.  Staff notes that it is 
suspect that KEDNY did not raise this defense in responses to interrogatories, or in rebuttal 
testimony.  Had it done so, Staff could have properly reviewed the claim.  Instead, KENDY 
raised this only at the evidentiary hearing, when there is no further opportunity to test KEDNY’s 
claim.  Moreover, KEDNY did remove the operating costs associated with the GBE Program 
from the Other Expense.109  If KEDNY removed the GBE costs from Other Expense, then, 
logically, it also would have removed fines and violations it asserts it accounts for separately 
related to GBE as well. 
 
 
In sum, KEDNY incurs fines when it fails to comply with work permit conditions.  
KEDNY asks that the Commission provide full rate recovery for the fines KEDNY incurs.  As 
set forth above, Staff recommends a conservative 25% reduction to KEDNY’s requested rate 
recovery of fines.  For the reasons set forth above, Staff’s recommendation is achievable and

b. 
Removal of Prior Period Costs (KEDLI) 
 
 
Staff recommended removing a prior period cost associated with the vendor 
PSEG Long Island LLC from KEDLI’s Other Expense.110  According to the response to IR DPS-
833, this expense regards a settlement of a matter between PSEG Long Island LLC and 
KEDLI.111  In reviewing KEDLI’s accounting, Staff determined that KEDLI has reversed 
accruals in the Historic Test Year that had been recorded in prior years.  The reversed accrual 
entries serve to partially offset the settlement booked to Other Expense.  However, Staff 
determined that $1.109 million related to the settlement remained in the Historic Test Year.112  
KEDLI agrees with the need for the adjustment states that the correct amount should be $1.131 
million, to account for inflation.113  Staff agrees with this calculation.  As this is an out of period 
cost that should not be included in the Rate Year forecast of Other Expense, the Commission 
should adopt the adjustment, as supported by both KEDLI and Staff.

proposed schedule given that a majority of these investments are in a pre-start-up or preliminary

the variances between the preliminary and final project costs ranged from minus or negative 100 
percent to plus or over an overspend of 918%.  In actuality, the costs experienced by the 
Companies’ have changed significantly and thus it is reasonable to believe that these costs could 
also change, significantly impacting the Rate Year budget.144

c. SAP S/4 HANA 
The Companies currently use SAP ECC 6.0, implemented in calendar year 2012, 
as its back-office platform supporting various essential functions, including Finance, Payroll, 
Human Resources, and Supply Chain operations.  SAP’s next generation platform is SAP’s 
Business Suite 4, also known as S/4 HANA.162  The Companies propose to replace SAP ECC 6.0 
with SAP S/4 HANA because SAP will no longer support the SAP ECC 6.0 by 2025.163 The 
Companies state, without the support, they would be unable to implement any critical security or 
compliance patches issued by SAP to address vulnerabilities discovered, software 
incompatibility with future products, and services offered by SAP, inability to scale in an 
increasing digital world to take advantage of leading practices, and overall higher operating

costs, decreased performance, and reduced overall reliability.164  The Companies plan to perform

be required to fund the Companies’ overspend of the budget developed in the discovery phase.

only reconciliation mechanism.171

e. 
Service Company Asset Recovery Charge 
National Grid’s Service Company owns or leases a number of shared assets that 
are used either by Service Company employees to provide services to affiliates or are used by the 
affiliates on a shared basis.  These are primarily shared office facilities, information technology 
equipment, and software platforms.  When the Service Company finances and owns the shared 
assets, it charges the affiliates an asset recovery charge based on a pre-tax return on the asset (net 
of deferred taxes) and booked depreciation expense.  This charge is recovered in the Company’s 
Service Company rent expense.  The Companies state in their testimony the asset recovery 
charge should be based on the Service Company’s equity structure, not the operating asset 
recovery charge of the operating companies.185   
Staff recommends using consistent a consistent common equity ratio and return 
on equity for KEDNY, KEDLI, and for the development of the revenue requirement for Service 
Company Rent expense.  As set forth in Section VII, below, Staff recommends a common equity 
ratio of 48% and a return on equity of 8.20% for KEDNY and KEDLI.  Using these rates would 
result in a pre-tax weighted average cost of capital of 7.58% and 7.54% for KEDNY and KEDLI, 
respectively.  We recommend that these rates be applied to assets at the Service Company level 
so as to avoid requiring customers to pay a higher return on these assets solely because the 
Companies have chosen to have a Service Company provide the assets.186  
The Companies argue that the Companies’ weighted average cost of capital is the 
appropriate rate of return component for the Service Company.187  Company Witness Cohen 
argues that the Service Company’s cost of long-term debt is lower than Niagara Mohawk’s long-

term debt, and therefore the proposed rate of return for Service Company is reasonable and

f. 
Downward-Only Reconciliation Mechanism 
Staff recommend adoption of a Service Company Rents IT and Gas Business 
Enablement (GBE) Program Net Utility Plant and Depreciation Expense Reconciliation 
Mechanism to protect customers from paying delivery rates higher than necessary to support the 
IT assets actually implemented.190 Staff’s mechanism would require the Companies to reconcile 
its respective actual IT and GBE program average net utility plant and depreciation expense 
revenue requirements to the forecast revenue requirements.191  At the end of the Rate Year, if the 
actual IT and GBE program average net utility plant and depreciation expense revenue 
requirement is less than the target average net utility plant and depreciation expense revenue 
requirement, the Companies would defer the revenue requirement impact for the benefit of 
customers.  The mechanism should be a one-way, downward only true-up. 
The Companies acknowledged Staff’s concerns over delays in the implementation 
of IT projects and agreed to a downward reconciliation mechanism with regard to service 
Company rent expense.192  However, on rebuttal, the Companies opposed Staff’s proposed 
downward-only Net Plant tracker for the GBE program.  The Companies opposed the tracker on

the basis that the mechanism is less relevant in a one-year litigated case, and the mechanism is

The Companies are correct, Staff proposed the same customer protections, which 
the Commission adopted in Case 17-G-0239.194  The Commission adopted a downward-only

necessary because the GBE project has continued to run into schedule changes and delays.197

dates.198  The schedule and cascading delays have continued to put upward pressure on costs.199  
Regardless of the number of years for the case it is important to protect customers and have 
customer protections for a program experiencing schedule changes and upward cost pressure.200    
The true-up mechanism, therefore, should reconcile only underspending to 
allocate these risks equitably between the Companies and customers. Staff recommends that the 
Commission adopt the downward-only mechanism for the GBE program, as discussed above.

h. 
Cyber-Security 
KEDNY and KEDLI proposes to complete 12 projects in the Rate Year, and eight 
in subsequent Data Years.  KEDNY and KEDLI also propose to complete 17 cyber security 
projects that are expected to be in service after the Historic Test Year, but prior to the Rate Year.  
There are an additional seven ongoing annual programs that will incur costs throughout the Rate 
Year and Data Years.208  
These projects will address a wide range of cyber security issues, including: 
Ensuring adequate security protection to cyber assets supporting critical reliability functions 
across KEDNY and KEDLI’s networks and systems; providing security information and event 
management capabilities to analyze security alerts in real time, and to collect, store and report on 
data for compliance and forensic analysis across critical assets and business functions; 
strengthening identity, access and authorization management capabilities to ensure that only 
authorized personnel can access critical assets and perform only those functions for which they 
are authorized; and system modernization cyber security framework utilizing a “defense-in-
depth” approach for ensuring a reliable and secure electricity and gas infrastructure and 
protecting the confidentiality and integrity of sensitive data. Defense in depth, or “layered 
security” as it is also known, reduces the chance of a successful attack by incorporating 
redundant security mechanisms.   This would require an attacker to circumvent each mechanism 
to gain access to a digital asset. 
The cyber security projects are forecast to require incremental capital investment 
of $27.8 million through FY 20, $10.2 million in the Rate Year, FY 21, $16.5 million in Data 
Year 1, $17.7 million in Data Year 2, and $17.3 million in Data Year 3.  Of the 36 projects cited 
above, there are five major cyber security proposals that are notable for both cost and their 
relative importance in achieving KEDNY and KEDLI’s proposed goals.  These projects are: 
High Value Discovery & Implementation, CNI Forensic Packet Capture, Enhanced DLP 
Gateway & Endpoint, CNI Security Enhancements Phase I, and Insider Threat Protection.209  
 
208  T. 977. 
209 T. 977 – 978.  


--- Page 51 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
47 
We believe that these investments are needed to address ongoing cyber threats.  
The implementation of these cyber security countermeasures are essential for establishing a high 
level of cyber security monitoring and protection.  The field of cyber security is one in which the 
risks, threat actors/vectors, and technologies involved are constantly changing and increasing in 
complexity at a breakneck pace.  This ever-escalating cyber threat to business and critical 
infrastructure information requires that utilities remain constantly vigilant and seek new and 
innovative ways to bolster their cyber security posture.210  
Each project in the cyber security program has its own Investment Proposal 
Summary, benefits case, cost estimate, list of benefitting companies, run the business 
breakdown, project plan, and project milestones.  The Information Security (IS) Portfolio 
Delivery Board reviews and approves each project based on that information.  A short form 
Investment Proposal paper for all projects approved by the IS Portfolio Delivery Board is 
provided to, and formally noted by, the US Sanctioning Committee.  Each project will report 
financial actual spending, forecasts, and budget variances back to the IS Portfolio Delivery 
Board.   
Despite the project development process just described, actual spending versus 
budget for cyber security for the previous five rate years was 23 percent less than forecasted.  
The Companies have also demonstrated a history of delaying projects due to shifting priorities.211  
Therefore, we also recommend that KEDNY and KEDLI’s cyber security programs be included 
in the IT project update meetings to ensure timely, consistent progress of the cyber security 
projects proposed in these proceedings.  The IT project update meetings are held quarterly to 
provide Staff with a progress report on KEDNY and KEDLI’s IT projects.  We also recommend 
that the Companies notify appropriate Department Staff as projects are sanctioned or partially 
sanctioned.212  
Staff supports the Cyber-Security projects proposed by the Companies, however, 
we recommend additional reporting and monitoring controls are implemented by the 
Commission to oversee the Companies progress on these critical and ever-evolving projects.

i. 
Service Company Rent Expense Model Calculation 
 
 
The Companies’ IT project assets remain on the Service Company’s books after 
KEDNY and KEDLI being using them.  The Service Company recovers the return on and return 
of investment from KEDNY, KEDLI, and other National Grid operating companies.  This 
recovery is calculated through a model used to calculate the appropriate amounts to be charged 
through Service Company Rent expense in each of the Companies’ revenue requirements.  
Staff’s proposed adjustments to IT reflect changes made to the Companies’ model.   
 
 
Staff’s Service Company Model had several errors in it as described in the 
Companies’ rebuttal.213  These errors included: (1) a double count of certain adjustments; (2) the 
application of a rate of return for certain adjustments was different than the rate of return that 
Staff otherwise recommended using; (3) clerical errors; and (4) the misapplication of the 
proposed 25.5 percent forecasting adjustments as a direct 25.5 percent reduction to Service 
Company rent expense.  These issues were addressed in the Staff Information Technology 
Panel’s Supplemental Testimony.214

i. 
Management Incentive Compensation 
The Companies request incentive compensation rate recovery. The variable pay 
amount requests are $6.876 million for the KEDNY ServCo and $2.379 million KEDNY OpCo, 
and $4.270 million for KEDLI ServCo and $0.891 million for KEDLI OpCo.  Witness Heaphy 
stated: 
If National Grid did not have a variable pay component, it would be out of step with 
the market and likely would not be able to attract the highly motivated employees 
necessary for KEDNY and KEDLI to deliver outstanding customer service in a 
reliable, efficient, and safe manner.218 
To support its management incentive compensation request, the Companies 
provided a total compensation and benefits benchmarking study that examined the total 
employee compensation and benefits package, inclusive of incentive pay, for approximately 92 
percent of the management positions at National Grid USA.219 The study compared the 
compensation packages of 38 utility and general industry peer companies.220  Staff reviewed the 
Companies’ benchmarking study methodology and determined the study demonstrates that the 
Companies’ compensation is in the reasonable range of plus or minus 10 percent of the market 
median.221  Staff also confirmed that the portion of the variable pay plan to be included in rates is 
based on performance targets that will advance the Commission’s goals and are not adverse to 
customers’ interests.222  Staff explained that the Companies’ incentive compensation request was 
in line with the Commission’s two-prong test for recovering incentive compensation in rates.223  
The Commission states that in order to obtain recovery from ratepayers of the costs of incentive 
pay plans, the utility must meet the criteria of at least one prong of the two-prong test. Either (1) 
demonstrate overall compensation is reasonable to similarly situated companies with no elements

ii. 
Labor Escalation Rate 
 
 
During the Historic Test Year, National Grid increased management wages by 
3.25 percent. 3.00 percent took effect on July 1, 2018 and 0.25 percent took effect on October 1, 
2018.  Following the Historic Test Year, and as a means to forecast the Rate Year the 
Companies’ applied  a 3.35 percent management wage increase on July 1, 2019, which includes 
a 3.1 percent average increase in base pay provided to National Grid’s entire management 
workforce, and the remaining 0.25 percent represents increases associated with career path 
progressions.  Effective July 1, 2020 the Companies applied a 3.1 percent annual management 
wage increase, and for each of the subsequent years.224   
Staff proposes a 3.0 percent escalation factor based upon WorldAtWork’s median 
3.0 percent “Total Salary Budget Increase.” WorldatWork is a non-profit human resources 
association and a leading compensation authority for professionals and organizations focused on 
compensation, benefits, and total rewards.225  The Companies argue that Staff did not properly 
address the timing and components of its proposed wage increases.226 
 
 
In response, Staff requested the Companies to confirm that the 2019-2020 
WorldatWork Salary Budget Survey results report indicates that total salary budget increases 
projected for the United States in 2020 is 3.2 percent for the mean and 3.0 percent for the 
median, and that WorldatWork’s total salary budget increase percentage captures base salary 
increases, merit budgets increases, salary structure adjustments, promotional increases, and 
variable pay plan increases.  Subsequently, the Companies argue that the WorldAtWork’s total 
salary budget increase percentage typically only includes base salary increases and merit

b. 
Vacancy Rate Adjustment 
 
 
To forecast Rate Year Labor expense, KEDNY and KEDLI began with the Labor 
headcount as December 31, 2018, or the end of the Historic Test Year, and then made 
adjustments to reflect conditions expected in the Rate Year.  The Companies removed employees 
from management and represented headcounts who are on long-term leave, and not expected to 
return.  Then, the Companies added the average headcounts for seasonal and temporary 
employees who were not captured in the December 31, 2018 employee headcounts.236  The 
Companies did not forecast a vacancy rate for the Rate Year.  Therefore, the Companies assume 
that every position forecasted for the Rate Year will be fully staffed for every workday of the 
Rate Year.237 
 
 
Staff recommended a vacancy rate adjustment for the KEDNY and KEDLI 
operating companies.  More specifically, Staff adjusted the number of full-time employee 
equivalents (FTEs) who directly charge Labor costs to KEDNY and KEDLI, but not for the 
FTEs that originate from the National Grid USA Service Company.238  The vacancy rate is the 
number of unfilled positions within KEDNY and KEDLI, divided by the targeted employee 
level.239  As a result, Staff recommends reducing KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s Rate Year Labor 
expense by $2.759 million and $3.229 million, respectively.240 
 
 
To establish a baseline, Staff began with the Companies’ adjusted headcounts as 
of September 30, 2015.  These adjusted headcounts were used in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-
0059 to forecast Labor expense for the Companies’ rate plans adopted by the 2016 Rate Order.  
The adjusted headcounts as of September 30, 2015 were 1,281, and 662 for KEDNY and 
KEDLI, respectively.  Staff then added the number of approved incremental FTEs from the 2016 
Rate Order issued on December 16, 2016 in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059.  That Rate Order

When comparing the total headcounts authorized in the 2016 Rate Order to the 
adjusted actual headcount at the end of the Historic Test Year, Staff observed that the actual 
headcounts were lower, by 48 FTEs for KEDNY and 63 FTEs for KEDLI.  Put another way, at 
the end of the Historic Test Year, KEDNY had a vacancy rate of 3.16% and KEDLI had a 
vacancy rate of 8.20%.244  This comparison is appropriate, because both the adjusted headcounts 
as of September 30, 2015 and December 31, 2018 have been adjusted for employees on leave 
and both serve as a basis for projected rate recovery.245  Further, this adjustment will not result in 
a double count of any savings.  As provided in response to IR DPS-940, any FTEs separated 
from National Grid following the Historic Test Year were Service Company employees.  Staff’s 
adjustment pertains specifically to KEDNY and KEDLI’s operating company headcounts.  
Therefore, because Staff did not make an adjustment to the Service Company headcount, there 
would be no double count of savings.246 
 
 
One of the main reasons for KEDNY and KEDLI’s variance in headcount is due 
to the hiring of incremental FTEs.  KEDNY requested an incremental 156.3 FTEs for the Rate 
Year.  Assuming Staff’s recommendations are adopted, that number would decrease by 18.8

internally.  It is reasonable to assume the Companies will continue to hire a portion of its 
incremental FTEs internally.249  As stated in response to IR DPS-470, the Companies’ frequently 
fill positions by internal promotions or transfers.250  By doing so, this better aligns employees’ 
experience and skills with the work.  It also helps the Companies manage attrition.  In the event 
an employee in an existing position transitions to an incremental position, that employee’s 
former position is vacant unless and until it is “backfilled.”  Staff’s analysis demonstrates that 
this occurs. 
 
 
As previously discussed, when comparing the authorized headcounts from the 
2016 Rate Order of 1,517 and 768 for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, to the adjusted 
headcounts as of December 31, 2018 of 1,469 and 705 for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, 
KEDNY had 48 fewer FTEs than allowed and KEDLI had 63 fewer FTEs than allowed.  
Accordingly, there are either lags, or periods of time where the Companies are unable to back-fill 
vacated positions; or the Companies are unable to or decide not to backfill the vacated positions 
at all. 
 
 
Absent applying a vacancy rate, should the Companies hire an internal employee 
for an incremental position and do not back-fill the vacated position, the Companies would 
continue to receive rate recovery for the vacant position.251  Applying a vacancy rate to the 
Companies’ proposed staffing levels for the Rate Year helps ensure that customers are not 
paying the Companies for non-existent FTEs in unfilled positions.

On rebuttal, the Companies raise four objections to Staff’s recommended vacancy 
adjustment.  The Commission should reject each of the Companies’ objections. 
 
 
First, the Companies assert that, by using adjusted headcounts as of December 31, 
2018, which only include positions actually filled by employees as of that date, the Companies 
already removed vacant positions from their headcounts.252  The Companies assert that applying 
Staff’s recommended vacancy rate to the adjusted headcounts results in a double count of vacant 
positions.253 
 
 
However, there is no double count of vacancies.  Indeed, without applying Staff’s 
recommended vacancy adjustment, the Companies are underestimating the amount of vacant 
positions likely to occur during the Rate Year.  The Companies’ proposed forecasts fail to reflect 
the fluctuation in headcount which occurs as a normal part of doing business.  Yet, the 
Companies acknowledge this in their responses to IRs DPS-690,254 and DPS-691,255 in which 
they stated that headcounts can vary month to month, or even day to day, due to several variables 
such as attrition related to retirements, terminations, voluntary separations, and long-term leave, 
as well as the filling of positions that have been vacated.256 
 
 
Second, the Companies try to shift the focus from KEDNY and KEDLI, which 
they admit did have less FTEs in 2019 than the amount supported in the rates adopted by the 
2016 Rate Order, to the number of National Grid Service Company employees.257  According to 
the Companies there were 1,306 more National Grid Service Company employees who may 
allocate their time, or a portion of their time, to KEDNY and KEDLI.258 
 
 
The Companies are merely trying to misdirect attention without identifying any 
actual errors in Staff’s recommended vacancy adjustment.  Staff’s adjustment is specific to the 
KEDNY and KEDLI operating Companies.  A significant increase in National Grid Service 
Company FTEs does not necessarily translate to additional time being charged to the KEDNY

in this case in the same methodology used in the 2016 rate cases for KEDNY and KEDLI and the

every rate case, facts and issues are presented differently.  In the instant proceedings, the Historic

proceedings, Staff has been able to directly observe the variance between the forecast of FTEs 
used to set rates for calendar year 2018, the Historic Test Year, and actual FTEs in that same

2016 rate cases, KEDNY and KEDLI had last had a full review of their rates completed in rate 
cases that began in 2006, ten years prior.  Those 2006 rate cases resulted in rates for five rate 
years, from 2007 through 2012.  Thus this same comparison was not available in the 2016 rate 
cases.  Similarly, the test year used for the 2012260 and 2017261 rate cases for Niagara Mohawk 
did not directly overlap with a specifically forecasted rate year from the previous rate orders for 
Niagara Mohawk.

Fourth, the Companies state that their proposed the incremental FTEs would 
perform incremental work expected during the Rate Year, and an application of a vacancy rate to 
the incremental FTEs would result in an insufficient number of FTEs to perform incremental 
work.262  However, the Companies argument is incorrect.  Staff’s vacancy rate adjustment does 
not undermine the Companies’ request for incremental FTEs.  Instead, Staff’s vacancy rate 
merely ensures that the Labor forecast for the Rate Year reflects the number of FTEs we can 
reasonably expect KEDNY and KEDLI to have during the Rate Year.  As explained above, there 
are many reasons that KEDNY and KEDLI will experience vacancies during the Rate Year, 
including, without limitation, the lag in backfilling the previous positions of employees who are 
promoted.  Rather than ignore that reality, Staff has looked to the Companies’ recent experience 
in the rate years under the 2016 Rate Order.  The Companies’ experience demonstrates that, 
when they are adding a significant number of FTEs, as they propose to do here, vacancies will 
occur, and should be recognized.  Staff’s recommended adjustment applies the lessons of the 
2016 rate cases to the instant cases.  While allowing the vast majority of the Companies’ 
proposed incremental FTEs, Staff’s adjustment merely ensures that the forecast of Labor expense 
for the Rate Year reflects the vacancies that are bound to occur during the Rate Year.  This helps 
to ensure that KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s customers will not pay for non-existent employees. 
 
 
In conclusion, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended vacancy rate.  
This results in reducing KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s Rate Year Labor expense by $2.759 million 
and $3.229 million, respectively. 
c. 
Downward Only New Hire True-Up 
 
 
In these cases, the Companies propose to hire a significant number of incremental 
employees.  As of this brief, Staff recommends allowing KEDNY and KEDLI rate recovery for 
137.5 and 78.7 incremental FTEs, respectively.  In light of these plans for significant hires, Staff 
recommends continuing the New Hire True-up deferral mechanism put in place in the 2016 Rate 
Order.  The Companies propose to discontinue its New Hire True-up deferral mechanism.263 
 
 
Staff recommended that the Commission should continue the downward-only 
New Hire True-up, such that, for every month that an authorized FTE remains unfilled, the

The Companies’ record of hiring the proposed FTEs since they filed these cases
