it is reasonable to assume that the Companies will experience similar delays in hiring.  
Customers should be protected from paying for FTEs the Companies fail to hire by the proposed 
start dates. 
 
 
In opposition to the New Hire True-up, the Companies state that there is no need 
for a downward-only true-up in a one-year case and that the current true-up was agreed to as part 
of a comprehensive settlement.266  The Companies’ position fails to acknowledge that the 
Companies are already delayed in hiring the incremental FTEs they requested in these cases.

throughout the Rate Year.  While it is true that the similar true-up adopted in the 2016 Rate 
Order resulted from a joint proposal, and that these cases are litigated does not undercut the 
demonstrated need for implementing a New Hire True-up here.  Indeed, due to the 
implementation of the true-up in the 2016 Rate Order, the Companies have deferred money for 
the benefit of customers, because they failed to hire their then-planned incremental FTEs on 
schedule.  This provides additional evidence to suggest that the Companies will be delayed in 
hiring the planned incremental FTEs during the Rate Year as well. 
 
264 Ex. 2379 – 2380, 2440.

Additionally, the Companies argue that a similar reconciliation was not included 
in the 2018 Niagara Mohawk Rate Order.267 The Companies argument is irrelevant.  First, as the 
Companies note regarding the 2016 Rate Order, the 2018 Niagara Mohawk Rate Order adopted a 
comprehensive joint proposal, agreed to by parties and as such, cannot be relied upon to 
demonstrate what is reasonable in the absence of a settlement.  However, it should be noted that 
the 2018 Niagara Mohawk Rate Order did included an adjustment to account for the delay in 
hiring.  Thus, even the 2018 Niagara Mohawk Rate Order did include protections for customers 
against the utility’s delayed hiring of planned incremental FTEs.  In these cases, rather than 
make an adjustment to the revenue requirement for the Rate Year to reflect an estimate of 
delayed hiring, Staff recommends that the Companies be required to true-up the costs of the new 
FTEs after the fact. 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation lessens the risk that customers or the Companies will 
overpay or under-recover, respectively, the costs of the new FTEs.  The Companies’ record of 
delays in hiring incremental FTEs, both during the rate plans adopted by the 2016 Rate Order 
and pursuant to the Companies’ own proposals set forth in these cases, demonstrates the need to 
protect customers from paying for new FTEs that are not hired in a timely fashion.  Accordingly, 
the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation for a New Hire True-up.

will change based on the Labor expense forecast adopted by the Commission.  There is no 
dispute regarding the calculation of this item.268 
6. 
Transportation Expense 
 
 
The Companies forecast of Rate Year Transportation expense comprises of lease 
expense, motor fuel, and other expenses.  We recommend adjusting the Companies’ lease 
 
267 T. 2011, 2262. 
268 T. 2010 – 2011, 2261 – 2262.

expense forecast.  Specifically, we recommend making downward adjustments of $0.619 million

The Companies use leases for their vehicles.  This means that the Companies 
make periodic payments for each vehicle for a defined lease term.  The Companies’ state, in 
response to IRs DPS-958270 and DPS-959,271 that they plan vehicle replacements based on the 
expected lifecycle for time and usage. The Company’s lease terms are generally one year less 
than the initial planned lifecycle to minimize loss exposure due to mechanical failures.  
Following expiration of the leasing term, the Companies take ownership of the vehicle.  After 
transfer of ownership, the KEDNY and KEDLI have the option to continue using the vehicle or 
replace the vehicle at that point or any point thereafter.272

plan to replace prior to and during the Rate Year.

reducing auction proceeds by $0.218 million.  Thus, Staff recommends a net downward 
adjustment of $0.559 million to KEDLI’s Transportation expense.290

Productivity 
 
 
The Companies proposed a credit relating to the estimated Productivity

represents a reduction in the revenue requirements. However, Productivity is a flow-through 
item.  Accordingly, the amount to be reflected in the Rate Year is dependent upon the Labor 
expense forecast adopted by the Commission.  There is no dispute regarding the calculation of 
this expense item.299

9. 
Site Investigation & Remediation 
 
 
In February of 2011, the Commission commenced a proceeding to examine, on a 
generic basis, the treatment of costs for utilities’ site investigation and remediation (SIR) of 
hazardous waste, primarily at former manufactured gas plant (MGP) sites previously owned and 
operated by the utility companies or their predecessor companies.306  SIR projects, including 
investigation and remediation of former MGP sites, are primarily overseen by the New York 
State Department of Environmental Conservation (DEC).  The United States Environmental 
Protection Agency (EPA) regulates the investigation and remediation of Federal Superfund sites.  
KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s SIR expenses generally consist of the costs of assessment, monitoring, 
cleanup and restoration of sites containing environmental contamination for which the 
companies have been found to be wholly or partially liable by the DEC or EPA.  The 
Companies’ seek recovery of these SIR expenses through rates. 
 
 
KEDLI is wholly or partially responsible for 29 MGP sites, of which 13 have 
received a “No Further Action” determination with no ongoing obligation.  KEDNY is wholly or 
partially responsible for 27 MGP sites, five of which have received a “No Further Action” 
determination, and two federal Superfund sites associated with the Gowanus Canal and 
Newtown Creek waterways.307 
 
 
KEDNY’s current annual base rate recovery for SIR costs is $65.288 million.  
This includes annual recovery of one-tenth of the forecast SIR deferral balance as of December 
31, 2016, or $18.521 million, and $46.767 million for forecasted expenses at its MGP sites.  Due 
to the uncertainty of KEDNY’s liabilities for the Gowanus Canal and Newtown Creek Superfund 
sites at the time of the 2016 rate case, costs associated with those sites are not currently included 
in base rates.308  Alternatively, the Commission Order adopting the terms of the Joint Proposal in 
Case 16-G-0058 and Case 16-G-0059, authorizes KEDNY to defer any costs related to those 
sites and implemented an SIR Recovery Surcharge.309  This surcharge mechanism is triggered if

10. 
Savings (Accelerate Program) 
 
 
In Fiscal Year 2019, National Grid announced the Accelerate Program, which it 
describes as an ambitious, three-year efficiency challenge.319  The Companies have developed 
and continue to develop many initiatives to reach their Accelerate Program targets.  Each 
initiative is placed at an implementation level based on its stage of development, ranging from 
Level Zero to Level Five, where Level Zero initiatives are purely ideas and Level Five initiatives 
have been fully implemented and the savings have been realized.320  The Companies reflected 
some of the outcomes of the Accelerate program in the Savings line item within O&M.  In this 
line item, the Companies reflect savings from initiatives in Levels Four and Five, that have not 
been reflected elsewhere in the revenue requirements, net of costs to achieve. 
 
 
Staff recommended three main adjustments.  First, Staff recommends removing 
the costs to achieve, as these were one-time costs that occurred before the Rate Year and are 
unlikely to recur during the Rate Year.  Second, Staff recommends reflecting the Companies’ 
forecasted savings from Level Three initiatives in the revenue requirements for the Rate Year.  
Third, Staff recommends updating the savings to reflect the latest known information regarding 
the initiatives at Levels Four and Five.  The Companies oppose Staff’s first two 
recommendations, but agree with the third recommendation. 
a. 
Costs to Achieve 
 
 
In forecasting Savings for the Rate Year, the Companies propose to amortize the 
costs to achieve over four years.  This translates into netting one quarter of the costs to achieve 
against the savings in the Rate Year.321  The costs to achieve represent severance payments 
associated with the Companies’ staffing level review in connection with the Accelerate 
 
319 T. 1891, 2154. 
320 Ex. 504, p. 362 (Response to IR DPS-394, Attachment 25). 
321 Ex. 13, Schedule 34. 
 


--- Page 77 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
73 
Program.322  The severance payments related to this staffing review totaled $0.845 million for 
KEDNY and $0.527 million for KEDLI.  Thus, the Companies propose netting $0.211 million 
for KEDNY and $0.132 million for KEDLI against Rate Year Savings, one-quarter of the total 
previous-period costs.323 
 
 
These costs should not be recovered in the Rate Year.  First, these severance 
payments are a one-time cost and will not recur during the Rate Year.  The Companies have not 
presented evidence that they will incur such costs in the Rate Year.  Second, the Companies have 
booked these costs to expense and have already flowed-through expense.  Thus, they already had 
an earnings impact.  Typically, utilities cannot collect such prior-period costs in rates during 
subsequent periods, here, the Rate Year. 
 
 
Third, the Companies will realize savings resulting from these severance payment 
costs in the period before the Commission sets rates in these cases.  The Companies’ 
shareholders will get to retain those pre-Rate Year savings.  Specifically, during the period 
between terminating the employees and April 1, 2020, when rates were scheduled to be reset in 
these proceedings, KEDNY and KEDLI calculated that the reduced operating costs resulting 
from these severance payment costs will amount to $2.032 million and $1.290 million, 
respectively.324  Moreover, this estimate is conservative, considering no wage increases were 
considered when calculating this estimate.325  The total savings the Companies will reap from 
terminating these employees is almost two and a half times the total severance costs the 
Companies state they have incurred to achieve the savings.  As the Companies have already 
retained more savings than the costs they incurred, it would not only be unreasonable, it would 
be unconscionable to force customers to pay for these costs during the Rate Year. 
 
 
In rebuttal testimony, the Companies primarily argue that it is unreasonable to 
disallow these costs without removing the savings.326  In particular, the Companies allege that 
removing the costs and not the associated savings from the revenue requirement is inconsistent 
 
322 Ex. 504, pp. 613 – 616 (Response to IR DPS-754). 
323 Ex. 13, Schedule 34. 
324 Ex. 635, p. 542 (Response to IR DPS-1010). 
325 Ex. 635, p. 543 (Response to IR DPS-1024). 
326 T. 2024, 2274. 
 


--- Page 78 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
74 
with positions taken by Staff in previous rate proceedings.327  The Companies point to only one 
prior instance, in the testimony of the Staff Accounting Panel in Case 10-E-0050.328  The 
Companies quote from page 284 of that panel’s testimony, which states: “Similarly, if the rate 
year cost estimates are removed, the expected savings should not be reflected.  It is not 
reasonable to exclude costs and include expected savings, or vice versa.” 
 
 
With regard to the specific quote relied on by the Companies, it does not apply to 
this situation.  In Case 10-E-0050 the Staff Accounting Panel was talking about costs and savings 
that will occur during the same period, the rate year.  In contrast, in the instant cases, the 
Companies are requesting recovery in the Rate Year of costs incurred during the Historic Test 
Year.  The Companies seek to tie those costs to savings that will actually occur in the Rate Year, 
two different time periods.  Thus, Staff’s position in these cases is not inconsistent with the 
position Staff took in Case 10-E-0050.  Indeed, it is the Companies that are trying to violate the 
principle they cite, by seeking recovery during the Rate Year of costs incurred before the Rate 
Year, while not providing customers with the benefit of the savings that materialized before the 
start of the Rate Year. 
 
 
Staff recommends removing the costs to achieve that the Companies seek to net 
against the Rate Year savings resulting from the Accelerate Program.  Staff’s adjustment 
removes $0.211 million for KEDNY and $0.132 million for KEDLI from the Savings line item.  
Staff’s adjustment reasonably removes these costs as they occurred in a prior period and will not 
recur during the Rate Year.  Moreover, The Companies have already achieved, and retained 
savings in excess of these costs.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
recommendation. 
b.

In forecasting Savings for the Rate Year, the Companies included the savings for 
all initiatives then at Levels Four and Five.329  The Companies did not include any savings in the 
Rate Year associated with initiatives then at Level Three.  However, the Companies state that 
one of the reasons the Companies are including a productivity adjustment is to account for the

the other Level Three initiatives, the savings for which should be reflected in the Rate Year.  The 
Companies provided no support demonstrating the savings for this initiative will not materialize 
until FY 22.

After our adjustments, we recommend reflecting savings associated with Level 
Three initiatives of $10.802 million for KEDNY and $3.080 million for KEDLI in the Rate Year 
revenue requirements for five reasons. 
 
 
First, the savings from Level Three initiatives are sound enough to include in the 
Companies’ internal budgets.338  The Companies themselves describe the savings forecast for 
initiatives at Level Three as “bankable” and “sound enough to ‘take to the bank’” in budgets.339  
The Companies would not describe the forecasted savings from Level Three initiatives as such if 
they believed these savings were uncertain or precarious.  The Companies have expressed 
confidence in these initiatives internally, and therefore the savings for these initiatives should be 
reflected in the Rate Year. 
 
 
Second, throughout the Accelerate program to date, initiatives have moved 
quickly through the implementation levels.340  On average it takes an initiative about two months 
to progress from Level Three to Level Five.  Put another way, once the Companies develop an 
implementation plan for an initiative, it has only taken about two months to implement the 
initiative and realize the savings.  Thus, it is reasonable to expect that initiatives currently at 
Level Three will progress to Level Four or Level Five, and the Companies will realize the 
forecasted savings, before the end of the Rate Year on March 31, 2021. 
 
 
Third, the results of the Accelerate program to date demonstrate that the actual 
savings realized for initiatives that have reached Level Five do not materially deviate from the 
forecasted savings when the initiatives were at Level Three.341  The Companies provided the 
Level Three total savings estimate for each initiative that had reached Level Five.342  Comparing 
the estimates from when the initiatives were at Level Three to the actual total realized savings, 
the savings amounts are identical for each initiative.343  This yet again demonstrates that the 
savings the Companies themselves forecast for initiatives at Level Three are relatively stable 
through implementation.  Thus, there is little risk in relying on the Companies’ Level Three

Fourth, Staff’s recommendation to reflect savings from only initiatives at Level 
Three is conservative.  Staff does not propose reflecting any savings associated with initiatives 
presently at Level One ore Level Two, even though one could reasonably assume that at least 
some of those initiatives will be executed, and savings realized, before the end of the Rate 
Year.344  As of July 2019, National Grid, including but not limited to KEDNY and KEDLI, 
estimated realizing total savings of approximately $175 million from initiatives then at Level 
One and Level Two.345  While Staff recognizes that the estimates for initiatives at lower levels 
may be less “bankable,” it would be unreasonable to assume that no savings will result from 
these initiatives during the Rate Year.  Given the increased level of uncertainty, Staff does not 
recommend reflecting savings for initiatives at Levels One and Two in the Rate Year revenue 
requirements.  However, National Grid continues to develop these initiatives provides yet more 
confidence that the Companies will realize Accelerate program savings at least at the level Staff 
recommends reflecting in the Rate Year revenue requirements. 
 
 
Fifth, the Rate Year savings that the Companies forecast will result from 
Accelerate program initiatives presently at Level Three should be reflected in the Rate Year 
revenue requirements separately from the productivity adjustment.  Perhaps unsurprisingly, the 
Companies assert that the savings from initiatives at Level Three should simply be assumed to be 
captured by the traditional 1% productivity adjustment discussed in Section V.A.7, above.346  
Because the savings that will result from Accelerate program initiatives at Level 3 are identified 
and quantifiable, those savings should be specifically reflected in the Rate Year revenue 
requirements, in addition to the traditional 1% productivity adjustment.

our policy that New York’s utilities can reasonably be expected to achieve nonspecific, 
unquantified and unidentified productivity gains.”347  Indeed, the Commission has specifically

In opposition to Staff’s recommendation, the Companies make a number of 
assertions.  Most notably, they state that the pace of initiatives progressing from Level Three to 
Level Four has slowed.  They assert that the initiatives currently at Level Three have been in 
there on average for 150 days.349  While this is a matter of statistics, it does not provide a reason 
for rejecting Staff’s recommendation.  The initiatives currently at Level Three may have been 
delayed, however, the Companies have not seen fit to cancel them.  Thus, the Companies must 
still judge these initiatives as “bankable.”  Moreover, even if it takes an additional 150 days from 
the date of this brief for these initiatives to move beyond Level Three, that is still well before the 
end of the Rate Year. 
 
 
Additionally, Staff inquired as to why the initiatives currently in Level Three have 
remained in Level Three longer than previous initiatives, the recurring theme was due to 
resource limitations, competing priorities and increased workloads.350  Reflecting the savings for 
these initiatives, which the Companies have stated are “bankable” in the Rate Year revenue 
requirements will incent the Companies to take action to ensure that these initiatives progress to 
Levels Four and Five, thus producing the forecasted savings. 
 
 
Staff’s adjustment to include the Companies’ forecasted savings for Accelerate 
program initiatives currently at Level Three in the Rate Year revenue requirements should be 
adopted.  The Companies have forecast these savings to result from these initiatives, and the 
Commission should hold the Companies to their forecasts.  Furthermore, including the savings 
associated with IL3 initiatives will incent the Companies to dedicate appropriate time and 
resources to implement these initiatives.  Additionally, at this time, Staff recommends not 
making further updates to the savings for initiatives at Level Three for the purpose of setting 
rates in these proceedings.

11. 
Paving 
 
 
Paving expense is the expense the Companies incur to restore pavement in areas 
where they do construction work in public ways.  The New York City Department of 
Transportation recently instituted Highway Rules §§2-01 through 2-09, 2-11 through 2-14, and 
2-20.  The Companies had to comply with these new rules for the first full year during the 
Historic Test Year.  The rules greatly impacted KEDNY’s Historic Test Year paving expense, as

Expense, related to the portion of LL30 costs get charged to Paving expense.  Since KEDNY 
updated its LL30 forecast, projecting that less LL30 work will be performed in the Rate Year, 
that change needs to be reflected in Paving expense.  As explained in response to IR DPS-869,358 
the amount related to Paving equals $1.177 million.  Therefore, Staff recommend decreasing the 
Rate Year forecast of Paving expense by $1.177 million, or a Rate Year forecast for Paving 
expense of $30.465 million.359  KEDNY agreed to this adjustment360 
 
 
In addition, Staff recommends instituting a downward-only reconciliation 
mechanism for KEDNY’s Paving Expense.  A downward-only reconciliation for this expense is 
reasonable, because it is difficult to ascertain the accuracy of KEDNY’s Rate Year request.  
KEDNY requested Rate Year Paving expense of $30.465 million, or an increase of $19.845 
million from the Paving expense allowance for calendar year 2019 under the 2016 Rate Order.  
According to the response to IR DPS-244,361 the primary driver of the increase is due to the 
change in the City of New York’s paving regulations.  KEDNY fully implemented changes 
reflecting these revised paving requirements by April 1, 2017, which has imposed significant 
requirements on KEDNY.362  Calendar year 2018, i.e., the Historic Test Year was the first full 
year under the new paving rules.  Thus, there is no prior year data to compare to the Historic Test

358 Ex. 504, p. 668.

Year to judge whether the amount incurred in that year would be representative of the amounts 
that will be incurred in future years.  Thus, Staff’s proposed downward-only reconciliation 
mechanism protects customers in the event KEDNY under-spends its rate allowance, as the 
difference would be deferred for the benefit of customers.  Further, the downward-only true-up 
mechanism also incents KEDNY to manage its paving costs efficiently to avoid exceeding the 
amount recovered through rates. 
 
 
On rebuttal, KEDNY argued that there is no basis for a downward-only deferral 
of Paving expense.  KEDNY explains that Staff has remarked several times throughout its filing 
in opposing the few symmetrical true-ups proposed.  Further, KEDNY explains that deferrals are 
generally not required in a one-year rate case.363  KEDNY continues, and states that as part of a 
multi-year rate plan, KEDNY would be willing to support a full symmetrical reconciliation of 
Paving expense, but is opposed to a downward-only reconciliation.364 
 
 
KEDNY’s arguments should be rejected.  As explained above, there is uncertainty 
regarding the amount of Paving expense KEDNY will incur during the Rate Year; however this 
uncertainty is asymmetrical.  The Historic Test Year was the first full year under the new paving 
rules.  Therefore, as time continues, Staff would expect KEDNY to become more familiar with 
the new paving rules and develop a more efficient process.  In doing so, this would result in 
lower costs, and customers should be credited for any savings KEDNY may realize.  Indeed, 
implementing a downward-only reconciliation provides a reasonable incentive for KEDNY to 
more efficiently manage these costs, so as to avoid incurring greater costs than those recovered 
through rates. 
 
 
Second, KEDNY never proposed a symmetrical reconciliation in its initial filing.  
This is significant, as the Companies have not been shy about seeking symmetrical 
reconciliations for other expenses.  For example, the Companies propose to continue the current 
property tax reconciliation, but modify it to a full symmetrical reconciliation, as discussed in 
Section V.D.1, below.365  If KEDNY expected a significant increase in the Rate Year, then one 
would reasonably have expected KEDNY to propose a reconciliation to protect its own interests.

reasonable protection for customers and should be adopted. 
B. 
Amortization of Regulatory Deferrals 
 
 
For the Rate Year, both KEDNY and KEDLI limit the amortization of regulatory 
deferrals to continuing the amortization of SIR costs authorized in the 2016 Rate Order.  The 
annual amounts amortized for KEDNY and KEDLI are $18.521 million and $14.168 million, 
respectively.366  Staff agreed that the annual amounts currently being amortized for SIR should 
continue. 
 
 
Staff also recommend amortizing regulatory deferral balance amounts for gas 
safety negative revenue adjustments, unexpended energy efficiency fund amounts, and 
unexpended economic development amounts.  The proposed amortization of the negative 
revenue adjustments, and unexpended energy efficiency fund amounts, either completely, or 
partially, offset the associated program costs.367 
 
 
For the gas safety negative revenue adjustment deferred balances, Staff reflected 
the amortization of $5.852 million and $1.231 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  
The unexpended energy efficiency fund amounts represent a five-year amortization of the 
estimated deferral amounts as of April 1, 2020.  Therefore, the annual amounts amortized are 
$7.295 million and $2.050 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.368  In addition, Staff 
recommends amortizing economic development amounts of $0.500 million for each of KEDNY 
and KEDLI to offset the incremental amounts of the grant program costs reflected in operation 
and maintenance expenses.369  Further, Staff recommended that the Commission require the 
Companies to track and reconcile the amounts being amortized to ensure that the use of the 
amounts is in compliance with what the Commission authorizes in those specific areas.

KEDLI in the amounts of $1.75 million and $2.25 million is reflected within the amortization of 
regulatory deferrals line item on the income statement, which results in a net rate allowance of

2. 
Gas Safety Negative Revenue Adjustments 
 
 
With regard to gas safety, the Companies supported using incurred NRAs to fund 
the residential methane detector, incremental instrument and regulation control line survey, and 
GPS mapping programs.371  NYC was opposed to this concept and stated that the “use of NRAs 
should not be conditioned upon, or tied to, specific capital programs.”372  NYC further stated that 
the Companies “should immediately return the full balance of historic NRAs, with interest, to 
customers” and the these “amounts should be applied as a credit against the overall revenue 
requirement so that they are not dependent on use for any specific capital program.”373  Staff 
supports using the incurred NRA balances to offset pipeline-safety related programs, such as 
residential methane detectors, low-pressure main valve installations, and the mapping of 
instrument and regulation control lines.  Staff also recommended that KEDNY be required to file 
a plan for the use of any excess negative revenue adjustment funds.374  In their rebuttal, the 
Companies maintained that for a one-year case, the residential methane detector, incremental 
instrument and regulation control line survey, and GPS mapping programs should remain in 
rates.375  The Companies refuted NYC’s position in that the practice of deferring gas safety 
related NRAs for future gas safety related programs is consistent with the practice for other 
LDCs and should be continued.376  By allowing the use unspent gas safety related NRAs on 
future gas safety related programs, the Companies would be provided with the opportunity to 
enhance the benefits provided to customers without increasing customer bill impacts.  Therefore, 
the practice of using unspent gas safety related NRAs on future gas safety related programs 
should be allowed, and the costs associated with the residential methane detector, incremental 
instrument and regulation control line survey, and GPS mapping programs should be offset with 
the incurred NRAs.

C. 
Depreciation 
The Companies propose to accelerate the depreciation of their LPP assets to align 
with their program to complete the removal of LPP assets over the next 20 years.384  To 
accomplish this, the Companies proposed new depreciation accrual rates and net salvage rates in 
order to recover all costs related to the removal of LPP within a 20-year period.385  The proposed 
net salvage rates reflect the costs to retire LPP assets over the three years of the LPP removal 
program from 2016-2018.386  The Companies proposed an increase in depreciation expense of 
$27.5 million for KEDNY and $24.5 million for KEDLI.387  KEDNY had a Historic Test Year 
depreciation expense of $100.8 million, and KEDLI had a Historic Test Year depreciation 
expense of $71.5 million.388  The Companies’ proposal would represent approximately a 30% 
increase over the Historic Test Year depreciation expense. 
Staff recommends moving to a 30-year amortization period in these 
proceedings.389  While Staff agrees with the need to increase depreciation rates, Staff is 
concerned with potential impacts on customers’ bills if depreciation was shortened to the

Property Taxes 
 
 
The Companies developed their forecast of Rate Year Property Tax expense by 
applying a two-year growth rate to the Historic Test Year Property Tax expense.  The Companies 
used Fiscal Year Property Tax amounts to develop their two-year growth rate.400  Ultimately, the 
Companies propose Rate Year Property Tax expense forecast of $168.094 million for KEDNY 
an $181.881 million for KEDLI.401  Additionally, the Companies proposed a fully symmetrical 
reconciliation mechanism for Property Tax.402 
 
 
Staff proposed a three-year growth rate based on calendar year Property Tax 
amounts.  Based on that premise, Staff’s testimony reduced KEDNY’s revenue requirement 
request by $1.274 million, and KEDLI’s by $2.326 million.403  Staff now agrees with using fiscal 
year amounts to develop the growth rate.  Relying on fiscal years provides the most recent data 
for forecasting Property Tax expense.  Staff maintains its recommendation to use a three-year 
growth rate.  Three years is an appropriate and reasonable measurement of time to establish 
enough data points to gather a trend line as it relates to Property Tax expense.  This provides

E. 
Amortization of Excess Accumulated Deferred Federal Income Taxes 
 
 
In December 2017, the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act was enacted which reduced 
the federal income tax rate from 35% to 21%.  At that time, the Companies recorded the rate 
change reduction to the accumulated deferred income tax (ADIT) with an offset to an income tax 
regulatory liability account.  This represents the customer benefit of the lowering of the federal 
income tax rate.  The Companies split the excess ADIT between protected plant, unprotected 
plant, net operating loss, and unprotected non-plant balances.408 
 
 
The IRS normalization regulation require the Average Rate Assumption Method 
(ARAM) as the proper method to calculate the amortization of the protected plant excess ADIT.  
The Companies have calculated ARAM lives for KEDLI and KEDNY of 47 years and 44 years, 
respectively.  The Companies propose amortizing the excess ADIT for protected plant, 
unprotected plant, net operating loss, and unprotected non-plant balances over the respective 
ARAM lives of 47 years for KEDLI and 44 years for KEDNY.409  The Companies state that they 
believe the amortization should match the service life of KEDLI and KEDNY assets due to cash 
flow concerns and increased downward pressure on their credit ratings.  Furthermore, the 
Companies expressed concern that a future federal income tax rate increase could require the 
 
407 T. 2572. 
408 T. 1907, 2170. 
409 T. 2030, 2278. 
 


--- Page 95 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
91 
restoration of the accumulated deferred taxes from 21% to a higher tax rate.  In such a 
hypothetical, the effect would have to be collected from customers at that time.410 
 
 
In contrast, PULP proposed amortizing the entire unprotected excess ADIT in the 
Rate Year to offset the rate increase.  PULP further stated that many of the customers will likely 
not be alive for another 44-47 years to realize the benefit of the unprotected excess ADIT.411 
 
 
Mr. Wyman also offers a competing proposal to use the unprotected excess ADIT.  
Mr. Wyman expressed concern with the potential accumulation of stranded gas assets due to 
customers terminating gas service between now and 2050.  Following from this, Mr. Wyman 
proposed that the excess unprotected ADIT be used to reduce depreciation deficiencies which 
exist now and which, he asserts, will grow in the future.412 
 
 
Staff acknowledges that the Companies are required, under IRS normalization 
regulations, to return the protected excess ADIT utilizing the ARAM, which is 44 and 47 years 
respectively for KEDNY and KEDLI.  The IRS normalization regulations, however, do not apply 
to the amortization period of the unprotected plant and non-plant excess ADIT.  Staff 
recommends amortizing the unprotected plant and non-plant excess ADIT over 10 years as 
allowed under the IRS normalization rules.413 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation will increase the KEDNY unprotected excess ADIT 
amortization from $3.8 million to $16.5 million, an increase of $12.7 million.  The KEDNY 
other non-plant excess ADIT amortization of negative $0.6 million will decrease further to a 
negative $2.4 million, a change of $1.8 million.  The net effect increases the amortization by 
$10.9 million, thus decreasing the Rate Year revenue requirement by that amount.414 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation will increase the KEDLI unprotected excess ADIT 
amortization from $2.3 million to $10.7 million, an increase of $8.4 million.  The KEDLI other 
non-plant excess ADIT amortization of negative $2.0 million will decrease further to a negative

$5.0 million, a change of $3.0 million.  The net effect is to increase the amortization by $5.4 
million, thus decreasing the Rate Year revenue requirement by that amount.415 
 
 
The return of the net excess ADIT over a 10-year period will more rapidly reduce 
the revenue requirement and return the benefits to customers, as compared to the Companies’ 
proposal.  Staff’s recommended 10-year amortization charts a middle ground between the overly 
slow return of the unprotected excess ADIT the Companies desire, and the too fast return of it 
that PULP proposes.  While adopting PULP’s proposal to return the entire unprotected excess 
ADIT in the Rate Year would significantly decrease the Companies’ revenue requirements, it 
would result in significant rate shock when rates must increase in the following period to account 
for the exhaustion of these credits. 
 
 
The Companies’ desire to slowly return the excess unprotected ADIT because of 
possible future federal tax law changes should be rejected.  The Companies allusion to future 
Congressional action is speculative and merely hypothetical.  It is not reasonable to address this 
issue with an eye toward potential future tax law changes that may or may not occur.  The impact 
of cash flows and credit metrics will be addressed by the finance panel.  Mr. Wyman’s proposal 
to use excess ADIT balances to offset depreciation deficits is similarly speculative.  It makes 
assumptions about how the Commission may address the future of gas utilities, when those 
assumptions are not based on clear facts at this time.  These rate cases are not the place or time 
for implementing Mr. Wyman’s dramatic proposals without consideration of the future of gas 
utilities on a state-wide basis.  Furthermore, as Mr. Wyman does not identify which types of 
excess ADIT amounts he proposes to make use of, his proposal may result in an IRS 
normalization violation. 
 
 
The Staff Accounting Panel’s recommendation of a 10-year amortization period 
for the return of the excess ADIT, along with other qualitative and quantitative factors were 
considered in Staff’s credit metric and cash flow analysis for the Companies, as discussed below 
in Section VII.D, below.416  Based on the foregoing, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
recommendations.  Thus, the Commission should adopt a 10-year amortization period for the 
Companies’ unprotected excess ADIT.

VI. 
RATE BASE ISSUES 
A. 
Capital Projects and Expenditures  
1. 
Customer Connections 
 
 
The Companies’ Customer Connections budget is comprised of the installation of 
Main, Services, Meter/Regulators, customer contributions program, meter purchase program, 
AMR, gas system reinforcement, and other specific projects associated with customer 
connections.  The total customer connections forecasts shown in the Companies second 
supplemental filing for RY are approximately $80.612 million and $93.081 million for KEDNY 
and KEDLI, respectively.  Staff recommends a downward adjustment of $545 thousand and $9 
million for KEDNY and KEDLI’s customer connections budget in Rate Year, respectively.417   
 
 
Staff and the Companies forecasted the budget for each line item contained in the 
Customer Connections budget category excluding Customer Contribution & Reinforcement, by 
taking the product a particular unit cost multiplied by the number of forecast units.418  The 
Companies’ Customer Connections budget has varied significantly between their initial filing, 
and with each of their subsequent filings.  The Companies’ connections budgets have ranged 
between approximately $34.5 million and $80.6 million for KEDNY, and between $29.0 million 
and $113.8 million for KEDLI.  In its second supplemental testimony, the Companies updated 
the capital forecast for customer connections for the Install Main, Install Service, Meter 
Purchase, Install Meter/Regulator, and AMR budget line items to reflect new customers 
additions, agreed to in the November 2019 Order in Case 19-G-0678 and to remove costs 
associated with the Clean Choice program for KEDLI.419     
 
 
In general, the Companies’ total unit costs is based on a two-year average unit 
cost using FY 2017 and FY 2018 escalated by 2.5 percent per year.  An exception are contractor 
costs which a subcomponent of the unit cost which is escalated by 3.0 percent.420  To forecast the 
number of units, the Companies use a different methodology based on various factors that impact 
on the number of new customer connections.  The Companies consider 1) anticipated changes in

For KEDNY’s Install Main Commercial (Comm) program, we found the unit

installed in those years, as well as the cost to meet s new paving requirements.  Thus, we reduced

the lowest unit cost, $266 in 2018 for arborists services.

increased cost is attributable to the higher cost of large commercial and industrial meters 
purchased and a decrease in the total number of residential meters purchased.423

The Companies project a level of system reinforcement need to address gas

insulated from baseless expenditures which do not provide any reinforcement benefits.  Staff 
urges the Commission to reject KEDLI’s proposal to shift $5 million from FY 2022 to FY 2021 
for Phase 1 of the Southeast Suffolk Infrastructure project.

NYC’s Rate Year construction budget of $1.275 billion.  Additional incremental funding on top 
of the total budget is unnecessary.  Furthermore, KEDNY currently utilizes a reconciliation 
mechanism which protects KEDNY from excessive expenditures above and beyond its

its recommended rate allowance, Staff considered the actual expenditures in those years.   
KEDNY spent 11 percent, 14 percent, and 17 percent, and 23% of CNY’s construction budget in 
FY 2016, FY 2017, FY 2018, and FY 2019, respectively.

FY 2018 and FY 2019.  FY 2018 and FY 2019 fully incorporate the new paving regulation in

c. 
City State Construction (CSC) Reconciliation (CapEx and 
O&M Tracker) 
The Companies’ current City State Construction (CSC) reconciliation mechanism 
or tracker allows KEDNY and KEDLI to recover from customers 90 percent of any CSC costs in 
excess of their rate allowance.  The other 10 percent is paid by KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
shareholders.  KEDNY and KEDLI propose a 100 percent reconciliation of capital expenditures 
(CapEx) in the Rate Year.  KEDNY also proposes adding a new 100 percent Operation and 
Maintenance (O&M) mechanism for CSC expenses related to “support and protect” activities.443  
Staff rejects KEDNY and KEDLI’s proposal and instead recommends an 80 percent 20 percent 
CapEx only Tracker.444  Staff also rejects KEDNY’s proposed O&M Tracker.445  
 
 
While the work performed under the CSC program is contingent upon the 
Companies’ coordination with the City of New York (CNY), and municipalities on Long Island, 
KEDNY and KEDLI still manage the projects and contractors who ultimately complete the work 
associated with this program.  This management and oversight includes the full range of project 
management such as contract oversight, quality control, material procurement, workforce 
control, and productivity.  Accordingly, we believe it is important to provide the Companies a

KEDNY and KEDLI each propose an approximately $3.0 million budget in the

material procurement for two projects, and the development of two additional station projects in

6. 
Corrosion Program (KEDLI) 
The corrosion program monitors, tests, and maintains the corrosion control 
systems on above ground pipe as well as underground pipe. The Companies’ forecast for the 
Corrosion Control program is based on taking the HTY unit cost and applying it to a three-year 
historic average mileage, specifically Calendar Year (CY) 16 through CY 18, and then adjusts 
for inflation each year.480  Staff recommends a downward adjustment of $.073 million in the 
Rate Year.  While this adjustment was not discussed in our testimony, the adjustment was 
reflected in Staff’s exhibit.481  
Staff used a straight three-year average of historical expenditures, FY 2017 
through FY 2019 and then inflated the average to the Rate Year.  Using an average unit price 
applied to a total number of miles, as the Company does is not an accurate approach because the 
program consists of various work items with different unit costs, and the single unit cost from the 
HTY does not capture the mix of work to be performed in the Rate Year.  This program is 
dissimilar to the Leak Prone Pipe program where a certain mileage of work with similar unit cost 
is performed each year.  Using an average of actual expenditures, as Staff has, more closely 
reflects the trend of the program costs.  Therefore, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
proposed downward adjust of $.073 million for the Rate Year.

8. 
Reactive Main & Reactive Service Replacements 
The Reactive Main and Reactive Service Replacement programs provide for the 
replacement of gas mains and associated services during unanticipated urgent or emergency 
situations that fall outside the normal scope of the Companies’ operations.  This includes leaks 
that cannot be repaired, damages, service abandonments due to inactivity, demolition requests, 
customer driven relocation of services, and addressing other substandard conditions.  KEDNY 
and KEDLI stated that these budget forecasts are developed by taking a two-year average of 
historical costs and inflating the average for the Rate Year.484   
Staff agrees with the methodology used to develop these program budgets.  The 
nature of the work associated with these programs is reactive as the name implies.  Using 
historical expenditures as a basis to forecast funding levels is appropriate.  Staff’s recommended 
rate allowances for these programs are based on using the most recent two-year average of FY 
2018 and FY 2019 costs plus inflation.485  Using the most recent two years establishes a budget 
which more closely reflects recent costs for this program.   
In rebuttal, Companies stated that their response to information request DPS-761 
was incomplete.  In addition to averaging the two years, FY 17 and FY 18, plus inflation, the 
Companies’ forecast also reflects their proposal to capitalize segments of main under 50 Linear 
Feet (LF) that were previously expensed.486  While Staff does not refute this explanation, Staff 
asserts that this added cost should only affect the reactive main replacement program, not the

10. 
Buried Vent Line Program 
The Buried Vent Line Program is a public safety program implemented in 
response to a 2016 incident in Brooklyn, NY in which a corroded buried vent line led to water 
intrusion which then flowed into the regulator, froze, and locked the regulator in the open 
position, resulting in over-pressurization of the downstream piping.  Buried vent lines are 
susceptible to corrosion, and subsequent water intrusion, as they are underground and lack

13. 
Reliability: Blanket Slippage Adjustment 
The Reliability category budget funds programs and projects which maintain safe 
and reliable service on the system for all customers.  The Companies propose significant 
increases to a number of their annual programs including the Pressure Regulating Facilities 
Program, Heater Installation Program, Gas Planning/Remote Control Valves (RCV) Program, 
and System Automation Program.   
The Companies’ have a history of inaccurately scheduling projects, contractor 
resources, and ultimately underspending the total reliability budget.494  Staff recommends that 
the Companies' budgets be adjusted to align with the levels of historic spending and project 
completion based on a three-year average, FY 2017 through FY 2019.495  
Utilizing this three-year average, Staff calculated that KEDNY and KEDLI 
historically spent approximately 74 percent and 71 percent of their reliability budgets, 
respectively.  We then applied these percentages as a slippage adjustment to the Companies’ 
overall proposed Rate Year reliability budgets.496 As stated in Staff’s response to information 
request NG-45: 
It is Staff’s intent that the Companies can reallocate funding from projects 
and programs where the budgets have been underspent to projects and 
programs where the budgets have been overspent within the reliability 
category, as they have historically done.  Staff does not propose to restrict 
the Companies’ ability to allocate the approved reliability budget to projects 
and programs as needed.497 
Staff and the Companies agree, safety and reliability is critical, however, Staff’s 
adjustments do not restrict the Companies’ ability to allocate funding to projects which ensure 
the safety and reliably of the system.  In fact, the budget including Staff’s proposed adjustments 
represents a total increase in funding of 55 percent for KEDNY and 214 percent for KEDLI.  The 
Companies may assert that past spending trends are not indicative of future spending needs or 
trends, however, without demonstrating any changes that would lead to more accurate project

--- Page 115 ---

111 
estimation or the Companies’ ability to stay on schedule, Staff finds that historic trends are the 
best indicator of future performance in this category. 
The Companies’ historical spend shows that previous rate allowances approved 
for this category have not been fully expended.  Aligning the Companies’ budgets with actual 
expenditures will ensure the rate allowance for this category is reasonable, ultimately to the 
benefit of KEDNY and KEDLI’s customers.  The Commission should, therefore, adopt Staff’s 
adjustments and the resulting budgets for the Reliability Category. 
14. 
Storm Hardening – Remote Service Shutoff Valves 
 
 
The Companies’ propose to install Remote Service Shutoff Valves, flood sensors, 
and a fixed communication network within the FEMA defined 500-year floodplain zones across 
the Companies’ operating regions, with significant deployment planned for FY 21.498  
The City of New York (NYC) supports the program moving forward “provided 
the devices are comprehensively tested, and customers are adequately educated about them” and 
“the devices should be tested robustly and deployed gradually.”499  Additionally, NYC believes 
the data collected by the devices should be shared with them.  
Staff generally supports the costs and scope of this program; however, we are 
concerned with the Companies’ ability to move forward with this program on the schedule that 
they have proposed.  Staff recommends that the Companies defer the project funding for one 
year and have the valves tested by an independent laboratory.500  The Companies have 
demonstrated a history of not moving forward as scheduled with projects and programs within 
this category.501  The Storm Hardening Valve program is a salient example.  In the Companies’ 
last rate cases, Cases 16-G-0059 and 16-G-0058, they proposed this Storm Hardening Valve 
program, which Staff supported.  Yet, the Companies were unable to obtain a viable shutoff 
valve mechanism commercially available for use on the system, which caused them to delay the 
launch of the program.502  Despite finding a commercial manufacturer for the valves, Staff 
 
498   T. 4677.  
499  T. 5454.

501  T. 4672.

--- Page 116 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

remains concerned that the Companies cannot move forward with their proposed schedule 
without risking deployment of valves that have not undergone adequate testing.  The Companies 
response to several information requests as well as technical conferences Staff attended 
regarding the valves leads us to believe that the valves need more time for testing.503   
  
 
Since these valves and the supporting communication technology is very new it is 
extremely prudent to perform adequate testing by an independent third party laboratory to assess 
and redress any issues.  The Companies stated, together with the manufacture, they continue to 
perform testing of the valves. Yet, the Companies' large investment in a new technology which is 
also not used anywhere else in the Country should tested by a party without any interest in its 
deployment.  More importantly, the valves serve a key safety and reliability function, which is 
emphasized by their location and target use in flood prone areas.  The location and role of these 
valves heightens the need to ensure they function properly when needed.  
If these remote service shutoff valves are deployed and there is some issue that is 
not captured by the Companies’ or the manufacturers testing there may be safety and reliability 
consequences, and there will be a large cost of repair or replacement.  These impacts to safety 
and reliability as well as the cost would fall on customers. While performing third party testing is 
an additional cost it is de minimis compared to the potential cost of replacing, repairing or 
retrofitting an entire deployment of valves.  The Commission should, therefore, adopt Staff’s 
recommendation.

KEDNY and KEDLI propose additional O&M spending in the Rate Year for

conducted via External Corrosion Direct Assessment (ECDA) on pipelines that are not enabled 
for In-Line Inspections (ILI) and via ILI on pipelines which have been retrofitted for ILI. The 
O&M program costs also include excavation and support activities required for ECDA

Staff adjusted the program budget by removing the incremental spending 
proposed in the Rate Year for both KEDNY and KEDLI, resulting in a downward adjustment of 
$4.285 million to KEDNY’s incremental operating expenses and a downward adjustment of 
$2.134 million to KEDLI’s incremental operating expenses in the Rate Year.508  The Companies 
proposed work plan does not increase the number of miles to be inspected over the four years 
beyond the mileages in the current work plan.509  Therefore, the incremental funding proposed is 
not justified, and the existing OpEx budget is sufficient to support the needs of this program in 
the rate year.

18. 
Capitalization Policy Change 
The Companies current capitalization policy has a threshold of 50 Linear Feet 
(LF).  Any installation or replacement of segments of main beneath this threshold are not 
capitalized, they are expensed under the Companies’ O&M budget.  The Companies are 
proposing to remove the 50 LF threshold and capitalize these segments of main.  50 LF threshold 
is an arbitrary threshold for KEDNY and KEDLI and changing this policy would align the 
accounting treatment of these work activities among all of National Grid’s New York Gas 
Distribution Companies.518  Since there is no regulatory minimum threshold for capitalizing gas 
main installation, Staff does not oppose the Companies’ proposed change in policy.519  The 
Companies’ have also adjusted their expenses to reflect this change.

19. 
Contractor Costs – Damage Prevention 
The Companies claim they have recently completed contract renegotiations with 
certain contractors that have resulted in known and measurable increases to the damage 
prevention mark out costs for both Companies.520  The Companies explained that while they are 
now proposing an update, they forecasted damage prevention contractor costs in their original 
filings by taking the HTY cost and applied inflation.521   
Staff strongly opposes the Companies’ request to revise their contractor costs 
relating to damage prevention mark-outs as it is far too late in the process and puts Staff and the 
intervening parties in this case at a significant disadvantage in reviewing the effect of this change 
in cost.  Therefore, we recommend that the Commission deny the Companies’ request to revise 
its contractor costs associated with damage prevention mark outs.522   
The Companies have known that their contracts associated with their damage 
prevention program were expiring long before their April 2019 filing.523  In fact, the Companies 
have been aware for approximately two years.  Regardless of the Companies knowledge, they 
never identified this issue in these proceedings, at any time, prior to their Second Supplemental 
filing.   More importantly, no savings were reflected.524  Further, the Companies’ omission has 
prevented any opportunity for analysis as to whether other contracts would lower costs.525  
The Companies shouldn’t be allowed to update specific costs this late in the 
process.  The Companies withheld information that could have easily been included early on in 
the cases.  Moreover, there should be savings associated with the new contract and the 
Companies failed to consider that analysis.  Therefore, the Commission should reject the 
Companies’ requested update of these costs.

B. 
Net Plant True-up 
Staff proposes to continue the Net Plant Tracker. The Net Plant tracker is the 
downward-only capital expense tracker, that applies generally to all capital projects.526  The Net 
utility plant and depreciation mechanism, as described in the Order Adopting Terms of a Joint 
Proposal and Establishing Gas Rate Plans issued December 16, 2016, is a one-way reconciliation 
provision that provides for annual reconciliations of the average net utility plant and depreciation 
expense.527  The mechanism is meant to protect customers from paying rates that would be too 
high if the capital budget is underspent or there are significant delays in completely projects.528 
Staff recommends that if the Companies add plant at levels in excess of the levels 
forecast in base delivery rates that there is no rate adjustment upward to recognize that increase, 
but if the Companies add plant less than provided for in rates that customers will be credited with 
the associated revenue requirement impact of the shortfall as compared to forecast levels.529  
Staff recommends comparing the actual net revenue requirement in the rate year 
with the net plant requirement approved by the Commission, inclusive of depreciation expense, 
and adjusting downward-only.530  The mechanism should be downward adjusting only because 
customers are paying a return on a forecast level of rate base, and the one-way mechanism 
protects customers if the Companies significantly underspend the capital budget or projects have 
significant delays closing to plant in service.531  
Regardless of the length of the rate plan, the downward only mechanism is needed 
to protect rate payers from the Companies significantly under spending their capital budget or 
having significant delays that will result in a lower revenue requirement than the level approved 
by the Commission for which rates were set.  Therefore, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
recommendation continuing implementation of the Net Plant True-up mechanism.

D. 
Earnings Base Capitalization / Working Capital 
 
 
The Earnings Base / Capitalization (EB/CAP) adjustment aligns the utility rate 
base with its capitalization devoted to utility service.  The Commission permits utilities to earn a 
return only on the capital devoted to utility service.  This ensures that customers pay no more, 
and utilities recover no less, than the indicated return on the capital supporting utility 
operations.532  With regard to the EB/CAP differential, capitalization represents funds provided 
by investors and customers supporting utility operations, on which utilities pay a return.  Some 
examples of capitalization, as used here, include: common stock, retained earnings, advances 
from associated companies, and short-term debt.  Accordingly, these items create a cost that 
must be recovered through utility rates.  In addition, as capitalization supports all assets, it must 
be reduced for assets that are not part of utility operations.  Assets that are not part of utility 
operations, for which capitalization must be reduced, include assets such as temporary cash 
investments, non-utility property, internal reserves, and goodwill.533  In other words, for 
purposes of the EB/CAP adjustment, capitalization could include more items than the term 
would usually imply when used generally to describe the utility’s capital structure.  The causes 
of the EB/CAP differential vary at each utility but are generally variations in cash flow items not 
in rate base, such as non-interest bearing liabilities, or cost-free sources of funds, that support a 
utility’s assets resulting from the timing of payments on accounts payable, sales, and other taxes 
payable. 
 
 
Another cause of the EB/CAP differential may be imperfections in the estimation 
of cash working capital funding requirements.534  Utilities have access to cost-free sources of 
funds, due to the delay, from when certain costs are recovered from customers, to when the 
utility must actually pay a cost.  Specifically, this results with accounts such as taxes payable, 
and accounts payable.  However, utilities do not pay a return on, and have no capital cost 
associated with, accounts or taxes payable.  Also, utilities provide cost free capital or use of 
funds to customers.  For example, utilities do not receive a return on current customer accounts 
 
532 T. 2401. 
533 T. 2401. 
534 T. 2402. 
 


--- Page 123 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
119 
receivable for utility service.  The net amount of these short-term cost-free capital requirements 
is usually referred to as working capital, which is included in the utility’s rate base.535 
 
 
KEDNY made a number of adjustments to EB/CAP and working capital.  Below 
is a brief summary of Staff’s review, followed by a more lengthy discussion of each issue.  First, 
KEDNY computed a Historic Test Year EB/CAP adjustment that decreases rate base by $32.828 
million.536  Staff does not dispute this adjustment. 
 
 
KEDNY then identified two specific items within the EB/CAP calculation that it 
said had working capital ramifications in the Historic Test Year.  To address these two items, 
KEDNY made an adjustment to working capital, and an offsetting adjustment to EB/CAP, 
having no net effect on rate base.  One of these items related to gas supply, and the other to 
city/state construction (CSC).  Staff does not object to the adjustments related to the gas supply 
item, however, Staff recommends reversing the adjustments related to CSC. 
 
 
KEDNY then made two adjustments to Rate Year working capital, one related to 
gas supply and the other related to CSC.  For each of these two adjustments, KEDNY did not 
make corresponding adjustments in the EB/CAP calculation.  These adjustments have a net 
effect of increasing rate base.  Again, Staff does not object to the adjustment related to the gas 
supply item, however, Staff recommends reversing the adjustment related to CSC. 
 
 
Finally, Staff identified a regulatory asset item related to CSC included in both 
KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s rate base that Staff recommends removing.  As a corollary, Staff 
recommends making offsetting adjustments to increase KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s EB/CAP 
adjustments.

which KEDNY made an adjustment of $31.865 million to Rate Year working capital and an 
offsetting adjustment of $31.865 million to the EB/CAP adjustment to account for the change in 
the gas supply cash working capital allowance for the Historic Test Year.537  The second

adjustment was made to separately identify the City State Construction (CSC) accounts

Staff’s recommendations to reverse the Historic Test Year normalizing adjustment to working

computation.

2. 
Adjustments to Working Capital 
 
 
KEDNY made additional working capital adjustments to reflect the projected 
increase in working capital requirements between the end of the Historic Test Year through the 
end of the Rate Year for two specific items.542  First, KEDNY adjusted the forecasted Rate Year 
working capital for a change in gas supply purchases, and the difference between Historic Test 
Year commodity revenue and the projected Rate Year commodity revenue.  This adjustment 
decreases Rate Year working capital by $7.699 million.543  Second, KEDNY increased Rate Year 
working capital by $28.273 million for the forecasted change in the CSC accounts receivable 
balances from the end of the Historic Test Year through the Rate Year.544 
 
 
Staff agrees with the Rate Year working capital related to the gas supply and 
commodity revenue.  KEDNY has been allowed to use a lead-lag study to project the Rate Year 
working capital requirements associated with the timing between when it pays its suppliers for 
gas purchases and the time it receives payments for the purchases from its customers since the 
2016 Rate Order (SRRP, page 76).  However, Staff disagrees with the KEDNY’s proposed Rate 
Year adjustment for what it has identified as forecasted incremental accounts receivable balance 
for CSC work (SRRP, page 78). 
 
 
KEDNY explains it included the Rate Year adjustment due to the expectation that 
the CSC accounts receivable balance will increase materially year-over-year, given KEDNY’s 
projected increase in CSC work.  KEDNY indicated that, as the accounts receivable balance 
increases, its working capital requirements will also increase, due to the delay in payments from 
NYC, which KEDNY projects to be 21-months.545  Specifically, KEDNY states that invoices due 
at December 31, 2018 showed a 20-month lag; those due at January 31, 2019 showed a 19-month 
lag; and those due at May 31, 2019 showed a 23-month lag.546  KEDNY indicates this rate base 
adjustment is necessary because it asserts that it should receive a return on prudently invested 
capital expenditures that are in excess of the Historic Test Year EB/CAP adjustment.547

KEDNY’s attempt to separately address working capital for CSC is improper 
because:  (1) it results in an isolated lead-lag study that ignores potential countervailing items in 
working capital; (2) it is inconsistent with the Commission’s reliance on the Federal Energy 
Regulatory Commission (FERC) formula for working capital; (3) it amounts to an improper 
projected Rate Year adjustment to the historic EB/CAP adjustment calculation; and (4) KEDNY 
is at least partially at fault for the lag in CSC reimbursements. 
 
 
First, KEDNY’s lead-lag approach for this adjustment is arbitrary in nature.  As 
stated above, working capital reflects the net of hundreds of lead-lag items that both increase and 
decrease working capital requirements.548  KEDNY seeks to extricate one single item from this 
list, and ignores others that provide KEDNY with cost-free short term capital.  State and federal 
income taxes are a prime example.  Tax monies are collected monthly from customers but are 
paid quarterly to the taxing authorities.  KEDNY has cost-free use of the amounts paid by 
customers from the time the tax monies are collected from customers until the time KEDNY 
pays the Internal Revenue Service and the NYS Tax Department.  Reflecting this lead-lag 
income tax cash flow enhancement would impact Rate Year working capital requirements.549 
 
 
On rebuttal, KEDNY claims that removing its proposed CSC working capital 
adjustment, its shareholders would be required to bear the entire cost of financing $28.273 
million of CSC related expenditures that KEDNY.550  However, the countervailing nature of the 
items in working capital means that this is far from a clear conclusion.  In order to determine 
whether KEDNY’s shareholders were truly bearing the financing costs of CSC-related 
expenditures, one would have to assess each of the lead-lag items in working capital and 
calculate the net result.  Staff does not advocate making lead-lag adjustments, but notes that if 
lead-lag adjustments were to be made, they should only be reflected if a comprehensive lead-lag 
study is conducted on all items affecting KEDNY’s cash flow.  Such a comprehensive lead-lag 
adjustment would replace, rather than supplement, the cash working capital allowance provided 
by the FERC formula.551

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
123 
 
 
Second, the Commission has a long-standing standard practice of relying on the 
FERC formula to calculate the amount of working capital to be included in rate base.  The 
Commission adopted the FERC formula as a generic means and proxy of estimating working 
capital without the need for recourse to exhaustive lead-lag studies.552  The FERC Formula 
remains the Commission’s preferred approach.  Using both the FERC formula and the results of 
a study that is akin to a lead-lag study to develop Rate Year working capital requirements is a 
double-count, and the lead-lag adjustment must be eliminated for revenue requirement 
purposes.553 
 
 
There is one recent exception, in its rate order in Case 10-E-0050, the 
Commission allowed Niagara Mohawk to use a lead-lag study for only the limited purpose of 
determining the working capital related to electric commodity purchases so that Niagara 
Mohawk could establish the proper merchant function charge.  This is the same reason that Staff 
does not object to KEDNY’s adjustments to EB/CAP and working capital related to gas 
commodity purchases, identified above.554 
 
 
However, the Commission’s decision in Case 10-E-0050 provides a prime 
example of the adage that “the exception proves the rule.”  In providing this exception to Niagara 
Mohawk, the Commission explicitly stated it was not authorizing Niagara Mohawk to deviate 
from the standard practice that relies on the FERC formula to calculate the amount of working 
capital to be included in rate base, and explicitly stated that “[l]ead-lag studies will not be 
accepted or used for rate base purposes as long as the FERC formula continues to be an 
acceptable and preferred approach.”555 
 
 
On rebuttal, KEDNY asserts that the FERC working capital formula establishes a 
level of working capital associated only with a utility’s O&M expense.  The reimbursable CSC 
amounts are not part of KEDNY’s O&M expense and therefore the working capital associated 
with those amounts is not recovered through the application of the FERC formula.556  As a

VII. 
FINANCIAL ISSUES 
A. 
Return on Equity 
While Staff and the Companies apply similar DCF and CAPM financial models to 
estimate the Companies cost of equity, we differ many critical details.571  Our disagreements 
relate to the: 1) composition of appropriate proxy group, 2) appropriate growth rates in DCF 
analyses, 3) key inputs employed in the CAPM analyses, 4) the relative weighting of the CAPM 
and DCF results, and 5) which appropriate version of Microsoft internal rate of return Excel 
function in discounting dividend cash flow in the DCF model to use.     
Beyond the Companies’ initial proposals, they claim that “Staff has applied the 
cost of equity models in a purely mechanistic fashion based on Commission precedent without 
using informed judgment or considering current economic and market conditions, as is 
appropriate and as the Commission has demonstrated a willingness to do in prior cases”572  Ms. 
 
569 T. 2426 – 2428. 
570 T. 2444, 2452. 
571  The terms cost of equity and return on equity (ROE) are often used interchangeably, we apply the same 
approach here. 
572  T. 5059. 
 


--- Page 132 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
128 
Bulkley, the Companies’ witness, has made similar claims in Case 16-G-0257 involving NFGD’s 
rates.573  The Commission rejected Ms. Bulkley’s argument by stating: 
As the [Recommended Decision] noted, the Commission has repeatedly affirmed 
certain key elements of the methodology we use in determining the appropriate cost 
of equity to be included in rates.  Those elements consist of the application of 
Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) and Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) analyses 
to a representative proxy group of utility companies; the use of a two-stage DCF 
computation with inputs derived from Value Line; the basing of CAPM results on 
an average of the outcome from standard and zero-beta models with a risk-free rate 
based on Treasury bonds, market risk premium provided by Merrill Lynch’s 
Quantitative Profiles, and betas taken from Value Line; and our use of a 2/3 – 1/3 
weighting of the DCF and CAPM results, respectively. As in those cases where we 
have continued to affirm these principles, we see no convincing reason to deviate 
therefrom today.574 
Consistency in the application of regulatory principles and practices lends a 
degree of predictability to the rate setting process and thereby reduces regulatory risk.575  
Therefore, we urge the Commission maintain the application its well-established approach in 
establishing return on common equity.  
1. 
ROE Methodology 
The Companies’ developed three multi-stage DCF models utilizing the low, 
mean, and high earnings growth rate estimates published by Yahoo Finance, Zack’s, and Value 
Line.576  Ms. Bulkley’s DCF calculations resulted in a low, mean, and high ROE estimate for 
each company in her proxy group.  Ms. Bulkley utilized both the traditional form of the CAPM 
and the zero-beta CAPM applied to both Bloomberg and Value Line betas that resulted in two 
sets of 12 ROEs for both her Combined Utility Proxy Group and Natural Gas Proxy Group.  Ms. 
Bulkley then concluded that an appropriate ROE for the Companies ranges between 9.65 percent 
and 10.55 percent.  In this proceeding, the Companies are seeking a 9.65 percent ROE for the 
rate year.577  Consistent with the Commission’s well established practice, Staff applied the 
standard weightings of two-thirds to its two-stage DCF results and one-third to its CAPM results,

2. 
Proxy Group 
The use of a proxy group analysis is a widely accepted standard, which has been 
consistently applied by the Commission to estimate the cost of equity for a utility.  Estimation of 
the cost of equity based on a proxy group is necessary because analysts’ estimates regarding an 
individual company are, by their nature, inaccurate and sometimes biased.579  In general, a proxy 
group analysis begins with the establishment of a set of selection criteria to construct a 
sufficiently large group of companies whose risk characteristics align as close as possible to the 
specific utility or utilities being analyzed.  By employing a proxy group with a sufficiently large 
number of similarly situated companies, the effects of inaccurate estimates may be reduced.580   
Once the proxy group is constructed, the average cost of equity for the group can 
be estimated.  The difference between Staff and the Companies’ cost of equity initially stems 
from the differences in each other’s approach in determining the proxy group for KEDNY and 
KEDLI.   
Staff disagrees with the Companies on the nature of the proxy group, the selection 
criteria used, and the adequate number of companies to include in the proxy group.  As discussed 
below, Staff recommends that its proxy group and Staff’s analysis resulting in an 8.2 percent 
ROE be adopted.  
a. 
Companies’ Proxy Groups 
The Companies’ developed two proxy groups, the first referred to as the Natural 
Gas Proxy Group, and the other referred to as the Combined Utility Proxy Group.  The Natural 
Gas Proxy Group consists solely of seven gas distribution companies.  The Combined Utility 
Proxy Group consists of the seven gas companies included in the Natural Gas Proxy Group and 
13 additional electric distribution companies.581   For the Combined Proxy Group, Ms. Bulkley’s

The six criteria consists of the following: 1) investment-grade credit ratings 2)

growth projections, 3) at least 70 percent of the candidate’s total operating income from 
regulated operations, 5) generation comprising less than 60 percent of the candidate’s MWh

To be included in the Natural Gas Proxy Group, Ms. Bulkley required one 
additional criterion, specifically, 50 percent of the candidate’s total operating income must be 
from regulated natural gas operation.  Ms. Bulkley applied the set of seven selection criteria to a 
universe of 10 companies classified as natural gas distribution companies by Value Line to 
determine the set of companies included the Natural Gas Proxy Group.583  Staff raised concerns 
regarding the nature of both Ms. Bulkley’s proxy groups, specifically, the selection size and use 
of the Natural Gas Proxy Group, the selection criteria relating to net operating income, analyst 
coverage, and generation MWH sales.584  
Staff contests Ms. Bulkley’s use of the Natural Gas Proxy Group as it is 
inappropriate.  The use of the Natural Gas Proxy group is inappropriate because the group 
consists of only seven companies which does not constitute an adequate sample size to be 
statistically reliable and is therefore too small to produce reliable results in estimating the cost of 
equity.  Staff believes that any proxy group should contain an adequate sample size of companies 
to produce a statistically accurate and reliable measurement.  A suitable sample size ranges 
between 20-30 companies, for instance, Staff’s proxy group has 28 companies.585   
Moreover, National Grid Plc, the ultimate parent company through which equity 
investors can indirectly invest in KEDNY and KEDLI, is viewed principally as an electric 
holding company by investors.586  Given that Value Line classifies electric and electric 
combination utility holding companies as “electric”, Staff believes that if National Grid Plc. were

b.

Staff constructed a proxy group of 28 investment grade rated electric and electric 
combination utility holding companies selected from a universe of 37 holding companies deemed 
by Value Line Investment Survey (Value Line) to be “electric utilities.”603 
Staff’s first and second criteria in establishing its proxy group requires that 
selected companies be tracked by Value Line and that those companies have investment grade

Staff began construction of its proxy group by identifying 37 holding companies 
deemed by Value Line Investment Survey (Value Line) to be “electric utilities” because electric 
and gas holding companies operating risk characteristics are fundamentally similar   Beginning

forecasts needed for calculating a company’s cost of equity.  As for the remaining 35 companies, 
we eliminated five companies whose regulated revenues constitute less than 70 percent of total 
revenue.  Regarding the remaining 30 companies, we eliminated Fortis Inc. because it is a 
Canadian-based company, which, although traded on both the Toronto and New York stock 
exchanges, has its financial reports and Value Line projections based on Canadian currency 
which would introduce the possibility that exchange rate variations could affect the DCF 
calculation.  Finally, we eliminated El Paso Electric because of a pending merger and acquisition 
transaction.  Therefore, we arrived at a proxy group comprised of 28 electric utility holding 
companies which met all our selection criteria.605

respectively.  Staff’s numerical scores for S&P rankings for business and financial risk profiles, 
also indicated that the proxy group’s S&P business risk ranked at 1.32, which is close to an 
“Excellent” rating and its S&P financial risk ranked at 3.9, which is close to a “Significant” 
rating.606  The proxy group’s resultant business risk and financial risk profiles, however, remain 
supportive of its average credit ratings at “BBB+/Baal.”  Finally, on average, approximately 7 
percent of the proxy group’s revenues are from non-regulated businesses.

In response to Staff’s proposal, the Companies argued two points to contest

Companies’ proxy groups, that the operating risks of a pure-play local distribution company and 
electric transmission & distribution (T&D) companies in New York are fundamentally very 
similar because both are regulated public utilities, subject to nearly identical ratemaking 
treatment.   
In addition, both electric and combination gas and electric utilities invest 
primarily in capital-intensive physical networks that connect the customer to the source of 
supply; and sell their products and services at regulated rates to customers.610  Therefore, the 
Companies fail to explain why Staff’s proxy group is dissimilar to KEDNY and KEDLI.  For 
these reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s proxy group and subsequent results based 
upon the proxy group because our selection criteria are based on Commission precedent and the 
overall ROE calculation logically follow from Staff’s analysis of its proxy group.
