3. 
Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) 
a. 
Companies’ DCF Methodologies 
The Companies use a multi-stage DCF analysis based on a three-month average 
stock price and a range of near-term growth rate assumptions that produces a mean ROE of 9.20 
percent for the Combined Utility Proxy Group and 9.04 percent for the Natural Gas Proxy 
Group.  Specifically, the Companies use a three-stage DCF analysis that relies on equity 
analysts’ earnings growth forecasts published by Value Line, Zacks, and Yahoo Finance.611    
We have several concerns with the Companies’ DCF approach.  Foremost among 
them, we find the third stage growth rate to be excessive and believe Ms. Bulkley applies an 
inappropriate internal rate of return (IRR) tool for calculating the ROE estimate.  Our primary 
concern is the use of an excessive growth rate in the long-run or third-stage growth rate, and to a 
lesser extent, some of the excessive growth rates used in the first stage of her analysis.  Ms. 
Bulkley’s use of an excessive third stage growth rate is the principal reason that the 9.20 percent 
DCF-based ROE recommendation is flawed.612   
Ms. Bulkley DCF model uses earnings growth rates to determine short-term 
dividend growth rates, ignoring available dividend growth rates or forecasts.613  This is in direct 
contrast to the basic premise of the DCF methodology which measures the present value of 
future dividends.  Utilizing earnings growth assumes that dividend growth will match earnings 
growth, and her testimony failed to present evidence to support such an expectation.614  Ms. 
Bulkley’s use of a historically-derived Gross Domestic Product (GDP) rate, in conjunction with a 
forecasted inflation rate, as the long-term growth rate of her proxy group is flawed.615  A 
historical average of actual or real GDP growth may not be reflective of the growth prospects of 
the proxy group holding companies.  The use of a historical GDP growth rate along with 
expected inflation as a long-term future growth rate for utility stocks also ignores changes that 
might be occurring with each proxy group members’ common stock.616  Simply put, we do not

consider her use of the historically-derived actual or real GDP as the long-term growth rate to be 
appropriate because historical growth rates measure time periods that encompass far different 
circumstances relative to current economic conditions.   
In contrast, Staff’s use of a 4.64 percent long-term growth rate is closer to the 
forward-looking GDP estimate by the Blue Chip Economic Indicators (BCEI) publication.  The 
BCEI publication reflects the forward-looking consensus estimate of nearly 50 of the financial 
community’s prominent economists.617  Thus, Staff’s average long-term growth rate of 4.64 
percent is a more comparable estimate to the 4.0 percent Blue Chip Economic Indicators nominal 
GDP growth rate, while Ms. Bulkley’s 5.56 percent long-term growth rate is approximately 40 
percent higher than the BCEI forecasted nominal GDP growth rate.   
The excessiveness of her estimate of long-term GDP growth, specifically, the use 
of the historically-derived real GDP as the long-term growth rate is inappropriate because 
historical growth rates are necessarily measured over a subjective time period, and in this 
instance, one that incorporates far different circumstances than exist today. Historical average 
growth rates by their very nature completely ignore economic conditions today.618   
Another area of Staff’s concern relates to Ms. Bulkley’s use of the extended 
version of the Internal Rate of Return (XIRR) in her DCF analyses versus the Commission’s 
preferred use of the regular IRR as used in our model.  Her use of the XIRR overstates her mean 
DCF result of 9.20 percent for her Combined Utility Proxy group by approximately 15 bps, we 
well as the mean DCF result of 9.04 percent for her Natural Gas Proxy Group by approximately 
14 bps.619   
In her rebuttal, Ms. Bulkley made three arguments to justify her use of the XIRR.  
First, she argues that Staff’s DCF analysis inappropriately relies on the regular version of Excel’s 
internal rate of return (IRR) formula that “cannot take into account the timing of quarterly 
dividend payments when applied to annualized calculations.”620  Second, she argued that “[i]f 
Staff were correct that the IRR function adequately reflected the timing of cash flows available

Staff believes that Ms. Bulkley’s Excel worksheet mismatched her DCF dividend

of all the companies in her proxy group Ms. Bulkley’s estimated annualized dividend per share

Staff believes that the correct date for the timing of the $2.46 dividend payment 
should have been February 28th, 2019 not August 31st.  Were Ms. Bulkley to properly account for 
the timing of the dividend cash flows, her DCF results would have produced results consistent 
with the results of the regular IRR.   
On cross-examination, the Companies attempted to justify their use of the XIRR 
by asking the Staff Finance Panel that if a rational investor is given the option of being paid $100 
lump-sum dividend once per year and another option of quarterly dividends of $25 which on an 
annualized basis is equal to the $100, which of these two options will a rational investor 
choose.624  As indicated in our response, a rational investor would be indifferent because a 
company which pays dividends on quarterly basis has less use of the earnings to create growth, 
thereby reducing the company’s sustainable growth that in turn results in reduced investor’s 
required return.625  Moreover, as explained during the Evidentiary Hearing, this is not the first 
time that Companies’ ROE witnesses have argued that Staff annual dividend DCF model ignores

calculation and adopt Staff’s DCF methodology. 
b. 
Staff’s DCF Methodologies 
Staff’s DCF model which has its roots in the Recommended Decision (RD) of the 
Generic Finance Proceeding and Commission precedent, which adopts a two-stage model, 
explicitly recognizing that short-term growth expectations do not necessarily equal long-term 
growth expectations.628  The first stage of Staff’s model uses Value Line near-term dividend 
estimates to derive a short-term dividend growth rate, while the second stage is based on a 
calculation of a sustainable growth rate, derived from both internal and external growth rates 
using Value Line three-to-five year estimates of earnings per share, dividend per share, book 
value per share, and amount of common stock outstanding for each of the proxy group 
companies. 629   This data is used to estimate the dividends that can be expected for each 
company in the future, then the discount rate (return) required to turn the stream of expected

dividend payments into the current stock price is calculated.630  Given the prominence of the 
DCF methodology in cost of equity determinations in general, the most critical element is the 
assumed dividend growth rate for the long term.   
Staff presents the same DCF analysis the Commission has accepted in all fully 
litigated rate proceedings over the past 20 years, predicated on the fundamentally-sound and 
internally consistent concept of sustainable growth that has been favored by the Commission for 
decades.   Staff’s two-stage DCF methodology yields a cost of equity estimate of 8.22 percent.631  
The Companies argue that Staff’s DCF model has shortcomings.  The Companies 
claim that Staff’s sustainable growth rate is short-term in nature and that the sustainable growth 
rate is circular.  The Companies contention that Staff’s sustainable growth rate is short-term in 
nature is misguided.  Staff’s sustainable growth rate projects sustainable growth for 195 years.632   
Moreover, consistent with the Commission’s practice of ascertaining the 
reasonableness of the sustainable growth rate, Staff compared its 4.64 percent sustainable growth 
rate with the 4.0 percent long-range consensus growth rate in nominal GDP.633  While Staff’s 
sustainable growth rate is somewhat optimistic, it is far closer to the nominal GDP than the 5.56 
percent long-term growth rate estimated by the Companies.   
In their rebuttal, the Companies also argued that Staff’s sustainable growth rate is 
circular because it relies on Value Line’s 2023 forecasted ROE to determine Staff’s long form 
DCF results.  To support her argument, Ms. Bulkley stated that Staff’s average 11.14 percent 
expected ROE for 2023 is inconsistent with its 8.22 percent DCF average result.   
Staff rejects Ms. Bulkley’s argument because the retention growth component of 
Staff’s sustainable growth calculation relies on a prediction of expected future earned rates of 
return on common equity for a proxy group composed of mainly holding companies owning both 
regulated and unregulated business and therefore it is expected that the holding companies in the 
proxy group will gain additional earnings from these unregulated operations.  Moreover, given 
that the market-to-book ratio of Staff’s proxy group is greater than one, it is expected that the 
expected return on common equity will be greater than the allowed return on common equity.

Furthermore, the issue of rate of return on common equity circularity has been raised by some

rejected companies’ argument.  For instance, in Case 08-E-0539: 
We remain unconvinced by the Company’s basic contention that, endorsed by the 
judges that it is unreasonably circular to assume a cost of equity to estimate 
retention growth to estimate the cost of equity…. forecast of future earnings used 
to project growth will consider the return that investors expect regulators to allow, 
those earnings forecasts will also reflect investor expectations about a wide variety 
of other factors unrelated to the cost of common equity.634  
Staff’s DCF methodology is consistent with the Commission’s precedent and the Companies 
arguments against the Staff’s DCF approach have repeatedly been rejected by the Commission, 
therefore, we urge the Commission to reject the Companies’ contention and adopt Staff’s 
methodology and analysis. 
4. 
Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) 
a. 
Companies’ CAPM Methodologies 
Ms. Bulkley’s CAPM results range between 9.96 percent to 11.55 percent with a 
mean of 10.87 percent.  Ms. Bulkley provided a total of 24 CAPM-derived ROE estimates for 
her Combined Utility and Natural Gas proxy groups using the same CAPM methodologies that 
we employ.  Ms. Bulkley calculated 12 ROEs using the traditional CAPM methodology and 12 
ROEs using the zero-beta CAPM methodology, using two different beta sources in combination 
with three different risk-free rate estimates for each version of the CAPM.635  We have three 
principal concerns regarding Ms. Bulkley’s CAPM analysis: 1) relating to her derivation of a 
13.77 percent market return, 2) her beta determinations, and 3) her risk-free rate.  Collectively, 
the impact of these infirmities is an overstatement in her cost of equity estimate of approximately 
200 bps.636  Ms. Bulkley employed a 13.77 percent long-term growth rate to estimate the market 
return in her CAPM.  The primary flaw contained in the market return estimate is that the 
derivation relies entirely upon a constant growth DCF analysis of the S&P 500. 
 
634  2009 Con Edison Rate Order, p. 126. 
635  T. 3186. 
636  T. 3188.   
 


--- Page 147 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
143 
As Staff acknowledged, Ms. Bulkley cited the FERC approach to support some of 
her positions, however, she could have equally considered the FERC’s proportionate weighting 
of short-term and long-term growth rate to check the reasonableness of her 13.77 percent derived 
market return.  Ms. Bulkley’s market return should be 11.37 percent, which is 240 basis points 
lower.637  Missing anywhere in the Companies’ analysis is such verification. 
Staff also contests, the Companies’ risk-free rate as it does not adequately reflect 
investors’ investment horizon.  Ms. Bulkley calculates the risk-free rate using a three-month 
average of the 30-year Treasury yields for a recent three-month period at 3.05 percent, based on 
the average of December 2018 to February 2019 yields, a near-term projected 30-year Treasury 
yield for the five forward-looking quarters Q2-2019 through Q2-2020) at 3.28 percent, and the 
long-term projected 30-year Treasury yield for the period 2020-2024 at 3.90 percent.638  The 
expectation that all utility equity investors have a 30-year investment time horizon is 
inappropriate because it fails to recognize that investors have both intermediate and long-term 
horizons, thus the Companies’ claims are not aligned with the Commission’s preference to 
preferred average of 10-year and 30-year Treasury yields.  Moreover, one needs to look no 
further than the Companies debt portfolios consisting of only 10-year and 30-year long-term debt 
obligations supporting the Companies own rate bases to understand that investors’ have different 
time horizons.639  Therefore, Staff believes that averaging the 10-year and 30-year current 
Treasury yields is appropriate and consistent with the Commission’s approach. 
b. 
Staff’s CAPM Methodologies 
Staff employed both the “traditional” and “zero-beta” CAPM methods, albeit with 
different inputs than the Companies.  Staff’s resulting risk-free rate is the average of 10-year and 
30-year Treasury bond yields.  For the three months ended June 30, 2019, the average rate was 
2.55 percent.640  Staff utilized only Value Line betas because of their superiority which is based 
upon a reasonable estimation period of five years as compared to the 10-year estimation period 
 
637  T. 3186 – 3287.   
638  T. 3186 - 3287.   
639  T. 3195.   
640  T. 3145.

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
144 
used by Ms. Bulkley for Bloomberg’s betas.641  While a 10-year estimation period contains more 
data points, Staff believes that a beta estimated over a longer estimation period is more likely 
biased.  A 10-year estimation contains more, possibly irrelevant historical observations including 
results related to dissimilar circumstances.  This may include events related to corporate 
restructuring or industry transformation which may not represent the level of earnings volatility 
expected in the future.642  Thus, a shorter estimation period is more reasonable.  
The average Value Line beta of Staff proxy group is 0.61.643  Staff used the 
market return estimate provided by Merrill Lynch as published in its monthly Quantitative 
Profiles, which is reliable, publicly available, and also a highly respected source.644  The Merrill 
Lynch report uses a multi-stage dividend discount model (“Implied market return”) and a CAPM 
(“Required market return”) to calculate an expected return for the S&P 500 each month.  Staff 
stated during the evidentiary hearing, using a multi-stage DCF approach to determine the market 
return is superior to the single-stage DCF-derived market return utilized by Ms. Bulkley.645  The 
risk-free rate is then subtracted from the market return estimate to arrive at the market risk 
premium (MRP).  The three-month average market return estimate as of June 2019 is 10.88 
percent.  Subtracting our 2.55 percent risk-free rate results in a MRP of 8.33 percent.646  This 
method of calculating a MRP has been used by the Commission in several litigated rate cases, 
most recently in Case 16-G-0257 involving NFGD.647  Using the inputs described, Staff 
calculated a traditional CAPM of 7.63 percent and a zero-beta of 8.44 percent.  The average of 
the traditional CAPM and the zero-beta CAPM results is 8.04 percent.648  
We recommend the adoption of Staff’s CAPM methodology when determining 
the appropriate ROE to use in the calculation of the overall cost of capital for the Companies.  As 
discussed earlier, Staff’s inputs for the MRP, the risk-free rate, and beta source are reasonable.

Additionally, Staff’s methodology has been upheld by the Commission most recently in Case 16-

[T]he Commission has repeatedly affirmed certain key elements of the 
methodology we use in determining the appropriate cost of equity to be included in 
rates.  Those elements consist of the application of Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) 
and Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) analyses to a representative proxy group 
of utility companies; the use of a two-stage DCF computation with inputs derived 
from Value Line; the basing of CAPM results on an average of the outcome from 
standard and zero-beta models with a risk-free rate based on Treasury bonds, 
market risk premium provided by Merrill Lynch’s Quantitative Profiles, and betas 
taken from Value Line;…” 
We believe the Commission should continue its long-held policy of adopting Staff’s 
methodology. 
c. 
DCF/CAPM Weighting 
Staff recommends that the Commission adopt its relative weighting of the CAPM 
and DCF as it is the appropriate weighting of the CAPM and the DCF results.  The Companies 
argue that “…it is reasonable to apply equal weighting to the DCF and CAPM methods when 
determining the ROE for the Companies” because: 1) of the effect of the Federal Reserve 
monetary policies on market conditions and its corresponding impact on utility stock valuations, 
2) other utility regulatory bodies’ recent response to the alleged impact of current capital market 
conditions on the DCF model, and 3) of market expectations of higher interest rates.  Ms. 
Bulkley concludes that the DCF method is producing ROE results that are significantly lower 
than the results derived from her CAPM calculations, and therefore, it is reasonable to place less 
reliance on the DCF results.650   
Staff has demonstrated that utility stock valuations in terms of market-to-book 
ratios (MBRs) and Price-to-Earnings ratios (P/E ratios) over the past nine years, are generally 
valued lower than they were in the three-year period preceding the 2008 financial crisis.  The 
significance of that timeframe is that it was a time period well before the Federal Reserve had 
begun its Quantitative Easing, and yet the market experienced MBRs even higher than they are 
 
649  2017 NFGD Rate Order, p. 53. 
650  T. 3196. 
 


--- Page 150 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
146 
today.  Therefore, Ms. Bulkley’s contention that utility stock prices are extraordinarily high 
specifically because of the Federal Reserve’s monetary policies is misguided.651   
Ms. Bulkley’s second point that other utility regulatory bodies like the Illinois 
Commerce Commission, the Pennsylvania Public Utility Commission, and the Missouri Public 
Service Commission have all recognized the understatement of the DCF is misleading and 
further demonstrates the selection bias shown throughout her analyses.652  Further, the opinion of 
another regulatory bodies should not trump the Commission’s own decision-making in setting 
rates for New York State utilities.  Staff is not compelled by the Companies’ reliance on a biased 
argument to abandon its approach.  Similarly, we do not recommend, nor should the Commission 
be persuaded to deviate from its long-standing approach.653   
Concerning Ms. Bulkley’s third point that interest rates are expected to increase, 
this assumption is simply not supported by facts.  We find no indication that the Federal Reserve 
is likely to increase interest rates in the near term as claimed by Ms. Bulkley.  In fact, the Federal 
Reserve cut interest rate on July 31, 2019, by 25 basis points, for the first time since December 
2008.  In total, the Federal Reserve cut interest rates three times in 2019.  It is unlikely based on 
the current economy interest rates will increase during the Rate Year. 
Regarding Ms. Bulkley’s contention that the DCF and the CAPM should be 
accorded equal weight because the DCF method is currently producing ROE results that are 
significantly lower than the results of the CAPM, Staff believes the argument is not persuasive 
because Staff has demonstrated that Ms. Bulkley’s DCF results are not understated.  Yet, Ms. 
Bulkley employs a methodology that yields such an egregiously high market return in her CAPM 
analysis, it is not surprising that her DCF analysis yields a lower ROE determination.  Such a 
finding would not be the case if Ms. Bulkley’s CAPM analysis employed reasonable 
assumptions such as included in Staff’s analysis.  Under Staff’s cost of equity model, the 8.04 
percent CAPM result is currently lower than the 8.22 percent DCF result.654

Staff also cited three cases in which other utility company witnesses raised 
concerns about the weighting accorded the DCF and argued for equal weighting of the DCF and 
CAPM when the market-to-book ratio exceeds one or when the DCF was producing lower 
results than other methodologies the witnesses were advocating.  In all the cases that we 
reviewed, the Commission repeatedly affirmed its preference for the two-thirds and one-third 
weighting of the DCF and CAPM.655 
Most importantly, the Commission has affirmed the proportionate weighting of 
the DCF and the CAPM when the utility stock valuations and dividend yields were similar to 
current levels.  In Case 16-G-0257, the Commission summarily rejected equal weighting of the 
CAPM and DCF results, instead accepting Staff’s position.  The Commission stated: 
NFGD argues …that the DCF should be weighted no more than the CAPM when 
determining an appropriate return on equity.  We have already affirmed our 
continued 1/3 CAPM and 2/3 DCF weighting to arrive at our ROE results, based 
on the fact that we view the DCF methodology as superior to the CAPM. We have 
explained this preference repeatedly in past orders, and we see no need to deviate 
from that viewpoint at this time.656 
Therefore, we believe that Ms. Bulkley has not presented any new evidence that 
would compel the Commission to reject its long-standing policy of a two-third and one-third 
weighting of the DCF and CAPM.  The Commission should reject Ms. Bulkley’s approach as it 
has done so numerous times before. 
5. 
ROE Recommendation 
a. 
Companies’ ROE Request 
In this proceeding, the Companies are seeking a 9.65 percent ROE for the rate 
year.  The Companies’ ROE request of 9.65 percent was selected from the low end of a range of 
twelve cost of equity estimates that were generated from DCF and CAPM methodologies 
utilizing a natural gas proxy group and a combined utility proxy group.657   
Staff has already pointed out the principal flaws in Ms. Bulkley’s methodologies, 
specifically, the inappropriate market risk premium used in her CAPM approach, the inadequate 
 
655  T. 3201 - 3202. 
656  2017 NFGD Order, p.56 
657  T. 3157. 


--- Page 152 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
148 
sample size of the Natural Gas Proxy Group, the selection criteria relating to net operating 
income, analyst coverage, and generation MWH sales, the inappropriate use of the extended 
version of the Internal Rate of Return (XIRR) in her DCF analyses, the use of an excessive 
growth rate in her long run growth rate, as well as the excessive growth rates she uses in the first 
stage of her DCF analysis.   
As Staff explained, the combined effect of correcting the flaws in Ms. Bulkley’s 
DCF and CAPM approach including the inappropriate use of the extended version of IRR, rely 
on only the Value Line betas, and weight her DCF and CAPM results at two-thirds and one third, 
respectively necessitates a reduction of 131 basis points from 9.71 percent mean results to 
approximately 8.40 percent.658   
Staff also notes that Ms. Bulkley did not account for the various risk-reducing 
mechanisms and additional earnings potential associated with the Companies’ proposed Earnings 
Adjustment Mechanisms (EAMs) in her 9.65 percent ROE recommendation.659  Absent inclusion 
of such mechanisms in an analysis, it is very likely that Companies’ results overestimate the cost 
of equity for the Companies.660 
b. 
Staff’s ROE Results 
Staff recommends an 8.2 percent ROE based on a two-thirds weighting of our 
DCF result and a one-third weighting of our CAPM result.  While Staff’s recommended ROE of 
8.2 percent is below the national average return on equity authorizations, it is important to 
consider that, as described by Ms. Bulkley, the authorized ROE should afford the Companies an 
opportunity to earn an ROE commensurate with the return on equity for investments in 
enterprises having comparable risks.661  To that end, Staff asserts that its methodology, which 
relies on a sound, time-tested financial analysis of a proxy group of electric utility holding 
companies whose risks are comparable to the Companies, produces just such a result.   
 
658  T. 3203.

First, Staff’s recommended ROE is reasonable in light of the current interest rate 
environment.662  Second, it is consistent with investor’s expectations of ROEs in New York.663 
Third, it is sufficient to balance the interest of both ratepayers and Companies’ investors interest 
by having the lowest cost of capital for an acceptable level of risk without sacrificing service 
quality.664  Finally, fourth, it provides the Companies the ability to access the credit market at 
reasonably priced interest rates.665  These four points will be discussed in turn.  
Concerning our first point, as of the time of our analysis and throughout the 
period including the Evidentiary Hearing Staff believes that current interest rates are extremely 
low by historical standards.  By comparison, the interest rate environment when the Commission 
approved the Companies’ current rate plans’ 9.0 percent ROE in December 2016 was modestly 
higher.666  At that time, “A-”/“A3” and “BBB”/“Baa1” rated public utility debt obligations were 
yielded approximately 4.45 percent and 4.62 percent, respectively.  Currently, as of January 
2020, the respective average yields on “A-”/“A3” and “BBB”/“Baa1” rated public utility debt 
instruments are yielding 3.39 percent and 3.50 percent, respectively.  These rates are 
approximately 105 and 112 basis points lower.  Further, utility stocks tend to perform better in a 
low interest rate environment, and these realities are captured in Staff’s DCF methodology which 
incorporates current stock market data.667   
With respect to investor ROE expectations, it is reasonable to assume that equity 
investors derive their expectations for utility stocks informed by the approach used by each state 
commission to determine allowed ROEs for utilities operating in that particular regulatory 
jurisdiction.  As Staff clearly states rational investors would not expect the Commission to 
authorize an ROE approximating the Companies’ 9.65 percent requested ROE.668  Rather, 
rational investors, well aware of the Commission’s preference for a formulaic approach to 
determine the cost of common equity and its preference for establishing rates over a multi-year 
 
662  T. 3226.

664  T. 3115.

period, would expect an ROE below the Companies’ current authorized 9.0 percent ROE.  
Reasonable investors would expect this lower ROE for a multi-year rate plan and would expect 
an ROE approximating 8 percent for a one year of Rates.  This expectation is further evidenced 
by Moody’s Credit Opinion December 30, 2019 reports, concerning KEDNY and KEDLI. In the 
report, Moody’s states, “[g]iven the relatively mechanistic, and well-established, approach 
followed by the NYPSC to setting RoE, we expect KEDNY (and KEDLI)'s base RoE to be 
below 9 [percent].”669 
In addition, Commission’s policy for utility regulation incorporates many critical 
elements that bolster a utility’s ability to achieve its authorized return on equity.  For instance, 
Commission relies upon a fully forecasted test year to determine revenue requirement, which 
recognizes changes in revenues and expenses expected through the end of the rate year.  The 
Commission also generally allows for a high level of expense reconciliation for such material 
cost items such as environmental remediation costs and pension and OPEB expenses.  The 
Commission has implemented a purchase power adjustment clause that allows full and timely 
recovery of this large and volatile cost element.  Many other jurisdictions do not incorporate 
these mechanisms into their utility rate plans.  For overall blanket-type protections, New York 
State, unlike most other states, allows utilities to request deferral accounting for material items 
actually incurred but not provided for in the base forecasts.  In addition, New York State allows 
utilities to revise their rate filings during the suspension period for known and material 
changes.670  
It is also important to note that in light of curtailing of natural gas expansion in 
the Companies’ service territories, the Companies’ request to switch to a revenue per class RDM 
would offer the Companies greater protection from a decline in sales.671  In addition, New York 
utilities are no longer exposed to the relatively higher risks associated with generation.672  There 
are, therefore, ample protections in place to support a more modest return to meet the 
expectations of New York utility investors.

6. 
Impact of COVID-19 and Oil Price Decreases on Cost of Capital 
The Commission consistently updates financial inputs, such as the cost of equity, 
to reflect the most recently available data in litigated cases.  For example, in Case 16-G-0257, 
the Commission adopted an ROE based on financial data refreshed just prior to their decision to

reflect investor’s latest expectations and likely interest rate changes.681  The Commission 
performed similar updates in Case 08-E-0539 and Case 10-E-0362.682  In this case, Staff offers 
that the Commission may choose to update, using Staff’s methodology, various inputs to inform 
its decision on Return on Equity (ROE).  Above, we urged the Commission to adopt our 
methodology and the resulting 8.2% ROE because it provides sufficient returns and represents 
un-biased results as compared to the Companies’ methodology.   
Yet, current market conditions are presently in stark contrast to those relied upon 
in Staff’s case, due to the global COVID-19 pandemic and decreasing oil prices, which are not 
reflected in Staff’s or the Companies’ analyses.683  Staff’s ROE model incorporates data over a 
three-month period.  Thus, should the Commission chose to authorize an ROE that reflects the 
most up to date estimate of investors’ return requirements, the Commission will have the 
necessary three months of data, at the very earliest, on or about May 15, 2020.  In the month of 
May, data including Merrill Lynch’s market returns, Value Line data, Treasury yields, and the 
three-month average stock prices for the proxy group, i.e., the financial data needed to run the 
cost of equity update, will be available. 
Inclusion of updates to the financial inputs in Staff’s methodology are 
distinguishable from the need to include broader updates to the Companies’ proposed rates.  In 
recent litigated rate cases, Administrative Law Judges have consistently evaluated updates at the 
time of the recommended decision.  More importantly the Commission has performed, reviewed, 
and adopted updates at the time it has rendered its decision.  While the Commission has a natural 
disinclination toward the inclusion of changes, especially those received late in a proceeding, this 
data is neither speculative nor is it conjectural.  As described above, in May concrete, objective 
data will be available to perform a reliable updated analysis.  Assuming the Commission issues 
its rate order in these proceedings in July 2020, at the time the Commission renders a decision, 
an additional two months of data will be available.  The financial data the ALJs and the 
Commission would need to calculate an updated ROE is the very same data that Staff used to

formulate its analysis presented in the testimony filed in these cases, just of a more recent

quantifiable impacts.  There is no need to conjecture as to how to incorporate the results of the 
analysis.  There is no need to speculate as to the results as there are reliable inputs upon which 
the analysis and the results are based.  
Concerning the equity market itself, the Volatility Index (VIX) also known as 
“Fear Index” created by the Chicago Board Options Exchange, which provides a measure of 
equity market risk and is indicative of investors’ sentiments.  Historically, the VIX has averaged 
around 22, and a relatively high number reflects increased uncertainty, volatility, risk and 
investors’ fear.  In late February, the index first began to rise noticeably over concerns related to 
COVID-19.  It rose from around 17 at the time, peaked at 82.69 by the middle of March, and has 
since moderated somewhat, but still stands at a relatively high 57.41 as of March 30, 2020, 
which represents a dramatic increase in the so called fear index of approximately 260% since 
investors first began to reflect upon the potential impacts related to the COVID-19 outbreak. 
In terms of the impact on stock prices overall and utility shares in particular, the 
S&P 500 is down around 21.3% since February 21st, and the Dow Jones Utility Index is down 
approximately 17.3%. 
Concerning the debt market, investors current yield requirements have increased, 
which indicates an increasing degree of uncertainty regarding the health of the overall economy 
and the increase in perceived risk even for some of the most reliable corporate financing issuers.  
Staff is tracking these yield requirements.  For two consecutive periods, the first covering 
February 21, 2020 to March 9, 2020, US Treasury yields and the yield requirements of public 
utility bond investors dropped.  The yield on the 30-year US Treasury bond fell approximately 
90 basis points, from 1.90% to 0.99% while the yields on seasoned public utility debt obligations 
fell from 3.05% to 2.63% for “A” rated, and from 3.37% to 3.06% for “Baa” rated debt.  The 
change in those yields represent an increase in spread requirements (yield minus Treasury yield) 
of 115 basis points to 164 basis points for “A” rated utility debt, and from 147 basis points to 207 
basis points for “Baa” rated utility debt.   For the second period covering March 9, 2020 to 
March 23, 2020, however, Treasury yields have trended slightly higher in anticipation of large 
Federal stimulus spending: the 10-year Treasury yield rose from 0.54% to 0.76% and the 30-year 
yield increased from 0.99% to 1.33%.   


--- Page 159 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
155 
Public utility debt yields on the other hand, have increased considerably: “A” 
rated debt which traded at 2.99% in late February, increased to 4.12% by mid March; for “Baa” 
rated debt, the increase went from 3.31% to 4.74%.  As of late March, the yields have receded 
somewhat, and as of March 30, 2020 stood at 3.71% and 4.36%, for “A” rated and “Baa” rated 
utility debt respectively.  Many investors expect the recent market volatility to persist given the 
challenges in getting the COVID-19 pandemic under control, the difficulty in resolving the oil 
price war raging between Saudi Arabia and Russia, and market reaction to the emergency 
measures put in place by the Federal Reserve and the US lawmakers, including the stimulus 
package designed to dampen the economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. 
 
 
In consideration of this stark change in circumstances in financial markets and 
relative uncertainty due to the COVID-19 outbreak and oil price decrease, Staff recommends that 
the Commission update the inputs to Staff’s cost of equity methodology using the most recent 
financial data available.  The very earliest that such data will be available is May 15, 2020.  
Further, consistent with Staff’s recommendations in these proceedings, and for the reasons 
discussed in this section, the Commission can choose to update the inputs at the time it makes its 
decision in these proceedings.  These prospective updates, should the Commission choose to 
pursue them, will enable the Commission to tailor the Companies’ ROE to evolving economic 
conditions to provide sufficient returns based on these changed conditions. 
7. 
Rate of Return/Cost of Capital 
Staff recommends an overall after-tax rate of return of 6.08% for KEDNY as 
opposed to KEDNY’s request of 6.76%.  On a pre-tax basis, our recommended overall rate of 
return is 7.58% compared to KEDNY’s request of 8.53%.  The difference between Staff’s 
recommended rate of return and KEDNY’s is attributable to a reduction in the ROE from 9.65% 
to 8.20%.  In terms of revenue requirement, the difference between Staff’s and KEDNY’s pre-
tax rate of return (PTROR) and after-tax rate of return (ATROR) account for approximately 
$49.0 million and $34.0 million, respectively.   
For KEDLI, Staff recommends an overall after-tax rate of return of 6.04 percent 
for KEDLI, as opposed to the Company’s request of 6.74 percent.  On a pre-tax basis, our 
recommended overall rate of return is 7.54 percent compared to KEDLI’s request of 8.51 
percent.  Similar to KEDNY, the difference between our recommended rate of return and

KEDLI’s is attributable to a reduction in the ROE from 9.65 percent to 8.20 percent.  The 
difference between Staff’s and KEDLI’s PTROR and ATROR account for approximately $31.4

approximately $3.3 million and 100 basis points in equity ratio is worth approximately $5.0

equity ratio together with our 8.2 percent return on equity recommendation will support the 
Companies financial integrity.684 
Staff demonstrates that its recommended ROE of 8.2 percent when combined with 
our recommended 48 percent common equity ratio is sufficient to balance the interest of both 
ratepayers and Companies’ investors’ interest by having the lowest cost of capital, maintaining 
the Companies current credit ratings and providing the Companies access to the credit market at 
reasonably priced interest rates.  Even if the Companies are downgraded by Moody’s to “Baa1”, 
the yield spread between the Companies current “A3” rating and “Baa1” has reduced 
considerably from about 20 basis points in 2019 to about 10 basis points as of January 2020.  
Given that 10 basis points in ROE is worth nearly $3.3 for KEDNY and approximately $2.1 
million for KEDLI in revenue requirement in the rate year compared to about $2 million for 
incremental interest cost for the $400 million planned debt issuance for KEDNY in the rate year 
as well as zero incremental interest cost for KEDLI  with no planned debt issuance in the rate 
year,  Staff believes that in an unlikely scenario of a one-notch downgrade by Moody’s, 
ratepayers are better off when compared with the incremental revenues needed to avoid a 
potential downgrade. 
In light of the foregoing, we urge the adoption of our methodology and resulting 
8.2% ROE because it provides stable and un-biased results based on publicly available data, 
compared to the methodology underlying the Companies’ requested 9.65% ROE, which relies on 
questionable data and methods.

B. 
Capital Structure 
The Companies propose to use their stand-alone capital structures and propose 
ratemaking capital structures that reflect a 48.0 percent common equity ratio, compared to 
KEDNY’s 51.3 percent and KEDLI’s 48.9 percent forecasted actual common equity ratios that 
the Companies are expected to maintain during the rate year. 685  The Companies’ rationale to set 
rates based on a capitalization with a 48 percent common equity ratio is to mitigate the impact of 
their rate request.686   
First, the Companies believe that they have implemented strong ring-fencing 
measures that have been recognized by the major rating agencies.  Second, the Companies 
believe that utility operating subsidiaries of their proxy group have, over the last few years (2014 
to 2017), maintained average common equity ratios of more than 8.0 percentage points above the 
48.0 percent equity ratio that the Commission approved in the Companies’ last rate proceeding.  
While Staff agrees with the Companies that the rating agencies still view KEDNY and KEDLI as 
adequately ring-fenced and thus rate the Companies’ credit quality substantially on a stand-alone 
basis, apart from their holding Companies, we do not agree with the Companies approach of 
using the operating subsidiaries historical equity ratios to support the reasonableness of their 48 
percent proposed common equity ratio because a subsidiary common equity balance may not in 
fact be financed by common equity at the holding company level.  Rather, some of the utility 
common equity balance may instead be proceeds from debt issued at the holding company level 
and classified on the utility subsidiary's books as common equity at the time the proceeds were 
invested in the utility subsidiary.687  Staff believes that the appropriate and relevant equity ratio 
analysis should be based on the utility holding companies.688   
Even though Staff does not agree with the Companies’ approach  of using the 
subsidiaries capital structure to justify the reasonableness of the 48 percent common equity ratio, 
our independent analysis using the holding companies capital structures indicates that from 2014 
to 2017, the holding companies in Ms. Bulkley’s Combined Proxy Group financed their asset

To determine the capital components separately for KEDNY and KEDLI, we

suggesting the need for a higher common equity ratio in light of Staff’s 8.2 percent 
recommended ROE.  Specifically, Staff responded by saying that “the overall cost of capital 
should be at the lowest level.”692  Lowest level of cost of capital will result in the lowest revenue 
requirement while providing the Companies an access to the credit market at reasonable rates 
and Staff’s analysis demonstrates that the 48% common equity ratio will result in such regulatory 
outcome.  Therefore, Staff continues to support a 48 percent common equity ratio, and 
recommend the Commission adopt its recommended ROE of 8.2 percent.

E. 
Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
An Earnings Sharing Mechanism (ESM) provides for the sharing of any earnings 
in excess of a threshold above a company’s allowed return on equity.  Such a mechanism is 
useful in two ways.  First, by requiring that a portion of such excess earnings be retained for 
customers, the mechanism helps to protect customers from paying for an excessive return.  
Second, as shareholders will receive the benefit of a portion of these excess earnings, the 
structure of the ESM maintains an incentive for a company to increase efficiencies.710  Staff’s 
recommends that the Commission institute an ESM for the period after the end of the Rate Year.  
Additionally, we note that the Commission instituted an ESM for the period following the rate 
year when it last set rates for NFGD.711 
 
705  T. 3123, 
706  T. 3125.   
707  T. 3124.   
708  T. 3124. 
709  T. 3124.   
710 T. 3581. 
711 2017 NFGD Rate Order. 
 


--- Page 166 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

Specifically, we recommend that the Commission require the implementation of 
an ESM for each 12-month period following the Rate Year, i.e., the 12-month period ending 
March 31, 2022 and so on and so forth.  As per Staff’s proposal, the ESM would only go into 
effect in the event the Companies do not file for new rates effective on or before October 1, 
2021.  By doing so, the Commission can ensure that the Companies’ rates remain just and 
reasonable in the event there are unanticipated consequences that may unreasonably increase the 
Companies’ actual earnings.712 
Staff’s proposed ESM has four bands.  For the first band, we recommend that the 
Companies be allowed to retain 100 percent of earnings up to and including 50 basis points 
above their allowed ROE.  For the second band, defined as earnings greater than 50 basis points 
above their allowed ROE and up to 100 basis points above their allowed ROE, we recommend 
that 50 percent of any earnings be deferred for the benefit of customers and the remaining 50 
percent be retained by the Companies.  For the third band, defined as earnings greater than 100 
basis points above their allowed ROE and up to 150 basis points above their allowed ROE, we 
recommend that 75 percent of any earnings be deferred for the benefit of customers and the 
remaining 25 percent should be retained by the Companies.  For the last band, defined as any 
earnings in excess of 150 basis points above their allowed ROE, we recommend that 90 percent 
of any earnings should be deferred for the benefit of customers and the remaining 10 percent be 
retained by the Companies.713 
In rebuttal the Companies disagreed with Staff’s recommendation to implement 
an ESM.  The Companies argue that ESMs are only a feature of multi-year rate plans, and that 
the Commission only recently began imposing ESMs in litigated one-year rate proceeding.714  
Further, the Companies’ argue that the Commission’s adoption of an ESM would be 
inappropriate and would be “procedurally defective and substantively confiscatory.”715   
The Companies are incorrect and misconstrue the purpose of an ESM.  In this 
case, Staff recommends a ROE to establish just and reasonable rates.  The adoption of an ESM is

a necessary control the Companies’ earnings for the benefit of its customers should the 
Companies choose not to file after the Rate Year.  The Commission held in Case 16-G-0257, the 
adoption of an ESM ensures that “savings accrue to the benefit of ratepayers.”716  The 
Commission went on to reject NFGD’s argument that ESMs constitute retroactive ratemaking 
and are beyond the Commission’s authority.  The Commission stated the ESM “is but one 
additional reconciliation mechanism that the Company is subject to post rate year.”717 
Staff concurs with Commission precedent and recommends that the Commission 
adopt its recommendations concerning an ESM for the Companies.  An ESM is a necessary 
mechanism to ensure customers are protected from any potential over-earnings.  
VIII. 
REVENUE ALLOCATION AND RATE DESIGN ISSUES 
A. 
ECOS Study 
 
 
The Companies filed Embedded Cost of Service (ECOS) studies following the 
traditional three step process of Functionalization, Classification and Class Allocation.718  The 
Companies used a minimum system study to classify the costs associated with distribution mains 
since these mains are used both to connect customers to the system and to meet those customers’ 
design day requirements.719  Through the minimum system study, the Companies allocated the 
distribution mains costs as part customer-related and part demand-related which resulted in 
47.08% and 46.17% classified as customer-related for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.720  
 
 
The Companies ECOS studies reflect Rate Year forecasts.  Such studies are 
referred to as pro-forma ECOS studies.721  The Companies updated their ECOS studies in the 
corrections and updates filing to reflect updated revenues and revenue requirement as well as a 
correction to the calculation of the functional labor allocator.722  The Companies maintained that 
pro-forma ECOS studies should be used in this proceeding since they reflect the forecasted Rate

As Staff stated above, Staff recommends using historic ECOS studies rather than 
pro-forma ECOS studies.  Pro-forma ECOS studies can introduce additional errors into the 
results of the studies and should only be used if very large changes in utility operation are 
expected.742  This is not an issue in these proceedings.  In response to IR UIU-20, the Companies 
compared the differences between the use of historic and pro-forma ECOS studies for KEDNY 
and KEDLI which indicates that the use of a historic ECOS study would not change KEDLI’s 
revenue allocation, and would produce only a slight change to SC-3 allocation for KEDNY’s 
revenue allocation.743  With only slight changes to the revenue allocation between the ECOS 
studies, Staff does not find this sufficient to support the Companies’ use of pro-forma ECOS 
studies.  Additionally, the movement towards the system average rate of return when a historic 
ECOS study is used can be determined in the next rate cases for the Companies.  If a pro-forma 
ECOS study is used, the movement towards the system average rate of return would be 
determined by the study based on the Rate Year’s forecasted rate base, expenses, and revenues 
associated with each service class and with each change in forecast the study would need to re-
run.  Thus, Staff recommends continued use of the historic ECOS study as this will be more 
efficient with similar accuracy.

disagree with Staff’s revenue allocation, which they argue is flawed because it treats classes just 
within the tolerance band differently than classes just outside of the tolerance band, and treats 
classes just outside the tolerance band the same as classes significantly outside the tolerance 
band.758  
 
The Commission should adopt Staff’s revenue allocation adjustments because it 
moves classes towards the system average rate of return while recognizing that the cost 
allocations in the ECOS studies could change by the next rate cases.  Additionally, by using 
historic ECOS studies, we can still make movement toward the system average rate of return, it 
is just that the movement towards the system average rate of return actually made is not 
quantified until the Companies’ next rate cases. 
C. 
Rate Design 
1. 
Firm Rate Design 
 
 
Gas rates are generally comprised of a minimum monthly charge that includes the 
first few therms, a block or multiple blocks with different rates depending on usage.  These usage 
blocks can include a tail block for higher usage, with the lowest per therm rate.  Some classes do 
not have multiple blocks and comprise of a minimum charge and a single rate for all usage above 
the therms included in the minimum charge.  For example, KEDNY’s Service Class No. 1B - 
residential heating rate is currently comprised of a minimum charge of $16.25 for the first three 
therms, a middle block of $1.7273 from three to 50 therms and a tail block of $0.7690 for all 
 
755  T. 2946. 
756  T. 2946. 
757  Ex. 496, pp. 427-428. 
758  T. 2821. 
 


--- Page 173 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
169 
usage above 50 therms.  Generally speaking, after allocating revenue among the service classes 
that target is then is used to calculate the rates. 
 
 
KEDNY and KEDLI proposed to increase the minimum charges for all firm 
classes.759  The Companies’ established several goals for their rate design.  These goals include 
collecting the revenue target established for each service class in the revenue allocation process, 
avoiding severely disproportionate impacts on customer subgroups within a class, and providing 
signals to customers to reflect the effects their usage has the overall system.760  
 
 
The specific rate designs discussed in this brief are predicated on Staff’s 
recommended revenue requirements.  To the extent that the revenue requirements change, the 
rate design should take that into account the revenue requirements ultimately adopted and ensure 
that the rate design does not create disproportionate impacts.  Staff’s revenue requirements are 
much lower than the Companies.  One of Staff’s overarching goals in approaching rate design is 
to move towards flat block rates, meaning towards the same per use therm charge for all usage.  
With that in mind, if KEDNY or KEDLI’s revenue requirement increase significantly, Staff 
reommends reducing the movement towards flat block rates, increasing the minimum charge to 
mitigate bill impacts, and allocating any increases to both minimum charges and block rates for 
SC Nos. 1BDG and 7 for KEDNY and SC Nos. 1DG, 9 and 15 for KEDLI.761  
 
 
Initially, Staff recommended that, for KEDNY SC Nos. 1A, 1B, 2-1, 2-2, 3 and 
4B, the minimum charge should remain constant, the tail block should be increased, and the 
middle block(s) should increase or decrease to effectuate a 20 percent movement towards flat 
block rates.762  For KEDNY SC Nos. 4 and 4A, Staff recommended setting the tail block and the 
first block rates equal to each other, as they were already close.763  For KEDNY SC Nos. 21-1 
and 21-3, Staff recommended equal increases to the minimum charge, winter block rate and 
summer block rate.764  At the time of the Evidentiary hearings, given the change in Staff’s 
recommended revenue requirement for KEDNY, Staff recommended increasing minimum

E. 
Non-Firm Service Sales Customers Cost of Gas 
 
 
The Companies currently charge non-firm demand response customers the 
weighted average cost of gas (WACOG) multiplied by the unaccounted-for gas factor plus the 
non-firm demand charge.789  The Companies propose to include non-firm demand response 
customers in its annual gas cost reconciliation so that they would share in the annual over/under 
recoveries that currently apply to firm sales customers.790  The Companies argue that non-firm 
demand response sales customers should continue to be charged the WACOG, instead of Staff’s 
recommended use of the incremental cost of gas (ICOG), because the Companies have a 
combined portfolio that has gas available to non-firm demand response sales customers down to 
15 degrees Fahrenheit.791  The Companies also disagree with Staff’s statement that the 
Companies did not provide an adequate incentive to prevent non-firm customers from migrating 
to firm service.792  Instead, they assert that having non-firm demand response customers pay the 
ICOG instead of the WACOG would increase gas costs since the ICOG is almost always higher 
than the WACOG.793  Furthermore, the Companies disagree with Staff’s argument that firm 
customers subsidize non-firm customers “because the times when the ICOG is likely to be set 
(e.g., the 17 coldest days) are the times when non-firm customers can expect to be 
interrupted.”794   Non-firm customers would have no cost responsibility for setting the ICOG.795

F. 
Paperless Billing Credit 
 
 
The Paperless Billing Credit is a credit given to customers who opt out of 
receiving paper bills.  The Companies proposed to update the paperless billing credits from $0.49 
per service period to $0.44 for KEDNY and from $0.35 per service period to $0.41 for 
KEDLI.805  The Companies do not believe 12 months of data are necessary to make a decision on 
KEDNY’s rates, as transactional costs should not vary significantly over a 12-month period, as 
opposed to a four-month period.806  Additionally, the Companies argued that there could be 
disconnects between the billing costs included in the revenue requirement and the paperless 
billing credit calculations if the Companies are required to revise the credit in Briefs on 
Exceptions.807 
 
 
Staff has no issue with the Companies’ methodology.  Staff also supports 
KEDLI’s proposed paperless billing credit, which is based on 12 months of data.808  However, at 
the time Staff submitted its direct testimony, Staff only had four full months of billing data to 
assess the appropriateness of KEDNY’s proposed paperless billing credit.809  Consequently Staff 
recommends the approval of KEDLI’s proposal, but recommends that a full year of data be 
available before making a judgment on KEDNY’s proposal.  Accordingly, Staff recommends

G. 
Make Whole / Rate Compression 
 
 
The Companies have consented to extend the maximum suspension period for 
these proceeding on four occasions and for a total of four months, through August 1, 2020.811  
The Companies provided the most recent such consent by letter dated December 9, 2019.  In that 
letter, the Companies stated that their consent is with the understanding that Staff supports: (1) a 
make whole provision that restores the Companies and customers to the financial position they 
would have been in had new rates been effective on April 1, 2020; (2) “a limited waiver of the 
Commission’s Policy Statement on Test Periods in Major Rate Proceedings, permitting the 
Companies to use a historic test year consisting of the twelve months ending December 31, 2019 
in their next base rate filings;” and (3) a make whole provision for the Companies’ next base rate 
filings, that would place the Companies and customers in the same financial position as they 
would have been had the Companies filed for new rates to be effective April 1, 2021, and as if 
new rates went into effect on April 1, 2021.  With regard to the latter two items, the Companies 
state that they “intend to file their next base rate filings on or about 15 days following issuance of 
a Commission order in the instant proceedings.” 
 
 
Staff supports the Companies’ requests, with two clarifications.  First, with regard 
to the make whole provision for the rate changes that the Commission may authorize in these 
proceedings, the make whole should be calculated for each service class by taking the difference 
in revenue between the current rates and new approved rates.  The difference should then be 
collected from, or returned to, that service class through a surcharge, or surcredit, over the 
remainder of the Rate Year.812  Second, with regard to the two requests affecting the Companies’

Commission issues its rate order in the instant proceedings, their requests are reasonable.

The Companies updated their lost and unaccounted for (LAUF) gas calculations

Companies believe the inactive account adjustment is unnecessary, because inactive accounts

deliveries.815  The Companies stated that when LAUF is above the upper dead band, the 
Company incurs a penalty for an inactive accounts adjustment.816  The Companies’ reasoning for 
changing the LAUF target effective date from January 1 to September 1 is to eliminate the 
 
Commission.”  When calculating the make whole provisions, the Commission should consider the 
impacts of this provision from the 2016 Rate Order.

programs and commit to outreach to the city and other non-firm customers.840

costs into base rates.  Additionally, Staff agrees, in part, that energy efficiency program costs can 
be recovered from the baseload DG customers, as long as the bill impacts are reasonable.841  
However, Staff does not recommend that energy efficiency program costs be separately 
recovered from non-firm customers.  As explained in Section VIII. E. 1 above, Staff 
recommends that the Companies charge non-firm customers rates based on a discount of their 
otherwise applicable firm rate.  Based on this, non-firm customers would be contributing to the

J. New York Facilities System Surcharge 
 
 
The New York Facilities System Surcharge allows the Companies to reconcile 
costs related to the transfer of gas within the New York Facilities System.842  The New York 
Facilities System is a gas transmission system that interconnects the Companies’ and Con 
Edison’s gas systems.  The carrying costs and operation and maintenance costs associated with 
the New York Facilities System are allocated to Con Edison, KEDNY and KEDLI pursuant to 
the NYFS Agreement.843  The Companies proposed to adjust the timing of the reconciliation 
from a calendar year basis to a fiscal year basis.  Presently, the surcharge changes three months 
after the beginning of the calendar year, i.e., April 1.  As the Companies’ fiscal year begins on 
April 1, the Companies propose that the surcharge would change three months after that, i.e., 
July 1 of each year.844 
   
 
Staff agrees with the Companies’ proposal in its entirety and recommends that the 
Commission adopt this adjustment because it would align the reconciliation with the costs 
included in the Rate Year. 845

currently being recovered through the Gas Safety and Reliability Surcharge.846  Additionally, the 
Companies proposed to revise their tariffs to allow recovery through this surcharge of the costs 
of professional engineering certification, operator qualification and other similar programs

IX. 
GAS POLICY AND ENERGY EFFICIENCY 
A. 
Energy Efficiency 
1. 
Base Rate Funding of Energy Efficiency 
 
 
The Companies propose to move all energy efficiency costs from the SBC into 
base delivery rates.861  Staff agrees with this proposal, as it is consistent with previous 
Commission direction provided in the March 2018 ETIP Order.862  In Earlier phases of these 
proceedings, the Companies proposed to separate evaluation, measurement, and verification 
(EM&V) costs from the rest of their energy efficiency budgets.863  Staff opposed the Companies’ 
proposal.864  The 2020 Energy Efficiency Order’s clarification that the Commission-authorized 
energy efficiency budgets include EM&V costs.  Now both Staff and the Company are in 
complete agreement that the Companies energy efficiency budgets should be moved from the 
SBC into base delivery rates, and that the EM&V costs should be part of the overall energy 
efficiency budgets.865  As there is no dispute on this issue, and the agreed upon resolution reflects 
the Commission’s recent 2020 Energy Efficiency Order, the Commission should approve the 
Companies and Staff’s recommendations on this issue. 
2. 
Energy Efficiency Budgets and Targets 
 
 
The Companies proposed to set their energy efficiency budgets and targets for the 
Rate Year at the amounts approved by the Commission for calendar years 2020 and 2021 as set 
forth in Appendix C to the 2019 Energy Efficiency Order.866  NYC questioned relying on the 
Companies’ proposed energy efficiency budgets and targets, based on New York City’s analysis 
of the Companies’ recent per therm cost of energy efficiency measures.

Commission expects for the Companies’ Rate Year energy efficiency budgets and targets.

budgets and targets.

3. 
Low and Moderate Income Customers 
 
 
In its direct testimony, NYC disagreed with the Commission’s then-set run-rates 
used to determine budgets and targets for low and moderate income (LMI) customers.872  These 
had been set by the Commission in its December 2018 Energy Efficiency Order.  Since NYC 
filed its direct testimony, the Commission issued its 2020 Energy Efficiency Order.  In this 
recent Order, the Commission reset the energy efficiency budgets and targets with regard to LMI 
customers.  Specifically, in the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order, the Commission required that 
20% of the incremental budgets being authorized therein, would be dedicated to LMI programs.  
As discussed above, the ALJs provided parties with an opportunity to file supplemental 
testimony to address the impacts of the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order.  NYC chose not to file 
any supplemental testimony.  Staff, therefore, concludes that NYC has discontinued its challenge 
on this issue.  Regardless, Staff recommends that the Commission find that these rate 
proceedings are not the appropriate venue for disputing the Commission’s recent determination 
in a separate case. 
4. 
Downward-Only Reconciliation 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Commission impose a downward-only reconciliation 
of the Companies’ energy efficiency spending during the Rate Year.873  If actual expenditures are 
less than the Companies’ budgets, the unspent funds would be deferred for future disposition by 
the Commission.  The Companies oppose Staff’s recommendation that the reconciliation be tied 
to the Rate Year.874  Instead, the Companies recommend that they be allowed to retain any 
unspent Rate Year energy efficiency monies to use for energy efficiency through 2025, even if 
the Commission only sets rates for the single Rate Year.875 
 
 
Staff maintains that Companies are collecting customer monies and are 
responsible for meeting the energy efficiency targets on an annual basis.  They should be held 
accountable for managing their programs to those budgets.  If the Companies underspend their 
budget, those funds should be reconciled and deferred.  Staff recognizes that, for Central Hudson 
 
872 T. 5298 – 5299. 
873 T. 672. 
874 T. 161. 
875 T. 162. 
 


--- Page 191 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
187 
Gas & Electric Corporation (Central Hudson), Niagara Mohawk, and O&R, their current rate 
plans have a downward-only reconciliation which covers the cumulative energy efficiency 
spending across multiple years, however, in those cases the reconciliation conforms to the length 
of those utilities respective rate plans.876 
 
 
Indeed, in the March 2018 ETIP Order, the Commission specifically addressed 
reconciliations of energy efficiency budgets and actual spending.  The Commission stated that 
downward-only reconciliations would be in place for the term of a utility’s rate plan.  During any 
“stay-out” period, the Commission required that the reconciliations occur annually.877  Here, 
where the Commission is being asked to set rates for a single year, the Rate Year is the term of 
the rate plan, and the subsequent year would be part of a stay-out period. 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation to require an annual downward-only reconciliation of the 
Companies’ energy efficiency spending is consistent with the Commission’s March 2018 ETIP 
Order, and with the treatment of other utilities.  Further, it protects customers, should the 
Companies underspend their energy efficiency budgets.  Accordingly, the Commission should 
adopt a downward-only reconciliation for the Companies’ energy efficiency spending for the 
Rate Year and each annual period thereafter, until the Companies rates are next reset.

Staff agreed with the Companies request, which increases the number of FTEs supporting the 
Companies’ energy efficiency program from approximately 15 to 22, almost a 50% increase.880 
 
 
After the Commission issued its 2020 Energy Efficiency Order, which set energy 
efficiency targets through 2025, the Companies requested an additional eight FTEs to support

6. 
Empower Replacement Program (HEAT) 
 
 
NYSERDA administers an Empower program, which provides energy assistance 
to low income customers across the state.  The funding for the NYSERDA program is collected 
through a surcharge on electric service.  That surcharge is collected from customers of all of the 
State’s investor-owned electric utilities.  However, that surcharge is not collected from customers 
of the Long Island Power Authority (LIPA).  KEDLI’s service territory overlaps with LIPA’s 
service territory.  Accordingly, KEDLI’s customers are not eligible to participate in 
NYSERDA’s empower program.  In order to ensure that KEDLI’s customers receive equitable 
service compared to other New York State gas customers, KEDLI operates an Empower 
replacement program, also known as “HEAT.” 
 
 
In its initial filing, KEDLI proposes to continue the HEAT program, but to shift 
the funding for the program into base rates.885  Additionally, KEDLI proposed to increase the 
HEAT program’s annual budget from $1.9 million to $2.5 million.  KEDLI proposed to use the 
additional funding to expand the program to reach moderate income customers, defined as those 
with incomes between 60% and 80% of the state or area median income, whichever is higher.886 
 
 
In its direct testimony, Staff supports these KEDLI proposals, including the 
proposed increase in funding.887  Like KEDLI did in its initial filing, Staff treated the funding for 
the HEAT program as separate from the funding for KEDLI’s general energy efficiency 
programs.  That said, Staff did recommend that KEDLI align its HEAT program with its energy 
efficiency portfolio.  At is core, the HEAT program is an energy efficiency programs and KEDLI 
should operate this program in accordance with the rules and requirements associated with the 
current energy efficiency portfolio, including any statewide LMI customer framework developed 
within Case 18-M-0084.888 
 
885 T. 54. 
886 Id.  
887 T. 678 
888 T. 679. 


--- Page 194 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
190 
 
 
The Commission’s 2020 Energy Efficiency Order included LMI energy efficiency 
budgets and targets, which were incremental to existing LMI funding and targets.  The Order 
also required the utilities and NYSERDA to work together to develop a statewide LMI portfolio 
that lays out a program framework that will best address the needs of the State’s LMI customers. 
 
 
In the supplemental testimony addressing the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order, 
KEDLI the Companies filed after the issuance of the Commission’s 2020 Energy Efficiency 
Order, KEDLI proposed to fund its HEAT program through its authorized energy efficiency 
budget.  As a corollary, KEDLI proposed to remove the separate $2.5 million in funding for the 
HEAT program from its revenue requirement.889 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Commission require KEDLI to fund the HEAT 
program at $2.5 million separate from KEDLI’s otherwise authorized energy efficiency budget.  
In the 2020 Energy Efficiency Order, the Commission set KEDLI’s energy efficiency budget and 
targets on par with other utilities’ budgets and targets.  However, when it comes to the HEAT 
program, KEDLI is unique.  Other utilities, including KEDNY do not operate a program like 
HEAT.  Instead, as explained above, customers of other utilities have the opportunity to 
participate in NYSERDA’s Empower program.  Were KEDLI to fund the HEAT program out of 
its otherwise authorized energy efficiency budget, KEDLI’s LMI customers would not have 
access to energy efficiency programs on an equal footing with similar customers in other 
utilities’ service territories.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations 
with regard to KEDLI’s HEAT program.

Other Energy Efficiency Issues 
 
 
Certain other issues related to energy efficiency are addressed in separate sections

B. 
Green Gas Tariff 
 
 
The Companies proposed to implement a Green Gas Tariff that would allow 
customers the option to choose a tiered level of renewable natural gas (RNG) at a flat rate per 
month residential customers and either a flat rate or a percentage of monthly consumption for 
non-residential customers.  The Companies proposed beginning to offer customers the Green 
Gas Tariff in the year after the Rate Year, however, the Companies proposed two FTEs to 
support the Green Gas Tariff.893 
 
 
Family Energy, Inc., an energy services company, opposed the Companies’ 
proposal.  Family Energy, Inc.  identified a number of concerns that the Companies’ Green Gas 
Tariff proposal could result in the Companies utilizing monopoly resources in a manner that 
would impact the competitive supply market and stifle competition.894 
 
 
Staff, in testimony, generally supported the Companies Green Gas Tariff 
proposal.  Staff, however, did recommend reducing the Companies’ proposed two FTEs to one 
FTE, whose time would be split equally between the Companies.  Additionally, Staff made 
recommendations regarding how gas costs under the Green Gas Tariff should be reconciled.895

C. 
Utility Energy Services Contracts Program 
 
 
The Utility Energy Services Contract (UESC) program, the Companies help 
customers analyze their current energy usage, identify opportunities for improvement, and work 
with pre-selected contractors to achieve efficiencies, reduce costs, and adopt renewable energy 
systems.  The Companies state that with the energy savings achieved through the UESC 
program, customers will further benefit by saving money and achieving clean energy goals and 
mandates.896 
 
 
The Companies propose an incentive to help customers offset the cost of energy 
feasibility studies, up to 50% of total cost of facility study.  They also propose a new marketing 
budget to engage new customers.897  The Companies propose two incremental FTEs to support 
the UESC program.  One would manage marketing to drive additional customer participation in 
the program.  The other is a project engineer to assist customers through the energy feasibility 
study and implementation process.898  In total, the Companies propose incremental spending of 
$0.257 million for KEDNY and $0.261 million for KEDLI on the UESC program in the Rate 
Year.899 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Companies fund the UESC program out of their overall 
energy efficiency program budgets.900  The UESC program provides energy efficiency measures 
 
896 T. 62. 
897 T. 60. 
898 T. 61. 
899 T. 62. 
900 T. 683 – 684. 
 


--- Page 197 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

and results in energy efficiency saving.  It should be operated so as to ensure consistent and 
comprehensive treatment of energy efficiency programs and their resultant savings.  This can 
best and most efficiently be accomplished by incorporating the UESC program into the 
Companies’ overall energy efficiency portfolio budgets and targets.901  Furthermore, historically, 
the Companies have not spent their annual energy efficiency budgets.  Thus, they can fund the

The Companies disagree with Staff’s recommendation.  Primarily, they state that

that developing energy efficiency targets for the UESC program would be duplicative.904  
Finally, the Companies state that the UESC requires dedicated employee resources, and thus they 
need the additional two FTEs.905 
 
 
The Commission should adopt Staff’s position.  That the Companies are already 
counting the energy efficiency savings that result from the UESC program, reinforces that the 
UESC program should be wholly integrated into the Companies’ energy efficiency portfolios.  
Additionally, the Companies have not adequately justified the need for two incremental FTEs 
dedicated to the UESC program, particularly in light of the incremental seven FTEs Staff 
supports providing the Companies to support energy efficiency generally.  Furthermore, there 
Companies have the flexibility to fund the UESC program within the energy efficiency portfolio 
without requiring separate funding.

E. 
Demand Response 
 
 
The Companies propose expanding their current gas demand response program.  
The Companies proposed to recover the costs of the program in base rates.913  The operations 
and maintenance costs include both non-labor O&M and an incremental FTE. 
 
 
Staff noted that the non-labor costs, such as incentive payments to participating 
customers, are uncertain.  Thus, Staff recommended allowing recovery of the non-labor O&M 
costs through a surcharge.914  Additionally, Staff argued that the Companies could support the 
expended gas demand response program with the existing personnel assigned to the gas demand 
response demonstration project.915 
 
 
The Companies stated that they would object to a surcharge cost-recovery 
methodology with a lagged cost recovery period, they would not object if the non-labor O&M 
costs were included in the Delivery Rate Adjustment surcharge mechanism.916  With regard to 
the FTE, the Companies assert that the existing FTE assigned to the gas demand response 
demonstration project splits time with the other gas demonstration projects previously approved 
by the Commission.  Additionally, the incremental FTE is required to manage the Company’s 
forecast approximately six-fold increase in DR assets.917 
 
 
After reviewing the Companies’ rebuttal testimony, Staff recommends that the 
Commission allow recovery of the non-labor O&M costs through the Companies’ Delivery Rate 
Adjustment surcharge mechanisms.  Further, Staff concedes that the additional FTE is reasonable 
to advance the expanded demand response program.

The Companies propose to expand the existing KEDLI geothermal demonstration 
project.918  The Companies propose to provide shared geothermal ground loops to interested

parties, who live outside the footprint of the Companies’ current and planned gas networks.919  
The Companies state that the geothermal technology used in this project offers a way to reduce 
the carbon intensity of the heating and cooling sector while providing homeowners with

Staff understands that geothermal heating, including shared ground loops may be 
a significant part of how New Yorkers heat their homes in the coming years.  As relevant to 
utility gas systems, the 2016 Rate Order approved the KEDLI geothermal demonstration project 
“to explore the use of geothermal technologies to improve sustainability and overall system 
efficiency by displacing peak gas consumption rather than by addressing those needs through the 
addition of pipeline capacity.”921  However, KEDLI just filed its final report regarding the 
existing demonstration project on April 3, 2020.922  This alone means that now is not the time to 
expand the program.  Rather it is the time to digest the lessons that can be learned from the 
results thus far.  Additionally, the Companies propose to use revenues from gas customers to 
support providing geothermal ground loops to customers that are not now gas customers.  Given 
the Companies’ description, those offered geothermal shared ground loops are not those who 
would even have the option of becoming gas customers. 
 
 
This situation poses legal questions that must be addressed before having gas 
companies forge ahead in providing geothermal services.  Such questions are best addressed 
holistically state-wide, rather than in rate proceedings that affect one, or in these cases, two 
utilities.  The specific legal issues that need to be addressed are whether or not a gas company, in 
this case KEDLI, can even own and offer service from shared geothermal ground loops.  As is 
relevant here, the PSL recognizes “gas corporations,”923 “electric corporations,”924 “combination 
gas and electric corporations,”925 and “steam corporations.”926  The PSL defines each of those, at
