heart, as entities that operate “gas plant,” “electric plant” and “steam plant,” respectively.  As the 
Companies are gas corporations, we must look at the definition of gas plant: 
The term “gas plant,” when used in this chapter, includes all real estate, fixtures 
and personal property operated, owned, used or to be used for or in connection with 
or to facilitate the manufacture, conveying, transportation, distribution, sale or 
furnishing of gas (natural or manufactured or mixture of both) for light, heat or 
power, but does not include property used solely for or in connection with the 
business of selling, distributing or furnishing of gas in enclosed containers.927 
No party to these proceedings has provided any reasonable argument that geothermal ground 
loops in areas unserved by the Companies’ gas networks928 are used in connection with “the 
manufacture, conveying, transportation, distribution, sale or furnishing of gas.”  The closest 
analogue in the PSL may be “steam plant.”  However, Staff notes that even Con Edison, a 
combination gas, electric, and steam corporation, does not comingle the three services’ rate bases 
or blend their rates. 
 
 
Additionally, the Companies are subject to the Transportation Corporations Law 
(TCL).  That statute also provides a definition of a gas corporation: 
A gas corporation is a corporation organized to manufacture, to produce or 
otherwise acquire and to supply for public use artificial or natural gas or a mixture 
of both gases for light, heat or power and for lighting the streets and public and 
private buildings of cities, villages and towns in this state.929 
Thus, the TCL also defines a gas corporation in terms of the service it provides, the provision of 
gas.  Furthermore, the TCL provides certain powers to gas corporations, specifically: 
A gas corporation and a gas and electric corporation shall have power to 
manufacture gas, and to acquire natural or artificial gas and to mix the gases and to 
sell and furnish gas for light, heat or power; and to lay conductors for gas in the 
streets, highways and public places, in each city, village and town in the county or 
counties named in its certificate of incorporation, with the consent of the municipal 
authorities of such city, village or town, and under such reasonable regulations as 
they may prescribe.930

Staff notes that the TCL extends the power of a gas corporation to lay down conductors “for gas” 
in the public ways of municipalities from which it has consent. 
 
 
Thus, in the current framework, gas corporations and entities that provide or 
operate geothermal equipment are two different entities.  While the coming years may call for a 
mingling of the two services, that requires serious contemplation of far-ranging regulatory, and 
perhaps even statutory changes.  These rate proceedings, which concern just two utilities and by 
statute require attention to myriad issues under a tight timeline, do not provide for appropriate 
review of these legal and practical issues.  Accordingly, Staff recommends rejecting the 
Companies’ proposal to expand the geothermal demonstration project at this time.

Staff addresses the Newtown Creek renewable natural gas project in Section

H. 
Power-to-Gas Project (KEDLI) 
 
 
KEDLI proposes a Power-to-Gas (P2G) project that would use “excess renewable 
electricity” to convert electricity to hydrogen or synthetic natural gas, using a process with 
involves using electricity to split apart water molecules into hydrogen and oxygen molecules, 
then combine the hydrogen molecules with a carbon dioxide source in a bioreactor to create 
synthetic methane, which could then be used as a supply source for natural gas, essentially using 
the natural gas system to store excess renewable electricity.931  Staff recommends rejecting the 
Power-to-Gas project because it is not consistent with the goals and uses for excess renewable 
electricity set forth in the Storage Order.932 
 
 
First, on cross-examination, the Companies’ witness admitted that there will not 
likely be excess renewable electricity in or around Long Island in the near future.933  
Furthermore, the Power-to-Gas project is estimated to have an overall efficiency in the range of 
30% to 48%.934  However, the Energy Storage incentive programs implemented in compliance

and Rochester Gas and Electric Corporation (RG&E) each requiring minimum efficiency of 70% 
and programs operated by Con Edison and O&R requiring minimum efficiency of 80%.  For 
these reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation and reject the Company’s 
Power-to-Gas proposal at this time. 
I. 
Hydrogen Blending Study 
 
 
The Companies propose to undertake a hydrogen blending study to analyze the 
impact of blending hydrogen into the natural gas system.  The goal of this study would be to 
determine the percentage of hydrogen that can be safely blended into the gas network without 
negatively impacting the network or customer appliances.  The process is envisioned to use 
“excess renewable electricity” to convert electricity to hydrogen with a projected cost of $0.446 
million in the rate year split evenly between KEDNY/KEDLI, assuming no additional grant 
funds are received.937  The Companies have applied for a grant from NYSERDA that, if 
awarded, would provide $0.346 million of the projected cost.  Thus, if awarded the grant, the 
costs to be recovered from customers would be only $0.100 million, of which only $0.070 
million is incremental to the Historic Test Year.938  However, NYSERDA has not yet announced 
whether or not it will award the grant to the Companies.939 
 
 
Similar to the Power-to-Gas project, the hydrogen blending study would not be 
consistent with the goals of the Storage Order.  Accordingly, in testimony Staff recommended 
that the Commission reject the proposal.940 
 
 
The Company asserts that the Storage Order analysis is not appropriate for this 
proposal.  Instead, the Companies state that the intention of the study is to assess a potential 
pathway toward decarbonizing the gas network.941  Staff recommends that, if NYSERDA awards

One-year Deferral of RNG Interconnection Program, FTE for RNG

The Companies propose facilitating, installing and owning renewable natural gas 
interconnections of successfully implemented renewable natural gas developer projects and 
creating a new future of heat engineering department that includes additional FTEs to review and

Companies and remove the associated O&M related to the KEDNY projects for the Rate Year.943

interconnection queue has increased significantly over the past few years.  Thus, claim the 
Companies, there is a need for additional resources to support the development of potential 
renewable gas interconnection projects.944

actually be developed and/or implemented in the Rate Year.  Thus, the Commission should adopt 
Staff’s recommendation.

K. 
Greenhouse Gas Inventory Methodology 
 
 
EDF proposes requiring KEDNY and KEDLI to develop a greenhouse gas (GHG) 
emissions inventory and assessment methodology in order to ensure that the Commission has 
enough information to determine whether its decisions in a utility rate proceeding is consistent 
with the requirements of the Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act (CLCPA).945  
EDF notes that the CLCPA directs the New York State Department of Environmental 
Conservation (NYSDEC) to develop a statewide GHG emissions inventory and report.946 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Commission reject EDF’s proposal to develop utility-
specific GHG emissions inventories at this time.947  While a utility-specific GHG inventory is 
one way the Commission could gather data that may be needed under the CLCPA, it is far from 
the only method of doing so.948  Furthermore, as EDF noted, NYSDEC is responsible for 
developing the state-wide GHG emissions inventory.  Thus, rather than strike out and undertake 
their own surveys immediately, KEDNY and KEDLI should work with NYSDEC to ensure that 
customer money is not wasted on duplicative efforts.949  Furthermore, this is another example of 
an issue better handled on a statewide basis, rather than in these rate proceedings that affect only 
two utilities.950  Accordingly, the Commission should reject EDF’s proposal. 
L. 
Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms 
 
 
The Commission has provided utilities with Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms 
(EAMs) “to align utility shareholder interests with New York State energy policy goals.”951  
Thus, EAMs are intended to incent utilities to take actions and achieve results that the utilities 
are not already incented to do through their existing business models, or otherwise required to do 
to provide safe, reliable and adequate service.  It is important to note that the Companies are in 
no way entitled to have EAMs available to them, and they certainly are not entitled to any 
particular level of financial incentives.  The Commission is not obliged to institute sub-optimal

The Companies proposed six EAM metrics to be measured on a calendar year

basis points and 77 basis points, respectively, worth of financial incentives for 2020, and 63 basis 
points 62 basis points, respectively for 2021.953  While the Companies propose to measure 
performance on a calendar year basis, they propose to value the basis points earned on a rate year 
basis.954  For simplicity, this brief will address only the proposals for calendar year 2020, since 
those proposals overlap with three-quarters of the Rate Year in these proceedings. 
 
 
KEDNY’s request for 78 basis points in financial incents and KEDLI’s request 
for 77 basis points far exceed the level of financial incentives the Commission has authorized for 
other gas utilities.  In comparison, the Commission provided Con Edison with zero financial

worth of total financial incentives for each of KEDNY and KEDLI, split between approximately 
15 basis points for the Share the Savings metric and 10 basis points for the Gas Peak Reduction 
metric.  This amount is in line with the financial incentives the Commission has authorized for 
other gas utilities across New York State.  It provides the Companies with appropriate incentives 
without potentially overburdening customers, and should be adopted by the Commission. 
 
 
With regard to specific metrics, the Companies propose the following metrics:  
(1) Peak Reduction; (2) Supply Diversification; (3) Incremental Energy Efficiency; (4) 
Affordability; (5) Carbon Reduced from Full-Service Customers; and (6) Carbon reduced from 
Transportation Customers.960  Staff agrees with a Peak Reduction metric, in concept, but 
recommends changes to the metric.961  Staff recommends rejecting the Companies proposed 
Incremental Energy Efficiency metric, and instead instituting an energy efficiency Share the 
Savings metric.962  Finally, Staff recommends rejecting the Companies’ proposed Supply 
Diversification metric,963 Affordability metric,964 and both the Full-Service and Transportation 
Carbon reduction metrics.965  The sections below present Staff’s reasons for rejecting the 
Companies’ proposed metrics, and then Staff’s recommendations for metrics, targets, and 
associated financial incentives. 
1. 
The Commission Should Reject the Companies’ Proposed Metrics 
 
 
As stated above, the Commission should reject each of the Companies’ proposed 
EAM metrics.  Below, Staff sets forth the primary problems with each of the Companies’ 
proposed metrics, which require that they be rejected.

The Companies proposed a programmatic Peak Reduction metric, which would 
reward the Companies for maximizing demand reductions achieved through the Companies’ 
demand response programs.966  While Staff supports a Peak Reduction metric in concept, Staff 
does not support the Companies’ proposed metric, which measures only performance of a single 
program.  However, the Commission has repeatedly and clearly communicated its general 
preference for outcome-based EAMs over such programmatic EAMs.967  Indeed, in the Track 
Two Order968 and the Storage Order,969 the Commission specifically requested reliance on 
outcome-based Peak Reduction metrics.970  Accordingly, the Commission should reject the 
Companies’ proposed Peak Reduction metric, and instead adopt Staff’s recommendations for 
developing a Peak Reduction metric, discussed in the next section of this brief. 
 
 
The Companies propose a Supply Diversification metric, through which the 
Companies could earn a financial incentive based on the amount of incremental renewable 
natural gas introduced into the Companies’ systems each year.971  According to the Companies, 
to achieve the targets the Companies would work with third parties to increase RNG 
development, for example by standardizing RNG project interconnection requirements.972  
However, this proposed metric is not consistent with the Commission’s Track One Order973 or 
Track Two Order.  While the Track One Order did identify fuel resource diversity as one of the 
six policy objectives of the REV proceeding, it did so in the context of diversifying the electric 
grid away from reliance on natural gas.974  Additionally, the Companies’ proposed metrics would 
allow them to earn $1 in financial incentives for each $1 in value created by the renewable 
natural gas interconnections.975

Moreover, the Companies’ obligation to provide safe, reliable and adequate

The Companies proposed a program-based Incremental Energy Efficiency metric 
that would measure the incremental million British Thermal Unit (MMBTUs) reductions

proposed Incremental Energy Efficiency metric ignored the requirement of the March 2018 ETIP 
Order that energy efficiency metrics be based on “Verified Gross Savings.”979  Additionally it 
runs counter to the December 2018 Energy Efficiency Order, which requires utilities to develop 
and propose new energy efficiency “Share the Savings” metrics.980  Accordingly, Staff 
recommends rejecting the Companies’ proposed Incremental Energy Efficiency metric.  In its 
place, Staff recommends an energy efficiency “Share the Savings” metric, discussed in the next 
section of this brief.

This metric is contrary to the Track Two Order, which had considered EAMs

each $1 that an average low income customer saves in a given year.986  PULP expresses concern

practices.987  Finally, presumably, by decreasing low income customer bills, the Companies 
would be decreasing revenues.  As revenues from residential customers are subject to an RDM, 
the Companies would recover any lost revenues from all residential customers, including low 
income customers.988  Thus, the Companies seek to earn an incentive, even though customers do 
not actually receive a benefit, or at least not the complete benefit the Companies purport to 
measure.  Fort these reasons, the Commission should reject the Companies’ proposed 
Affordability metric. 
 
 
The Companies propose two related programmatic Carbon Reduction metrics, one

full-service metric, the Companies would earn a reward based on reduced carbon emissions 
measured through the adoption rates of three specific offerings:  oil-to-geothermal conversions, 
sales of renewable natural gas through the proposed Green Gas Tariff,989 and use of natural gas

used to fuel such vehicles, for which they will earn revenues.995  Thus, customers need not also 
provide a separate financial incentive to the Companies’ shareholders.  For these reasons, the 
Companies’ proposed Carbon Reduction metrics should be rejected. 
2. 
The Commission Should Adopt these EAM Metrics and Targets 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Commission adopt an energy efficiency Share the 
Savings metric and an outcome-based Peak Reduction metric. 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Share the Savings metric allow the Companies to retain 
a 30% share of dollar per lifetime-MMBTU savings achieved through efficient operation of the 
Companies’ energy efficiency programs.996  This metric should be based only on Verified Gross 
Savings.997  The Share the Savings metric should have certain guardrails to ensure that the metric 
does not result in unintended consequences.  These guardrails include precluding the Companies 
from earning an EAM under this metric unless the minimum energy efficiency program targets 
required by the Commission are achieved, and ensuring that the Companies use the correct

Staff recommends developing a Peak Reduction metric based on peak-day usage 
reductions.  The metric would measure the usage reductions of firm customers on a per-Heating

to be allowed to earn the financial incentives reserved for this metric.1003 
 
 
Unlike the Companies proposed EAMs, Staff’s two recommended EAMs are 
aligned with Commission guidance.  They provide the Companies with sufficient incentives to 
shift behavior in the targeted areas.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
recommended EAM metrics and targets.

would earn PSRs from the operation or facilitation of distribution-level markets.1004  The 
Companies propose to earn two PSRs.  First, the Companies propose to retain 20% of the fees

1. 
E-Commerce Platform PSR 
 
 
The Companies propose to earn a PSR based on retaining 20% of the fees

such fees to be deferred for customer benefit.1007  The Companies state that the 20% share of

electric side of that company, not the gas side,1009 whereas KEDNY and KEDLI do not have 
electric operations.  In testimony, Staff also questioned whether the Companies’ proposed PSR 
was consistent with the Track Two Order.1010 
 
 
On rebuttal, the Companies asserted that their proposed E-Commerce Platform 
PSR is consistent with the Track Two Order.  Specifically, the Companies stated that the Track 
Two Order provided examples of PSRs including “connecting customers with DER [Distributed 
Energy Resource] providers via a marketplace portal.”1011 Additionally, the Companies argued 
that the Commission had previously approved a marketplace-related PSR for Central Hudson in 
Cases 17-E-0459 and 17-G-0460.1012  Having considered the Companies’ rebuttal testimony, 
Staff now recommends that the Commission adopt the 20% share of net revenues for the E-
Commerce Platform PSR.

2. 
UESC Program PSR 
 
 
The Companies propose to retain 33% of the fees received from UESC program 
participants, with the remaining two-thirds of fees deferred for customer benefit.1013  The 
Companies claim that this higher level PSR is warranted because the Companies’ ability to earn 
a PSR is uncertain despite increased marketing and feasibility study management efforts.1014  
Furthermore, claim the Companies, the enhanced service they provide as part of the UESC 
program, and the enhanced value of customer participation in the UESC program toward State 
energy policy goals, justify providing the Companies with 33% of the fees.1015 
 
 
Staff agrees that the Companies should earn a PSR related to the UESC program.  
However, Staff recommends that the PSR be calculated as 20% of the net revenues generated by 
the UESC program.  First, the Track Two Order provides guidelines for PSRS, including 
identifying a 20% PSR as appropriate for programs that do not include shareholder funds at 
risk.1016  Indeed, the Track Two Order specifically considers higher percentage PSRS, and says 
that they may be reasonable with higher levels of shareholder funds at risk.1017  The costs of the 
Companies’ UESC program are recovered from customers through rates; there are no 
shareholder funds at risk.  Accordingly, the PSR should be limited to 20%. 
 
 
Additionally, Staff recommends that the PSR be calculated as a percentage of net 
revenues, that is a percentage of the fees collected from UESC participants less the costs of 
operating the UESC program.1018  Providing a PSR based on a percentage of net revenues would 
only reward the Companies if the UESC Program is successful in producing fees from 
participants in excess of what it costs customers for the Companies to operate the program.1019  
Without this modification, i.e., if the Companies received a percentage of the gross fees received, 
the Companies could earn a reward through the PSR roughly double the benefits that would 
accrue to customers.1020

Staff’s recommendations are reasonable as they provide the Companies with a 
PSR equal to 20% of net revenues from the UESC program.  This PSR is in line with prior 
Commission decisions where the PSR is based on a program supported by customers.  Further, 
by calculating the PSR off of net revenues, it ensures that the Companies do not earn a reward 
unless and until the UESC program produces net monetary benefits.  For the reasons discussed 
above, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended UESC program PSR. 
N. 
Empower Replacement Program Incentive Mechanism (KEDLI) 
 
 
KEDLI currently earns a shareholder incentive to encourage it to maximize the 
effectiveness of the Empower Replacement Program, also known as the HEAT program.1021  The 
HEAT program has four tiers, and the mechanism provides incentives for each household 
assisted at Tiers Three and Four.  The per-household incentive for Tier Three is currently 
calculated as 0.007% of the of the Tier Three program budget.1022  The per-household incentive 
for Tier Four is currently calculated  as 0.014% of the Tier Four program budget.1023  In light of 
the increased program budget proposed in these proceedings and discussed in Section IX.A.6, 
above, the Tier Three per-household incentive would increase from the present $122.88 to 
$148.75 under KEDLI’s proposal.1024  Similarly, the Tier Four per-household incentive would 
increase from the present $245.76 to $297.50 under KEDLI’s proposal.1025 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Commission continue to provide KEDLI with a per-
household incentive for Tiers Three and Four, however Staff recommends certain 
modifications.1026  First, rather than set the incentive as a percentage of the program budget, Staff 
recommends setting the incentive as a flat dollar per household.1027  Tying the incentive to the 
budget provides KEDLI with greater per-household incentives as the budget increases, regardless 
of the number of households served.  Second, Staff notes that the primary goal of the HEAT 
program is met at Tier Three.  While Tier Four provides benefits to customers, those benefits are

1023 T. 603.

Staff recommends setting the Tier Three per-household incentive at $125.00.  
This closely approximates the current $122.88 level.1029  With the Tier Three incentive level in 
mind, Staff recommends setting the Tier Four per-household incentive at $62.50, half the

conceptual changes that Staff recommends, however, it proposes setting the per-household 
incentives for Tier Three at $240, and for Tier Four at $120.1031  KEDLI asserts that these 
incentives more closely reflect the current level of shareholder incentives.1032 
 
 
The Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended HEAT program incentives.  
Everyone agrees that the HEAT program creates useful positive benefits for low income 
customers.  The only disagreement between Staff and the Companies is regarding the actual 
dollar level of the incentives.  The Commission should not provide greater incentives than 
necessary.  KEDLI has not expressed that the present Tier Three per-household incentive of 
$122.88 has not provided an adequate incentive.  There is no evidence to suggest that now 
KEDLI would need an incentive twice as large in order to continue effectively operating this 
program in the Rate Year.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended 
incentive levels, as they should provide sufficient incentives to encourage KEDLI to effectively 
continue the HEAT program.

S. 
Gas Hedging Changes 
 
 
The Companies proposed modifications to the way they hedge gas supply 
purchases.  Specifically, they propose to incorporate financially settled Dominion South Point 
options, and shift to favoring up to 100% of options, as opposed to swaps, for the financial hedge 
component of their portfolios.1035 
 
 
Staff supports these proposals.  Additionally, Staff recommended that the 
Commission require the Companies to continue to monitor their hedging strategies on an 
ongoing basis, and to review decision such as these with Staff as part of the current annual winter 
supply review process.1036  The Companies agreed with Staff’s recommendation.1037  These 
changes will help to decrease the impact of gas price volatility for the Companies’ sales 
customers.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt these recommendations that both the 
Companies and Staff support.

In their initial filings, KEDNY and KEDLI propose more stringent pipeline safety 
performance targets; the consolidation of certain metrics; redistribution of negative revenue 
adjustments (NRAs) amongst the metrics, while maintaining the same overall exposure; and to 
align their metrics with that of other local distribution companies (LDCs).1038  Only in their

be implemented, and a review of KEDNY and KEDLI’s recent performance demonstrates the 
needs for such metrics in these particular cases. 
 
1039 T. 3790.

First, as a general manner, the Commission has historically imposed performance 
metrics with associated potential NRAs, even in one-year litigated rate cases.1045  The 
Commission has explained the use of pipeline safety and customer service metrics with 
associated potential NRAs as: 
…both practical and a proper use of Commission authority to ensure that safe and 
reliable service is maintained by the utility company as the foremost aspect of its 
responsibilities to the public.  We have authority to reduce rates of return to reflect 
poor service quality.  We have approved incentive programs in the context of 
accepting multi-year rate plans as “just and reasonable.”  We can index portions of 
the Company’s return for the rate year in this case to achievement of key measures 
of safety and service quality.1046 
Further, the Commission has made clear that utilizing performance metrics with associated 
NRAs provide a utility with the ability to “earn its fair return by meeting its obligation to provide 
safe and reliable service through the designated service and reliability metrics.”1047  Indeed, the 
Commission has expressly rejected requests by utilities to discontinue performance metrics during 
one-year litigated rate cases.  For example, in Case 08-E-0539, Con Edison sought to discontinue the 
electric reliability performance mechanisms applicable to it.  In the 2009 Con Edison Rate Order, the 
Commission unequivocally denied Con Edison’s request, stating: 
We reject outright the Company’s proposal to eliminate or reduce the effect of the 
Reliability Performance Mechanism that applies to it. The identification of 
performance criteria and specific consequences for failure to meet those criteria 
help to focus management attention on the provision of reliable electric service.1048 
 
 
Thus, the Commissions rate decisions over the past 15 years plainly state a 
preference for performance metrics with associated potential NRAs, including in litigated cases.  
There is one exception, the Commission’s 2017 NFGD Rate Order.1049  The Commission’s 
decision in that case demonstrates that it was not generally revisiting the appropriateness of

performance metrics with associated potential NRAs in litigated rate cases.1050  The Commission 
explained that it was NFGD’s particular historical record on safety and customer service that 
lead the Commission to decide not to require performance metrics with associated potential 
NRAs: 
…the Commission’s review of the record shows that despite the fact that use of 
performance metrics statewide has encouraged improvement in performance 
related to customer service and gas safety, NFGD’s past performance, for the most 
part, gives the Commission sufficient comfort to allow the Company to operate for 
the time being, and in the absence of a multi-year rate plan, without the addition of 
potential revenue adjustments based on targeted performance. Staff will continue 
to monitor NFGD’s performance and, to that end, the associated reporting 
requirements remain in place.1051 
Thus, imposing performance metrics with associated potential NRAs is the normal outcome for 
rate cases, including litigated ones such as these.  It has been the rare exception to not include 
such mechanisms, and KEDNY and KEDLI cannot demonstrate they warrant such an exception 
in these cases. 
 
 
Second, as explained above, the Companies have incurred NRAs pursuant to 
pipeline safety metrics in the recent past.  In addition, they are currently subject to potential 
penalties in two enforcement proceedings.  This demonstrates that their performance is not of a 
sufficient level that the Commission can or should trust them to perform appropriately during the 
Rate Year in the absence of performance measures with potential NRAs.  The sections below 
provide Staff’s recommendations for specific pipeline safety metrics and associated potential 
NRAs.

adjustments (PRAs) of 2 basis points for each mile of LPP removed above the targets, with the

Staff supports KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s calendar year 2020 LPP removal targets

Setting an LPP removal target with associated NRAs is designed to ensure that 
both KEDNY and KEDLI continue to appropriately remove LPP from their systems.  Absent 
such a metric, LDCs would typically only remove LPP to ensure compliance with the pipeline 
safety regulations, to respond to significant customer complaints, or through coordinated 
municipal or state projects where a portion of the associated costs are shared.1069  However, it is 
important that LDCs go beyond that level of LPP removal, as removing LPP improves public 
safety.  Thus, for a one-year litigated case, KEDNY and KEDLI should be required to remove 65 
and 150 miles, respectively, for calendar year 2020 with associated NRAs of 15 basis points 
should they fail to remove these amounts. 
 
 
With regard to the PRAs, Staff recommends reimagining the PRAs for LPP.  The 
Companies should be focused on how to deliver necessary safety and reliability expenses and

repair.  In other words, the Companies should be required to conduct recheck inspections should 
be performed for all leaks, including Type 3 leaks.1092

With regard to incentives, Staff supports the Companies’ proposal for associated

incentives for repairing up to 300 additional leaks.1094  Furthermore, Staff confirmed that

Leak management refers to KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s ability to monitor and repair 
existing and newly discovered leaks.1096  By reducing the total number of leaks, the Companies 
decrease the threat to public safety and reduce methane emissions.  Thus, the overall goal for this 
metric should remain the elimination of all leaks.  However, Staff recognizes that, due to the size 
of the leak backlogs, eliminating all leaks in a cost-effective manner will take some time.  
Accordingly, the Commission should adopt total leak reduction targets of 150 leaks for KEDNY 
and 750 leaks for KEDLI for calendar year 2020.  Based on known leak counts, the targets for 
year-end 2020 should be set at 2,200 for KEDNY and 6,600 for KEDLI.  In order to properly 
incent the Companies to eliminate leaks, the Commission should set incentives as Staff 
recommends above.

Staff supports the Companies’ proposal to consolidate this metric into a single

the number of damages per 1,000 one call notifications.  An operational audit, which focused on 
the accuracy and effectiveness of the data submitted by nine New York State natural gas and 
combination natural gas and electric LDCs, identified that the way the Companies were reporting 
data was inconsistent with that of other major LDCs.1107  Specifically, the Companies were in 
fact counting refresh and retransmit tickets which resulted in the appearance of superior 
performance when compared to that of other major LDCs.  This was recognized in the 
Companies’ prior rate proceedings.  The 2016 Rate Order adopted targets for damage prevention 
based on the methodology the Companies had been using at that time.  However, the 2016 Rate 
Order also required that the Companies report damages per 1,000 one-call notifications using the 
methodology consistent with the other major LDCs.1108  At this point, Staff recommends that the 
Commission set the targets for the damage prevention metric using the methodology consistent 
with the methodology used at other LDCs; which excludes refresh and retransmit tickets.  
Specifically, Staff recommends setting the targets and associated NRAs as follows: for a rate of 
damages per 1,000 one-call notifications greater than 2.50, the Companies would incur an NRA 
of 20 basis points; for a rate between 2.25 and 2.50, the Companies would incur an NRA of 10 
basis points; for a rate between 2.00 and 2.25, the Companies would incur an NRA of five basis 
points; for a rate between 1.50 and 2.00, the Companies would not incur a revenue adjustment; 
for a rate between 1.25 and 1.50, the Companies would earn a PRA of five basis points; and for a 
rate less than 1.25, the Companies would earn a PRA of 10 basis points.1109

metric is not appropriate.1122

performance in 2017.1123  Staff proposed targets for this metric, which are consistent with those 
applicable for other major LDCs.1124  While Staff supports continuing the 10 violation cap per 
code section for record audit violations, Staff recommends not providing such a cap for 
violations identified during field audits.  Staff supports the use of remediation plans for 10 or

across the other pipeline safety performance metrics.  Finally, it is reasonable to include each and 
every occurrence of a violation of a single code section observed by Staff.  While the Companies 
should be performing with the utmost regard for safety at all times, it is logical that the 
Companies performance should be expected to be at its peak when it is being actively observed 
by Staff.  Field audits are not merely a records review of what transpired during the prior year,

B. 
Safety Programs 
1. 
First Responder Training 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Companies continue with their efforts to train first 
responders; adopt the principles of the Pipeline Emergency Responders Initiative (PERI); 
conduct regular drills with local fire departments and municipalities; and file an annual report 
detailing its progress which includes, but not limited to, the date and times of drills, who was in 
attendance, what topics were reviewed and any applicable recommendations.1128 
 
 
Continual efforts to train first responders would benefit customers by reinforcing 
the knowledge required to respond to an incident with first responders, to review actions first 
responders should take, and those to avoid, and educate first responders on how to safely operate 
certain field devices.  The Companies should be required to participate in PERI to train fire 
departments on how to prevent and manage pipeline incidents. 
2.

The Companies proposed an incremental operating and maintenance program to 
continue exploring limited use of cavity ring down spectroscopy (CRDS) technology to enhance 
the leak repair programs.  The Companies proposed to hire a contractor to operate a Google Car 
with CRDS for use in areas previously identified as possible high leak concentration areas to aid 
in prioritizing leak repairs.  The methane leak program requires an additional gas distribution 
engineer “to work with procurement and contracts strategy to develop a contract for leasing the 
CRDS technology, manage the contractor, and monitor progress, and analyze and report 
results.1129  The Companies stated that if this case is litigated, the program should be funded

4. 
Buried Vent Lines 
 
 
The Companies state that “approximately five percent of their respective high-
pressure services with inside meter sets have regulator vent lines that are buried 
underground.”1139  “In February 2016, KEDNY experienced an incident on its system that 
involved a buried bent line that was found to be corroded and could have contributed to an over 
pressure condition due to freezing water in the regulator.”1140  To rectify this situation, the 
Companies proposed a capital program to systematically address buried vent lines.1141  The 
Companies stated that they began conducting inside inspections in 2017, which led to increased 
 
1135 T. 3796. 
1136 T. 4119. 
1137 T. 3823. 
1138 Ex. 481, p. 105 (Response to DPS-676). 
1139 T. 3738. 
1140 T. 3739. 
1141 Id. 
 


--- Page 236 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
232 
identification of buried vent lines.  Generally, the Companies have identified and addressed 
buried vent lines for years during their regular course of operations and maintenance.1142 
 
 
The pipeline safety regulations require that each buried or submerged pipeline 
installed after July 31, 1971, must be protected against external corrosion, must have an external 
protective coating meeting the requirements of 16 N.Y.C.R.R., §255.461, and must have a 
cathodic protection system designed to protect the pipeline in its entirety, installed and placed in 
operation within one-year after completion of construction.  The Companies explain that 
“[r]egulator vent lines are installed at the time of the original service and/or service regulator.  
The Companies have not identified installation dates of the buried vent lines that have been 
identified through the inside service line inspections to date.”1143  Thus, the Companies should 
have known of these buried vent lines, potentially for decades.  It is unacceptable that the 
Companies are only now identifying buried vent lines as a part of their inside service line 
inspection programs. 
 
 
Buried vent lines are more susceptible to corrosion and damage because the 
pipelines are not coated and do not have cathodic protection.  Vent lines are a critical component 
to the safe and reliable distribution of natural gas through pipelines.  Should a regulator 
experience an over pressure situation, the vent line would transport the excess natural gas to a 
safe location; typically, venting to atmosphere.  Pursuant to 16 NYCRR §255.603(c), the 
Companies are required to maintain maps or records of services lines.  Pursuant to 16 NYCRR 
§255.3(a)(43), buried vent lines are included within the definition of a service line.  Had the 
Companies appropriately identified the buried vent lines as required by the regulations and 
ensured proper maintenance for them over the years, the current remediation efforts would not be 
necessary.  Therefore, the Companies request to have rate recovery to remediate buried vent lines 
should be rejected.  The costs should be borne by shareholders due to the Companies’ failures to 
adequately address these vent lines in the past. 
C. 
New York City Local Law 152 
 
 
New York City Local Law 152 (Local Law 152), requires owners of many 
buildings in New York City to perform periodic inspections of their buildings’ natural gas 
 
1142 T. 3826. 
1143 Ex. 481, p. 111 (Response to DPS-678). 


--- Page 237 ---

piping.  The inspections required by Local Law 152 would include examinations for atmospheric 
corrosion and testing for leakage.  The natural gas piping inspected under Local Law 152 would 
include the inside piping that, due to the recent change in the definition of a service line, the 
Companies must inspect.1144 
 
 
The Companies expressed disappointment that the Department of Buildings 
(DOB) rules were written in a manner that precluded coordination with the Companies for inside 
service line inspections.1145  The Companies stated that is should not be assumed any efforts to 
coordinate these inspections would be fruitful.  Thus, the Companies budgeted for independently 
conducting inside service line inspections.1146 
 
 
According to New York City, the Companies’ inside service line inspections and 
the DOB rules are not duplicative.  Indeed, New York City asserts that two independent 
inspections may bolster safety.  Further, New York City states that its inappropriate to suggest 
that it “ought to incorporate the State and Federal operator qualification requirements.”  
However, New York City explains that the DOB may revisit the inspection cycle upon 
completion of at least one full cycle of periodic inspections.1147 
 
 
There are three difference between the Companies inside service line inspections 
and the DOB rules.  The first, is the training and qualifications of those persons conducting 
inspections.1148  The second, is that the Companies are required to inspect tenant spaces, e.g., 
apartments, whereas the DOB do not require inspections of tenant spaces.1149  And the third, is 
that the timeframes of both inspections do not align.  The DOB requires that their inspections be 
completed once every five calendar years.  The Commission’s regulations require either annual, 
three-year, or five-year inspections depending on where the service is located.1150 
 
 
It seems unreasonable that there would be benefits from both building owners and 
the Companies conducting separate, overlapping inspections of the same inside piping.  If a

require the Companies to file a plan, within 60 days of a Commission Order in this proceeding, 
on how they will encourage building owners to use qualified plumbers that can conduct 
inspections that meet the requirements both of Local Law 152 and the pipeline safety 
regulations.  To that end, the Companies should conduct an education campaign to explain to 
building owners the benefits of having a single inspection performed.  In the meantime, the 
Companies request for rate recovery of the required inspections should be allowed. 
D. 
Residential Methane Detectors 
 
 
KEDNY and KEDLI propose to distribute approximately 54,000 and 36,000 
residential methane detectors (RMDs), respectively, in the Rate Year.  The Companies propose 
to include enhancements to customer education and outreach at the same time the RMD units are 
distributed.1151  The Companies will only be providing the RMD units to customers.  They will 
not be installing, tracing, or supporting the RMD units once they are provided to the 
customers.1152 
 
 
New York City stated that it is concerned with customers properly installing, 
maintaining, and using the RMD units.1153  It asserts that false positive could place unnecessary 
burdens onto its emergency resources, and notes that the useful life of RMD units is seven 
years.1154  It proposes that the Companies be required to provide concise verbal instructions to 
customers.  Additionally, New York City proposes requiring that the Companies test the RMD 
unit’s functionality twice each year.  Furthermore, New York City proposes that the Companies 
be required to coordinate RMD distribution with the deployment of AMI meters.1155 
 
1151 T. 3746 
1152 T. 3828 
1153 T. 5428 
1154 T. 5429 
1155 T. 5431. 
 


--- Page 239 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
235 
 
 
Staff supports the Companies RMD program.  Staff recommends that the 
Companies target customers with room meter sets, low income customers, multi-unit dwellings, 
and customers served by two-inch and four-inch cast-iron piping.  Additionally, Staff 
recommends that the Companies coordinate their efforts with other planned work to minimize 
costs and fund the RMD program out of the net NRA funds owed to customers.  Finally, Staff 
recommends requiring that the Companies file annual report detailing deployment strategies and 
costs to date.1156 
 
 
The use of RMDs directly benefits those customers who install, maintain, and 
utilize the units.  Ideally, RMD units would communicate directly with the Companies’ 
infrastructure for early detection.  However, and due to the Companies current infrastructure 
limitations, communication is not yet available.  RMD units can still benefit customers, and the 
Companies should distribute the units as recommended by Staff.  NYC’s recommended 
conditions turn a useful and simple program that distributes functional residential methane 
detectors to customers for those customers’ use into an onerous program that entangles the 
Companies for years to come; no doubt at customers’ expense.  NYC’s proposals should be 
rejected.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations. 
E. 
Inside Meter Relocations 
 
 
Staff recommended that the Companies should be required to move meters 
presently inside buildings outside whenever the Companies perform service line replacements, 
either by insertion or direct burial, or service transfers.1157  The Companies stated that they agree 
with the safety benefits of relocating meters outside, however, they do not want specific 
requirements at this time.  KEDNY explained that it has suspended its meter relocation efforts to 
pursue ongoing discussions with the NYC Mayor’s Office, Staff, and the DOB.1158 
When the Companies perform service work, they have a construction crew on site and 
often are already interrupting service to the customer.  Thus, these instances provide the most 
convenient, cost effective, and practical opportunity to relocate meters.1159  The primary benefit

whether and where to relocate meters if the premise is located in a flood plain (e.g., elevating the 
gas meter to a higher location).  However, Staff recognizes that there must be exceptions.

refuses to provide consent to such relocation; where local building codes or regulations preclude 
outside meters; where space constraints or physical barriers preclude relocation; and/or when the 
work involved is an emergency service line repair and/or replacement.1161

relocated outside; the number of meters left inside; and of the meters left inside, the number that

F. 
Service Line Inspections 
 
 
The Companies stated that more frequent leakage surveys and corrosion 
inspections are required by the service line proceeding.1164  According to the Companies, these 
increased surveys and inspections impose additional operating and maintenance expenses, 
require incremental full-time equivalent field inspectors, and necessitate incremental capital 
funding to replace services.1165 
 
 
Staff recommends that the Companies complete inspections during their normal 
course of business and during dedicated visits.1166  Additionally, Staff recommends that the 
Companies submit a procedure by which they will automatically apply a provision, for example, 
after one refusal, two scheduled and then cancelled appointments, or if the customer does not 
respond to notifications.  Furthermore, the Companies should consider using the funds collected 
through the provision to offset inspection costs.1167 
 
 
In response to Staff’s recommendations, the Companies stated that they have 
adequate records to document inaccessible properties.  Additionally, the Companies state that 
that they already have a provision in place for those instances of inaccessibility, and that they 
will consider using the funds collected through the provision to offset inspection costs.1168 
 
 
As a result of the service line proceeding, both KEDNY and KEDLI are now 
required to inspect service lines to the outlet of each customer’s meter, or at the connection to a 
customer’s piping, whichever is further downstream.  Therefore, additional funding is required to 
complete these inspections.  Regarding inaccessible service lines, Staff confirmed that a 
provision is set in the respective tariffs, thus rendering a formal procedure unnecessary.1169  The

G. 
Inactive Accounts 
 
 
When the Companies do not have a customer of record for gas service, they need 
to shut off the flow of gas to ensure that unsafe conditions do not result in the premise.  The 
Companies refer to accounts without a customer of record as “inactive accounts.”  The 
Companies stated that for conditions warranting immediate action, they cut services or otherwise 
shut off the flow of gas to a premise within 24 hours.1170  Otherwise, the process is designed to 
resolve inactive accounts within 60 days.  Thus, the Companies proposed to reduce this 60-day 
period to 30 days.1171  In their rebuttal testimony, the Companies claim that Staff’s 
recommendation to take “immediate and continuous” action to remedy inactive account is 
unachievable for the reasons beyond their control.1172 
 
 
Staff explained that accounts typically remain inactive due to the Companies’ 
inability to gain access, the referral of accounts to field operations for physical disconnection, 
and the legal replevin process.1173  However, according to the Commission’s regulations at, all 
meters and service regulators are required to be installed in a readily accessible location and be 
protected from corrosion and other damage, including vehicular.1174  From 2014 to 2018, 
KEDNY and KEDLI reported that 1,980,919 and 90,362 hundredths of cubic feet (CCF) were 
consumed by 28,490 and 1,575 inactive accounts, respectively.1175  The impact of the usage at 
inactive accounts on the Companies LAUF gas calculation is addressed in Section VIII.H, above.  
Additionally, Staff recommends that, for any meters or services that are not readily accessible, 
the Companies should be required to take immediate and continuous action to remedy these 
conditions.1176

Annual Reporting 
 
 
The Companies proposed to report their pipeline safety performance for each

requirement for the Companies performance as they related to LPP removals, leak management, 
damage prevention, and emergency response times.1178 
XI. 
CUSTOMER SERVICE 
A. 
Customer Service Performance Metrics 
 
 
The Customer Service Quality Program, or CSQP, measures and tracks customer 
service quality and satisfaction provided by a utility company.  This program recognizes that in 
New York, utilities are monopoly providers of delivery service, and customers have no option to 
choose another delivery service based on the quality of the service.  As such, the CSQP 
mechanism is put in place to align shareholder and customer interests by providing earning 
adjustments related to the quality of service and satisfaction a company provides its 
customers.1179  This mechanism is currently in place in both KEDNY and KEDLI, as well as 
most other major, investor-owned utilities in New York State.  In KEDNY and KEDLI, the 
mechanism is comprised of four metrics for each Company: PSC Complaint Rate per 100,000 
customers, measured by the Department of Public Service Staff; Residential Customer 
Transaction Satisfaction, measured through a customer satisfaction survey administered by a 
third-party vendor; Percentage of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds as measured by each 
 
1177 T. 3767. 
1178 T. 4123. 
1179  T. 1461 – 1462. 
 


--- Page 244 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
240 
Company; and Percentage of Adjusted Customer Bills.1180  The current methodology uses a 
historical average that is comprised of three-years of customer service data.1181  Staff explains 
that, “[t]he Commission has generally assumed that the historical average represents a 
satisfactory level of service, and adopted CSQP targets to require negative revenue adjustments 
if service degrades to a level two standard deviations removed from the utility’s actual average 
performance.”1182  
 
 
The Companies proposed to maintain the current categories, targets, and NRAs 
for the CSQP mechanism.1183  Although both Companies have met all performance targets since 
2015 and have, in fact, exceeded these targets, the Companies claim the targets are challenging at 
their current levels.1184  The Companies also state that there is a collaborative in Case 15-M-
05661185 to measure customer satisfaction using a common survey across the New York States’ 
utilities; however, Staff notes that there is no established or targeted end date for that 
proceeding.1186

there is no basis to include metrics in a one-year litigated rate case.”1188  The Companies assert 
that Staff’s recommendations create a “perverse incentive” by responding to good performance

[t]his performance indicates that the Companies have established a history of

predict the level of performance the Companies will provide. This reinforces the importance of 
this mechanism, and its regular updates, to provide a consistent safeguard for customers against 
that uncertainty of the Companies future performance. 
 
The SQPM metric is in place to provide customers with a safeguard against the 
degradation of customer service and satisfaction.  Without this metric, utilities do not have a 
direct financial incentive to, at a minimum, maintain these levels.  In order to remain effective 
and continue to protect customers, the metric targets must also be updated to appropriately 
reflect the current operating environment.  As such, Staff’s recommendations help to ensure 
customers are protected from failing service and poor satisfaction if the Companies provide 
poorer quality of service. 
B. 
Terminations/Uncollectible Incentive Mechanism 
In both KEDNY and KEDLI, an incentive mechanism is in place that encourages the 
Companies to reduce residential terminations by finding alternatives that do not negatively 
impact uncollectibles.  Currently, both KEDNY and KEDLI can earn a PRA if they are able to 
manage collections in this way.  The Companies describe the relationship between terminations 
and uncollectibles as,  
The short-term effect of reducing terminations is to suppress uncollectible write-
off.  However, the long-term effect of reducing terminations is to increase accounts 
receivable, which with all else equal, in turn leads to higher uncollectible write offs 
when these accounts with larger receivable balances are eventually written off.  If 
 
1212  T. 1559 – 1560; T.  1466. 
 


--- Page 250 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
246 
effort to further reduce terminations for the Companies continue, accounts 
receivable will continue to rise (with potential higher write-off).1213 
To incent utility companies to find ways to reduce terminations without increasing 
uncollectibles, the Commission has approved an incentive measure at several utilities to offer 
financial incentives for managing the collection process to result in this goal.1214  
 
 
Specific to KEDNY and KEDLI, the Commission’s 2016 Rate Order adopted a 
mechanism to measure residential terminations and uncollectibles with a positive-only revenue 
adjustment incentive.1215  Using a normalized historical average, this metric established an 
average target and a threshold target for both categories in both Companies, with the threshold 
target set at one standard deviation from the average target.1216  The adjustments were set at two 
levels, where the Companies would earn the full PRA if the threshold targets for both 
Terminations and Uncollectibles were met, and a partial PRA if the threshold target was met in 
one category and the average target was met in the other.1217  Since that time, both Companies 
have been able to reduce terminations without increasing the average amount of uncollectibles 
and earn a Positive Revenue Adjustment, or PRA, for their efforts. 
 
 
In the current case, the Companies defined the purpose of the metric as, 
“provid[ing] an incentive for the Companies to limit the number of terminations and to address 
the Commission’s interest in utilities pursuing alternatives to terminations.”1218  The Companies 
propose to continue the Terminations and Uncollectible Metric as a PRA only with updated 
targets.1219  KEDNY proposes to use a normalized five-year average, from seven years of data 
for the average target, and reduce that target by one standard deviation to set the threshold target.

this metric is in place to incent the utilities to find effective ways to lower terminations while 
balancing the other collection pieces, not to lower terminations by harming other areas of 
collections.1233 
 
In response to IR DPS-989, the Companies submitted data to Staff which 
concluded that while both KEDNY and KEDLI have been able to decrease terminations since the 
incentive went into effect in 2017, allowing each company to earn a PRA, arrearages have 
increased for both Companies in the same time period for, both, the number of customers in 
 
1228  T. 1804. 
1229  T. 1802 – 1804; Ex. 634. 
1230  T. 1620. 
1231  Id. 
1232  T. 1620.

--- Page 253 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
 
249 
arrears and the total dollar amount.1234  As stated above, and in multiple cases, the Terminations 
and Uncollectibles Incentive Mechanism was put into place to incent the Companies to lower 
terminations through alternative measures without negatively affecting other aspects of the 
collections process that may harm customers.  Based on the Companies data from response to IR 
DPS-989, the Companies’ arrears were trending up while terminations were trending down.1235  
Therefore, Staff recommends to add an additional arrears metric to ensure the effectiveness of 
this mechanism.  SCSP also recommends adjusting the criteria that needs to be met for the 
Companies to earn a PRA in order to reflect the addition of the new arrears measure.1236  SCSP 
proposes for the Companies to earn the full PRA when the threshold target is met in all three 
measures, and earning a partial PRA if the Companies meet the threshold target for terminations 
while maintaining the average target of the other two measures.1237  This adjustment will further 
the Commission’s goal of finding alternatives to service terminations. 
 
Staff recommends updating the threshold and average targets for the Terminations 
and Uncollectibles metric using the last three calendar years of data (2016, 2017, and 2018) for 
the average target with one standard deviation removed for the threshold target for both 
Companies.1238  The time frame of three years was chosen to not only better represent the current 
operating environment, but also to better represent the Companies’ ability to manage 
terminations and collections when the incentive is in place.  Utilizing seven years of data when 
there are only two years available within the current incentive structure is not representative of 
the Companies’ methods or abilities when a PRA is involved.  Using the three-year average 
mentioned above, SCSP recommends to continue the use of one standard deviation to set the 
threshold target.1239  
 
 
Staff agrees that there are external factors outside of the Companies’ control that 
can affect collections but continues to stress that the incentive is in place to reward the 
Companies for finding ways to mitigate and manage those factors. In addition, this incentive is

positive only, meaning there is no negative adjustment if the Companies cannot manage their 
response to external factors and continue to do “business as usual.”1240  
 
To measure arrears, Staff used data that the Companies provided in response to DPS-989: 
“[f]or the last five calendar years, provide the total residential customer arrears, in both customer 
count and dollar amount, for each Company.”1241  Staff relied upon this data provided by the 
Companies to calculate the arrears targets.  In its rebuttal testimony, the Companies’ Shared 
Services Panel failed to explain why the arrearages data the Companies provided in response to 
DPS-989 is not the best way to look at arrears and why a ratio of the data is more indicative of 
the measure. 
 
 
Staff’s recommendation to structure the mechanism in a way that rewards 
lowering terminations while lowering or maintaining the other measures is in line with the 
purpose of this metric as it was defined when it was established in the 2016 Rate Order; not the 
Companies’ partial definition offered in its testimony in the instant proceedings.  Although the 
Companies claim, “they cannot realistically push down performance on all three metrics at 
once,” this argument fails to take into account the nature of the incentive.1242  The purpose of this 
incentive is to encourage the utilities to better serve customers by finding alternatives to 
terminations that do not simply shift the harm to another category and offer a financial incentive 
for success in doing so.  In addition, Staff emphasized during cross-examination that the 
Companies are not penalized in the event the targets for the full or partial PRA are not 
achieved.1243 
 
 
At the evidentiary hearing, the Companies reiterated their position that one 
standard deviation of the normalized average was an aggressive goal that was not easily 
achievable, but possible.1244  Further, the Companies’ testified that when comparing the use of 
two standard deviations to actual results, the results do not mirror two standard deviations and 
would not be reasonable targets.1245

At the evidentiary hearing, Staff clarified for the record the purpose of the metric 
as stated above, that the metric is positive only, and that the metric incents the Companies to find 
alternatives to terminations above their normal course of business.1246  
 
 
Staff’s recommendation benefits the customers in several ways and does not place 
undo harm on the Companies.  By restructuring the metric, Staff takes a more inclusive look at 
the collections process of the Companies by including arrears and continues to further the 
Commission’s goal to find alternatives to terminations without negatively impacting other areas 
of collections.  This benefits the customers by incenting the Companies to keep their service on 
while slowing the growth of arrears or the Companies’ bad debt to balloon as a result.  As this is 
a positive only incentive, the Companies are encouraged to provide better customer service and 
satisfaction and more effectively manage collections through financial incentives while, at the 
same time, not imposing a negative adjustment if the Companies are unable to effectively 
manage these three areas of collections as they claim in testimony. 
C. 
Credit/Debit Card Payments and Fees 
 
The Companies propose to include the estimated cost in base rates to recover the 
fees incurred by KEDLI and KEDNY when residential customers make Credit Card/Debit Card 
(CC/DC) payments.1247  This is referred to as the “no-fee” model, which would eliminate the per-
transaction cost to customers paying by a CC/DC and shift the cost into base rates.1248 
 
 
Staff supports the Companies’ proposal to shift the per-transaction costs into base 
rates; however, Staff does have concerns that once socialized in base rates, these per-transaction 
fees will increase.1249  Therefore, Staff recommends that the Companies use their aggregate 
buying power to lower the transaction amount to ensure protection of customers, and that the 
per-transaction costs do not exceed the current amount of $2.25.1250  Staff also proposes a 
downward-only reconciliation to further incent the Companies to keep costs low.1251

On rebuttal, the Companies disagreed with Staff’s recommendation that the per-
transaction fee should have a limit of $2.25, as this would incentivize the utility to discourage 
and/or seek to limit card payments.1252  Additionally, the Companies argue they already have 
sufficient incentive working with vendors to keep the per-transaction fee cost down. 
 
 
In response to IR DPS-368, the Companies explain that they “recently issued a 
Request for Proposal (RFP) with a group of selected credit and debit card vendors that could 
result in an updated estimate of projected transaction fee costs.”1253  During cross examination of 
the Companies’ Shared Services Panel, Staff inquired after the status of the RFP process and if a 
vendor has been selected.1254  The SSP responded that it is still in process, “the vendor that 
currently is being vetted, with respect to RFP, is formulating responses, [and] expected to visit 
with [the Companies] in the coming weeks with conclusion expected by the end or middle of 
March.”1255 
 
 
Staff has yet to receive the Companies’ decision on its RFP responses from 
vendors in order to determine what the per-transaction costs would be after socialization, and as 
such, Staff affirms its position that the Commission adopt a downward-only reconciliation 
mechanism, and a per-transaction fee limit of $2.25.  Additionally, other utilities that have been 
approved to socialize credit/debit card fees have either lowered the per-transaction rate or have 
had varying per-transaction rates.  For example, Central Hudson and NYSEG/RG&E’s quarterly 
credit card transaction reports show varying transaction costs.  Central Hudson’s latest report for 
the fourth quarter of 2019, filed in Cases 17-E-0459 and 17-G-0460, shows that the original per-
transaction cost of $3.35 decreased to a rate of $1.85 per transaction after the fees were 
socialized.1256  NYSEG/RG&E’s reports filed in Case 15-E-0283, et al., these reports show the 
volatility in transaction costs.  To compare, NYSEG/RG&E’s transaction fee decreased from 
$2.95 to $1.09 per-transaction, after socialization in base rates.1257  Since fees were socialized in

D. 
Collections 
1. Electronic Deferred Payment Agreements (EDPAs) 
 
The Companies propose to enhance the deferred payment agreement (DPA) 
process in order to permit customers to electronically enter into a DPA, rather than the current 
procedure of mailing in the DPA form or going to a customer office.1259  
 
 
Staff supports the Companies’ proposal to implement EDPAs and believes this 
electronic option will increase access for customers to avoid disconnections or potential delays in 
restoring service, as well as other unnecessary hardships.1260  Additionally, Staff recommends 
that the Companies submit quarterly reports on the status of EDPAs, including specific 
information on several aspects of DPA participation as well as a summary of any complaints 
and/or inquiries received regarding EDPAs.1261  PULP also supports the implementation of 
EDPAs.1262  
 
 
On rebuttal, the Companies disagreed that the quarterly reports should include a 
summary of any complaints and/or inquiries received regarding EDPAs, as it would be redundant 
to the existing Department of Public Service complaint process.1263   
 
 
In Case 17-E-0238, the Commission in its Order Approving Electronic Deferred 
Payment Agreements, issued October 18, 2018, directed Niagara Mohawk to include these 
summaries as part of the Company’s quarterly reporting.1264  Additionally, as KEDNY and 
KEDLI may receive complaints and/or additional inquiries that Staff should be provided updates

F.  
Outreach and Education 
 
The Companies did not propose any changes to its outreach and education 
program.  Staff recommends additional outreach on the rate block changes, as proposed in the 
Staff Gas Rate Panel, and that the Companies file its annual outreach and education plan in the 
generic outreach proceeding, Case 17-M-0475, by April 1 each year to align with the rate 
year.1265  Additionally, PULP proposes that the Companies conduct additional outreach to 
customers about balance transfers.1266   
 
 
The Companies do not support Staff’s proposal for expanded outreach unless 
incremental costs were added to current O&E budgets.1267  The Companies also state the 
outreach plans should be due at least 60 days following April 1 of each year instead of by April 
to allow time to prepare the reports and address the fiscal year-end.1268  
 
Staff asserts that the Companies can use low-cost outreach options, such as bill 
messaging, websites, and customer emails within its existing outreach budget to conduct the 
expanded customer education regarding the rate blocks, as stated during the SCSP’s cross-
examination.1269  Additionally, the Companies have inadequately demonstrated why the outreach 
plan cannot be filed by April 1; however, Staff understands that employee resources may be 
constrained during the fiscal year-end timeframe and therefore Staff is willing to accept a filing 
date of May 1.

B. 
Budget and Low Income Program Offerings 
 
PULP proposed that the Companies provide six years of billing data to customers 
as well as a customer’s availability of public assistance benefits through an e-billing portal.1296  
The Companies replied that they “would have to modify the legacy billing systems, data storage 
systems, and operating procedures in multiple ways, including increasing the amount of 
customer data stored and available in the current billing systems.”1297  The Companies also state 
that the costs would be “prohibitive, and there is no evidence such capabilities would provide a 
meaningful benefit to customers or the customer experience.”1298  Staff was unconvinced by the 
PULP’s lack of support, that six years of billing data would benefit customers.  Further, Staff has 
concerns regarding the cost impacts of providing and maintaining such data for every customer.  
Staff recommends that responsibility for tracking of customer public assistance benefits should 
lie with the New York State Office of Temporary and Disability Assistance, the New York City 
Human Resources Administration, or the County Department of Social Services.  
E. 
Request for Additional Full-Time Equivalent Employee for Energy 
Assistance Program Participation 
 
PULP supports the Companies’ proposal for a one-half FTE if an additional one-
half FTE could be dedicated to increasing KEDLI EAP participation, thus increasing the FTE 
request to one FTE, split between KEDNY and KEDLI.1299  The Companies disagreed with 
PULP’s proposal to add one full FTE if three-quarters of an FTE had to be dedicated to KEDLI 
and one-quarter to KEDNY.1300  The Companies’ Shared Services Panel agrees that additional 
resources would be beneficial in supporting EAP implementation but states the resource should 
not be dedicated solely to increasing KEDLI EAP participation rates and instead should be 
flexible between both Companies.1301  Staff supports PULP’s proposal for one full FTE 
dedicated to the EAP at both Companies, as we believe this additional resource would be 
beneficial to the Companies’ EAP enrollment.

F.

The Companies did not provide a proposal regarding its Energy Affordability

Association and the NYS Office of Temporary and Disability Assistance.1302  Additionally, the

PULP’s direct testimony advocated for the Companies to accelerate efforts to enroll customers in 
EAP and energy efficiency, and to partner with the NYC HRA and Nassau/Suffolk Departments 
of Social Services to increase EAP enrollment.1304 
 
While Staff would like to see an increase in KEDLI’s EAP participation, we have 
not seen any evidence that an additional .5 FTE, as proposed by PULP, would actually 
accomplish this.  Staff suggests that this issue includes a more robust file matching process 
between the counties and/or NYS Office of Temporary and Disability Assistance and be 
addressed in Phase Two of the generic Affordability Proceeding, Case 14-M-0565.

increase the HRA reimbursement budget from the current $100,000 to $200,000.1306  The 
Companies disagreed with NYC’s file-matching proposal because more frequent matches may 
not increase participation levels and may also increase removal of customers.1307  Staff agrees 
with the Companies; NYC did not reasonably justify the increased costs for quarterly file 
matching.

XIII. 
MANAGEMENT AUDIT COMPLIANCE 
Public Service Law §66(19)(c), requires that “upon the application of a gas or 
electric corporation for a major change in rates…the Commission shall review that corporation’s 
compliance with the directions and recommendations made previously by the Commission, as a 
result of the most recently completed management and operations audit.”  Since KEDNY and 
KEDLI’s rates were last reset in the 2016 Rate Order, the Companies have participated in the 
implementation of recommendations from three audits.  Accordingly, Staff addressed KEDNY 
and KEDLI’s status in relation to three recently completed management and operations 
audits.1308  These audits include Case 13-G-0009;1309 Case 13-M-0314;1310 and Case 13-M-
0449.1311 
On September 28, 2018, Staff issued a letter to KEDNY, KEDLI and Niagara 
Mohawk confirming the completion of the implementation of all audit recommendations from 
Case 13-G-0009.1312  On March 13, 2018, Staff issued a letter to KEDNY and KEDLI, as well as 
Niagara Mohawk, confirming the completion of all audit recommendations from Case 13-M-
0314.1313  On July 30, 2019, Staff issued a letter to KEDNY and KEDLI, as well as Niagara 
Mohawk, confirming the completion of all audit recommendations from Case 13-M-0449.1314  
Staff confirms that the Companies have completed the implementation of their most recently 
completed audits.1315
